# AN ESSAY
## ON THE NATURE AND CONDUCT OF THE PASSIONS AND AFFECTIONS.
### WITH ILLUSTRATIONS ON THE MORAL SENSE,

---

->By the Author of the *Inquiry into the Original of our Ideas of Beauty and Virtue*.<- <note>The author is Francis Hutcheson (1694–1746), a primary figure of the Scottish Enlightenment.</note>

---

### THE
# PREFACE.

ALTHOUGH the main *practical principles* which are instilled in this treatise have this prejudice in their favor—that they have been taught and spread by the best of men in all ages—yet there is reason to fear that renewed treatises on subjects so often well-handled may be looked upon as *superfluous*; especially since little is offered about them that has not often been well-stated before. But

<summary>The author introduces the preface by acknowledging that the moral principles discussed in the book are well-established by history's greatest thinkers, though he admits that writing yet another book on such common topics might seem unnecessary.</summary>
<keywords>preface, practical principles, moral philosophy, tradition, authorship</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface, expanding on why new books on old subjects are valuable despite the availability of previous works.</meta>

<header>The P R E F A C E.</header>

beside that general Consideration, that old Arguments may sometimes be set in such a Light by one, as will convince those who were not moved by them, even when better expressed by another; since, for every Class of *Writers*, there are Classes of *Readers* adapted, who cannot relish anything higher: Besides this, I say, the very *Novelty* of a Book may procure a little Attention, from those who overlook the Writings which the World has long enjoyed. And if by *Curiosity*, or any other means, some few can be engaged to turn their Thoughts to these important Subjects, about which a little *Reflection* will discover the Truth, and a thorough *Consideration* of it may occasion a great Increase of real Happiness; no Person need be ashamed of his Labors as useless, which do such Service to any of his Fellow-Creatures.

<sig>IF</sig>

<summary>The author explains that new books on familiar topics are useful because different writing styles appeal to different readers and novelty can attract attention to important truths. He argues that any effort that increases the happiness of others through reflection is a worthwhile endeavor.</summary>
<keywords>persuasion, readership, novelty, reflection, happiness, human nature, moral philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface, addressing potential criticisms that the work is too complex for general moral instruction.</meta>

<header>The PREFACE.</header>
<sig>A 3</sig>

If anyone should look upon some things in this *Inquiry* into the *Passions* as too subtle for common understanding, and consequently not necessary for the instruction of men in *Morals*, which is the common business of mankind: let them consider that the difficulty on these subjects arises chiefly from some *previous notions*, at least equally difficult, which have been already received to the great detriment of many a *natural temper*. Since many have been discouraged from all attempts at cultivating kind, generous *affections* in themselves by a previous notion that there are no such affections in nature, and that all pretense to them was only *dissimulation*, *affectation*, or at best some unnatural *enthusiasm*. And further, that to discover *truth* on these subjects, nothing more is necessary than a little *attention* to what passes in our own *hearts*,

<summary>The author argues that studying the passions is necessary to overcome harmful misconceptions that deny the existence of genuine, kind human affections. He suggests that the truth about morality can be discovered by reflecting honestly on one's own heart.</summary>
<keywords>passions, morals, human nature, affections, natural temper, truth, introspection, ethics</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's argument that understanding the heart's natural inclinations is accessible to everyone, regardless of specialized training.</meta>

<header>The PREFACE.</header>

and consequently every man may come to certainty in these points, without much art or knowledge of other matters.

Whatever confusion the *Schoolmen* introduced into *philosophy*, some of their keenest *adversaries* seem to threaten it with a worse kind of confusion, by attempting to take away some of the most immediate *simple perceptions*, and to explain all *approbation*, *condemnation*, *pleasure*, and *pain*, by some intricate relations to the perceptions of the *external senses*. In like manner, they have treated our *desires* or *affections*, making the most generous, kind, and disinterested of them proceed from *self-love*, by some subtle trains of reasoning to which honest hearts are often wholly strangers.

<summary>The author critiques certain philosophers for attempting to reduce all moral approval and genuine kindness to mere sensory perceptions or calculated self-interest. He argues that such complex reasoning is often foreign to the simple reality of an honest human heart.</summary>
<keywords>Schoolmen, philosophy, simple perceptions, external senses, self-love, affections, moral philosophy, human nature</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on how understanding our natural faculties can help us improve them and explains the relationship between disinterested virtue and personal interest.</meta>

# The PREFACE.

<sig>a</sig>

...opinions are quite wrong about them. *True opinions*, however, about both, may enable us to *improve* our natural powers and to *rectify* accidental disorders incident to them. And true speculations on these subjects must certainly be attended with as much *pleasure* as any other parts of human knowledge.

It may perhaps seem strange that, although in this *treatise* virtue is supposed to be *disinterested*, yet so much effort is taken, by a comparison of our several *pleasures*, to prove the *pleasures* of *virtue* to be the greatest we are capable of, and that consequently it is our truest *interest* to be virtuous. But let it be remembered here that, though there can be no *motives* or *arguments* suggested which can directly raise any *ultimate* *desire*, such as that of our own *happiness* or *public affections* (as we attempt to prove in *Treatise IV*);

<summary>The author explains that correct understanding of our natural faculties allows us to improve them and suggests that while virtue is disinterested, it ultimately provides the greatest personal pleasure and interest. He notes that while arguments cannot create ultimate desires like happiness, they can clarify how to achieve them.</summary>
<keywords>true opinions, natural powers, pleasures of virtue, disinterested virtue, interest, ultimate desire, happiness, public affections</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the relationship between disinterested virtue and personal interest, arguing that demonstrating the alignment of these interests is the key to promoting public-spirited behavior.</meta>

# The PREFACE.

Yet if both are natural *dispositions* of our minds, and nothing can stop the operation of public *affections* but some *selfish interest*, the only way to give public *affections* their full force, and to make them prevalent in our lives, must be to remove these opinions of opposite *interests*, and to show a superior *interest* on their side. If these considerations are just and sufficiently attended to, a natural *disposition* can scarcely fail to exert itself to the full.

In this *essay* on the *passions*, the proofs and illustrations of a *moral sense* and *sense of honor* are not mentioned; because they are so in the *Inquiry into Moral Good and Evil*, in the first and fifth *sections*. If people would reflect upon what they feel in themselves, all *proofs* in such matters would be unnecessary.

<meta>catchword: SOME</meta>

<summary>The author argues that the best way to encourage public-spirited behavior is to demonstrate that it aligns with one's superior interests rather than opposing them. He also notes that he has omitted certain proofs regarding the moral sense from this volume, as they were already addressed in his previous work.</summary>
<keywords>public affections, selfish interest, moral sense, sense of honor, passions, dispositions, Inquiry into Moral Good and Evil</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author’s critique of philosophers who limit human experience to a narrow definition of sensation and reflection. The text picks up from the previous page's discussion of those who argue that all ideas arise solely from these two sources.</meta>

<page-num>xi</page-num>

# The PREFACE.

plained themselves, so that no one should take their meaning to be that all our *ideas* are either *external sensations* or *reflex acts* upon *external sensations*. Or if by *reflection* they mean an *inward power* of *perception*, as I imagine they do, I wish they had as carefully examined the several kinds of *internal perceptions* as they have the *external sensations*; then we might have seen whether the former are not just as *natural and necessary* as the latter. Had they similarly considered our *affections* without a preconceived notion that they all stem from *self-love*, they might have found an *ultimate desire* for the happiness of others to be just as easily conceivable, and as certainly implanted in the human breast, though perhaps not as strong as *self-love*.

# The PREFACE.

The principal objections offered by *Mr. Clarke* of *Hull* against the second section of the second *Treatise* occurred to the author in conversation, and had apprised him of the necessity of a further illustration of *disinterested affections*, in answer to his scheme of deducing them from *self-love*, which seemed more ingenious than any which the author of the *Inquiry* ever yet saw in print. gentlemen mistaken some things about the **moral sense** alleged to be in mankind: Their objections gave an opportunity for further inquiry into the several schemes for accounting for our moral ideas, which some believe to be wholly different from and independent of that *sense* which the author attempts to establish.

The following papers attempt to show that all these *schemes* must necessarily presuppose this *moral sense* and be resolved into it. Nor does the author endeavor to overturn them or represent them as unnecessary superstructures upon the foundation of a moral sense; though what he has suggested will probably show a considerable confusion in some of the terms much used on these subjects. One may easily see from the great variety of *terms* and diversity of *schemes* invented that all men *feel* something in their own hearts recommending *virtue*, which

This difficulty probably arises from our previous notions of a small number of *senses*, so that we are unwilling to resort in our theories to any more; we would rather strain to find some explanation of moral *ideas* in relation to some other natural powers of perception that are universally acknowledged. A similar difficulty attends several other *perceptions*, to which philosophers have not generally assigned their own *distinct senses*; such as natural *beauty*, *harmony*, the perfection of *poetry*, *architecture*, *design*, and similar matters of *genius*, *taste*, or *fancy*: The explanations or theories on these subjects are likewise full of confusion and metaphor.

To define *virtue* by its *agreeableness to this moral sense*, or to describe it as *kind affection*, may

Our moral sense shows this to be the highest perfection of our nature; what we may see to be the *end* or *design* of such a structure, and consequently what is required of us by the Author of our nature: and therefore if anyone likes these descriptions better, he may call virtue, with many of the ancients, "*Vita secundum naturam*" <note>"Life according to nature."</note>; or "acting according to what we may see, from the constitution of our nature, we were intended for by our Creator."

<sig>a 2</sig>

If this *Moral Sense* were once set in a convincing light, those vain shadows of objections against a virtuous life, in which some take wonderful delight, would soon vanish: alleging that whatever we admire or honor in a *moral species* is the effect of *art*, *education*, *custom*, *policy*, or subtle views of *interest*; we should then acknowledge

->*Quid sumus, & quidnam victuri*<-
->*gignimur.* Pers.<-
<note>"What we are, and for what kind of life we were born." From Persius, Satire III.</note>

It is true, a *power of reasoning* is natural to us; and we must admit that all arts and sciences which are well-founded and tend to direct our actions are, if not to be called *natural*, an *improvement upon our nature*: yet if virtue is looked upon as wholly artificial, there are certain suspicions against it; as if indeed

<summary>The author argues that if the moral sense is recognized as natural, objections claiming virtue is merely a product of education or self-interest would vanish. He acknowledges that while reasoning and sciences improve our nature, viewing virtue as entirely artificial creates skepticism about its validity.</summary>

<keywords>moral sense, virtue, natural law, Persius, reasoning, art vs. nature, social constructs, ethics</keywords>

--- Page 27 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s argument against the view that virtue is a purely artificial social construct. He addresses the suspicion that if virtue were artificial, it would only serve the interests of rulers or large groups, rather than the individual.</meta>

<page-num>xix</page-num>
<header>The PREFACE.</header>
<sig>a 2</sig>

it might tend to the greater *interest* of *large bodies* or *societies* of *men*, or to that of their *governors*; whereas a private person may better find his *interest*, or enjoy greater *pleasures* in the practices counted *vicious*, especially if he has any probability of secrecy in them. These suspicions must be entirely removed, if we have a *moral sense* and *public affections*, whose gratifications are constituted by nature as our most intense and durable *pleasures*.

I HOPE it is a good omen of something still better on this subject to be expected in the learned world, that *Mr. Butler* <note>Joseph Butler (1692–1752), a philosopher and cleric known for his "Fifteen Sermons" which argued that human nature is inherently moral.</note>, in his *sermons* at the *Rolls Chapel*, has done so much justice to the wise and good order of our nature; that the gentlemen, who have opposed some other sentiments of the author of the *Inquiry* <note>Refers to the author's own work, *An Inquiry into the Original of Our Ideas of Beauty and Virtue*.</note>, seem convinced of a *mo-*

<summary>The author argues that a natural moral sense and public affections ensure that virtue is personally fulfilling, refuting the idea that it is merely a tool for social or political control. He cites the work of Joseph Butler as evidence that the learned world is increasingly recognizing the inherent moral order of human nature.</summary>

SOME letters in the *London Journals*, signed by *Philaretus*, gave the first occasion for the *Fourth Treatise*; the answers given to them bore too many visible marks of the haste in which they were written, and therefore the *author* declined to continue the debate in that way, choosing instead to send a private letter to *Philaretus* to request a more private correspondence on the subject of our debate. so talented a correspondent. The *objections* proposed in the first *section* of *Treatise* IV are not always those of *Philaretus*, though I have endeavored to leave no objections of his unanswered; I also interspersed whatever objections occurred to me in conversation on these subjects. I hope I have not used any expressions inconsistent with the high regard I have for the memory of such a talented gentleman, and one of such distinction in the world.

THE last *section* of the Fourth *Treatise* was occasioned by a private letter from a person of the greatest merit in *Glasgow*, representing to me some sentiments not uncommon among good men, which might prejudice them against any scheme of morals not wholly founded upon piety. This point is, I hope, treated in such a way as to remove the difficulty.

<page-num>xxii</page-num>
# The PREFACE.

THE respect due to a person who has appeared so much in the learned world as *Mr. Le Clerc* would seem to require that I should make some defense against, or submission to, the remarks he makes in his *Bibliotheque Ancienne & Moderne*. But I cannot help but conclude from his abstract—especially from that of the last section of the *Inquiry*—either that I do not understand his *French*, or he my *English*, or that he has never read more than the titles of some of the sections; and if any one of these three is the case, we are not suited for a controversy.

IN the references at the bottom of the pages, the *Inquiry* into *Beauty* is called *Treatise* I. The inquiry into the ideas of moral *good* and *evil* is *Treatise* II. The *Essay* on the *Passions* is *Treatise* III. And the *Illustrations* on the moral *sense* is *Treatise* IV.

<summary>The author explains why he will not engage in a public debate with Mr. Le Clerc, citing a likely failure of translation or a lack of thorough reading by the critic. He also clarifies the numbering system used to reference his four treatises throughout the book.</summary>

### SECTION I.
### *A general account of our several* SENSES *and* DESIRES, *selfish or public.*

The nature of human actions cannot be sufficiently understood without considering the *affections* and *passions*; or those *modifications*, or *actions* of the *mind* that follow the *apprehension* of certain *objects* or *events*, in which the *mind* generally conceives *good* or *evil*.

<summary>This page introduces the essay on the nature and conduct of the passions, beginning with a section that explores how human actions are driven by the mind's response to perceptions of good and evil.</summary>
<keywords>affections, passions, human actions, mind, apprehension, good, evil, senses, desires, Francis Hutcheson</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Section 1 from the previous page, further defining how the mind perceives moral qualities and sensations.</meta>

Section 1. In a similar way, *Affections*, *Temperaments*, *Sentiments*, or *Actions*, when we reflect on them in ourselves or observe them in others, are the constant *causes* of pleasant or unpleasant *perceptions*, which we call *Approval* or *Dislike*. These *Moral Perceptions* arise in us just as necessarily as any other *sensations*; nor can we alter or stop them as long as our *previous opinion* or *understanding* of the *affection*, *temperament*, or *intention* of the *agent* <note>the person acting</note> remains the same, any more than we can make the *taste* of *wormwood* sweet or the taste of *honey* bitter.

<margin>the</margin>

Although it is not easy to assign accurate *divisions* to such subjects, we may reduce them to the following *classes*, leaving it to others to arrange them as they think convenient. <header>of the PASSIONS. Sect. 1.</header>

<margin>A similar division of our desires.</margin>
II. **DESIRES** arise in our minds, from the structure of our nature, upon the perception of good or evil in objects, actions, or events. We desire to obtain for *ourselves* or *others* an agreeable *sensation* when the object or event is good, or to prevent an uneasy *sensation* when it is evil. Our original desires and aversions may therefore be divided into five classes, corresponding to the classes of our senses. 1. The desire for *sensual pleasure* (by which we mean that of the external senses) and aversion to the opposite pains. 2. The desires for the *pleasures of imagination* or the internal sense <note>See *Treatise 1.*</note>, and aversion to what is disagreeable to it. 3. Desires for the pleasures arising from *public happiness*, and aversion to the pains arising from the *misery* of others. 4. Desires for *virtue*, and aversion to *vice*, according to the notions we have of the tendency of actions toward the public advantage or detriment. 5. Desires for *honor*, and aversion to *shame* <note>See *Treatise 2, Section 5, Articles 3–8.*</note>.

**THE** third class of *public desires* contains many very different sorts of affections, specifically all those which tend toward the happiness of others or the removal of misery, such as those of gratitude, com-

### Sect. 1. *passion*, *Natural Affection*, *Friendship*, or

the more extensive calm *Desire* for the universal Good of all sensitive beings, which our moral Sense approves as the Perfection of Virtue, even when it limits and counteracts the narrower Attachments of Love.

<margin>
Secondary
Desires for
Wealth and

</margin>
Now since we are capable of *Reflection*, *Memory*, *Observation*, and *Reasoning* about the distant Tendencies of Objects and Actions, and are not confined to things present, there must arise, as a consequence of our original *Desires*, “secondary *Desires* for everything imagined useful to gratify any of the primary *Desires*, with a strength proportioned to the various original *Desires* and the imagined *Usefulness* or *Necessity* of the advantageous Object.” Hence it is that as soon as we come to understand the *Use* of *Wealth* or *Power* to gratify any of our original *Desires*, we must also desire them. From this arises the *Universality* of these *Desires* for Wealth and Power, since they are the Means of gratifying all other *Desires*. “How foolish then is the inference some would make from the universal prevalence of these *Desires*, that human nature is wholly selfish, or that everyone is only concerned with his own *Advantage*; since *Wealth* or *Power* are as naturally fit to gratify our *Public Desires*, or to serve *virtuous Purposes*, as they are to serve *selfish* ones?”

<meta>catchword: " How</meta>

<summary>Hutcheson explains that reflection leads to "secondary desires" for wealth and power because they are useful tools for satisfying primary desires. He argues that the universal desire for wealth does not prove human nature is inherently selfish, as wealth can be used for public and virtuous goals.</summary>
<keywords>secondary desires, wealth, power, human nature, selfishness, public desires, reflection, primary desires</keywords>

Section 1. From our desire for honor. When any *circumstance*, *dress*, *state*, or *posture* is established as a mark of infamy, it may likewise become the *object* of aversion, even though it is in itself completely inoffensive to our senses. If a certain way of *living*, of *receiving company*, or of *showing courtesy* is once accepted among those who are honored, those who cannot afford the expense of this may be made uneasy with their condition, even though they are much freer from trouble than those in higher positions. Thus *dress*, *retinue*, *equipage*, *furniture*, *behavior*, and *diversions* are made into matters of considerable importance by additional *ideas* <note>See Treatise 1, Section 1, Article 7, and Treatise 2, Section 6, Article 2.</note>. Nor is it in vain that the wisest and greatest men regard these things; for however much it may concern them to break such associations in their own minds, yet, since the majority of mankind will retain them, they must comply with their sentiments and humors in innocent matters if they expect the *public esteem*, which is generally necessary to enable men to serve the *public*.

Hence our natural love of honor raises in us a **desire for distinction**, either through higher degrees of **virtue**; or, if we cannot easily or likely obtain it this way, we attempt it in an easier manner, through any circumstance which, through a *confusion of ideas*, is considered honorable.

**This** desire for distinction has great influence on the pleasures and pains of mankind and makes them choose things for their very *rarity*, *difficulty*, or *expense*; by a confused imagination that they evidence *generosity*, *ability*, or a *finer taste* than ordinary; indeed, the merest trifles are often ardently pursued by these means. *A style of dress*, *a foreign dish*, *a title*, *a position*, *a jewel*; *a useless problem*, *a criticism of an obsolete word*, the *origin of a poetic fable*, or the *location of a razed town* may employ many an hour in tedious labor:

->Subruit aut reficit. — HOR.<-

Art. III. **THERE** is another division of our desires taken from the persons for whose advantage we pursue or shun any object.
<margin>
Desires,

> "The desires in which one intends or
> "pursues what he apprehends as advantageous
> "to himself, we may call **SELFISH**; and

If there is a just foundation for this division, it is more extensive than the former division, since each of the former classes may come under either member of this division, according to whether we are desiring any of the five sorts of pleasures for *ourselves*, or desiring them for *others*. The former division may therefore be conceived as a subdivision of the latter.

**THIS** division has been disputed since
*Epicurus*, who, with his ancient followers
and some of late who detest other parts of

<meta>This page continues the examination of the argument that all human desires are fundamentally selfish. It addresses the difficulty of reconciling this view with the reality of social bonds and the inherent sympathy people feel for one another.</meta>

Sect. 1.
</margin>

It requires a good deal of subtlety to defend this scheme, which is so seemingly opposite to *natural affection, friendship, love of country, or community*, which many find very strong in their hearts. The defenses and schemes commonly offered can scarcely free the supporters of this cause from manifest absurdity and *affectation*. But some do * acknowledge a *public sense* in many instances, especially in *natural affection and compassion*, by which “the observation of the happiness of others is made the necessary occasion of pleasure, and their misery the occasion of pain to the observer.” They must admit that this *sympathy* with others is the *effect* of the constitution of our nature, and not brought upon ourselves by any choice with a view toward any *selfish advantage*. Whatever advantage there may be in *sympathy* with the *fortunate*, none can be alleged in *sympathy* with the *distressed*. And everyone feels that this *public sense* will not leave his heart upon a change in the fortunes of his child or friend; nor does it depend upon a man’s choice whether he will be affected by their fortunes or not. But supposing this public sense, they insist, “That by means of it there is a *conjunction of interest*: the

If a *public sense* is acknowledged in men, by which the happiness of one is made to depend upon that of others, independently of his choice, this is indeed strong evidence of the goodness of the AUTHOR of our nature. But whether this scheme truly accounts for our *love of others*, or for *generous actions*, may be determined from the following considerations; which, being matters of *internal consciousness*, everyone can best satisfy himself concerning their truth and certainty by paying attention.

Let it be stated beforehand that *desire* is *generally uncomfortable*, or *attended with an uncomfortable sensation*, which is something distinct from that uncomfortable sensation arising from some *event* or *object*, the prevention or removal of which sensation we are intending when the *object* is perceived as *evil*; just as this uncomfortable sensation of *desire* is obviously different from the *pleasant sensation* expected from the *object* or *event*

Of private good.</margin> As soon as we form this opinion, a desire for the event immediately arises: But if having the *desire* or *affection* is imagined to be the means of private good, and not the *existence* of the *event desired*, then from *self-love* we should only desire or wish to have the *desire* for that event, and should not desire the *event* itself, since the *event* is not conceived as the *means* of good.

For instance, suppose God revealed to us that he would confer happiness on us if our country *were happy*; then from *self-love* we would immediately have the *subordinate desire* for our country’s happiness as the means of our own. But if we were assured that, whether our country were happy or not, it would not affect our future happiness—but that we would be rewarded provided we *desired* the *happiness* of our country—our *self-love* could never make us now desire the *happiness* of our country, since it is not now conceived as the *means* of our happiness, but is perfectly indifferent to it. The *means* of our happiness is having a *desire* for our country’s *happiness*; we should therefore from *self-love* only *wish* to have this *desire*.

Now, that affections do not arise simply because we *wish* to have them, or from our volition to create them—as if we conceived of the *affections* themselves as the means of private good—is plain from this: if they did arise in this way, then a *bribe* might raise any desire toward any event, or any *affection* toward the most improper object. Don’t we find that we often desire the happiness of others without any such selfish intention? How few have thought upon this part of our constitution which we call a *public sense*? Were it our only view, in *compassion*, to free ourselves from the *pain of the public sense*; should the DEITY propose it to our choice, either to obliterate all ideas of the person in distress while continuing him in misery, or on the other hand to relieve him from it; should we not, under this scheme, be perfectly indifferent and choose the former as soon as the latter? Should the DEITY assure us that we should be immediately annihilated, so that we should be incapable of either pleasure or pain, but that it should depend upon our choice at our very exit whether our children, our friends, or our country should be happy or miserable; should we not, under this scheme, be entirely indifferent? Or, if we should even desire the *pleasant thought of their happiness* in our last moment, would not this desire be the faintest imaginable?

<meta>This page continues the argument from page 22 concerning the nature of our desires at the moment of death to prove that benevolence is not based on self-interest.</meta>

<page-num>23</page-num>
<header>of the PASSIONS.</header>

<margin>Sect. 1.</margin>
It is true, our *Public Sense* might be as acute at our *Exit* as ever; just as a *man’s taste for food or drink* might be as lively the instant before his *death* as in any part of his *life*. But would any *man* have as *strong desires* for the *means* of obtaining these *pleasures*, only with a *view* toward *himself*, when he was to *perish* the next *moment*? Is it conceivable that any *desire* for the *means* of *private pleasure* can be as strong when we only expect to enjoy it for a *minute*, as when we expect the *continuance* of it for many *years*? And yet, it is certain, any *good man* would as strongly desire the *happiness of others* at his *death* as in any part of his *life*. We do not, therefore, desire it as the *means* of *private pleasure*.

Should anyone allege that this *desire* for the *happiness of others* after our *death* is from some *confused association of ideas*—as a *miser*, who loves nobody, might desire an *increase of wealth* at his *death*, or as anyone may have an *aversion* to having his *body dissected* or made a *prey to dogs* after *death*—let any *honest heart* try if the deepest *reflection* will break this *association* (if there be any) which is supposed to raise the *desire*. The closest *reflection* would be found rather to strengthen it.

Section 1. How would any *observer* like the *temperament* of one thus rendered indifferent to all others at his own *death*, so that he would not even open his mouth to procure *happiness* for *future generations*? Would we consider it *refined wisdom*, or a *perfection* of mind, and not rather the vilest *perversity*? It is plain, then, that we feel this *ultimate desire* for the *happiness* of others to be a *most natural instinct*, which we also expect in others, and not the *effect* of any confused *ideas*.

The cause of the imagined difficulty in conceiving *disinterested desires* has probably been the attempt to define this simple idea: *desire*. It is called *an uneasy sensation in the absence of good*. Whereas *desire* is as distinct from any *sensation* as the *will* is from the *understanding* or *senses*. Everyone who speaks of *desiring to remove uneasiness* or *pain* must acknowledge this.

We may perhaps find that our *desires* are so far from always tending toward *private good* that they are more often employed about *the state of others*. Furthermore, we may have a *propensity* toward an event which we perceive neither as a *means to private good* nor to *public good*. Thus, an *Epicurean* who denies a *future state*, or someone to

The prospect of any *interest* may be a motive for us to desire whatever we perceive as the *means* of obtaining it. Particularly, "if rewards of any kind are proposed to those who have virtuous affections, this would raise in us the desire of having these *affections*, and would incline us to use all means to cultivate them in ourselves; specifically, to turn our attention to all those qualities in the *DEITY*, or our fellow humans, which are naturally suited to raise virtuous affections." Thus it is that interest of any kind may influence us indirectly toward virtue, and rewards in particular may outweigh all motives toward vice.

<header>OF THE PASSIONS. SECT. 2.</header>

## SECT. II.

### Of the Affections and Passions: The Natural Laws of Pure Affection: The Confused Sensations of the Passions, with their Final Causes.

<margin>
Proper affections are desire and aversion.
</margin>

I. After the general account of *sensations*, we may consider other *modifications* of our minds, resulting from these *perceptions*, whether pleasant or uneasy. The first which occur to anyone are *desire* for the pleasant perceptions, and *aversion* to the uneasy ones, either for ourselves or others. If we would confine the word *affection* to these two, which are entirely distinct from all *sensation*, and directly incline the mind toward *action* or the *volition* of *motion*, we would have no debate about the number or classification of *affections*. But since, by universal custom, this name is applied to other *modifications* of the mind, such as *joy*, *sorrow*, and *despair*, we may consider what universal *distinction* can be assigned between these *modifications* and the several *sensations* mentioned above; and we shall hardly find any other than this: that we call “ the *direct immediate per-* ” “ *ception of pleasure or pain from the pre-* ”

He notes that while some limit 'affection' to desire and aversion, common usage includes broader emotional states resulting from perceptions of pleasure and pain.</summary>

<margin>General desires, and particular affections or passions.</margin>
II. WE have little reason to imagine that all other agents have such *confused sensations* accompanying their *desires* as we often have. Let us abstract from them, and consider in what manner we should act upon the several occasions which now excite our passions, if we had none of these *sensations* by which our *desires* become *passionate*.

THERE is a distinction to be observed on this subject, between “*the calm desire of good, and aversion to evil, either selfish or public, as it appears to our reason or reflection; and the particular passions towards objects immediately presented to some sense*.” Thus nothing can be more distinct than the *general calm desire* of private good of any kind, which alone would incline us to pursue whatever objects were apprehended as the means of good, and the particular selfish passions, such as *ambition*, *covetousness*, *hunger*, *lust*, *revenge*, *anger*,

In like manner, our public *desires* may be distinguished into the *general calm desire for the happiness of others*, or *aversion to their misery* upon reflection; and the *particular affections* or *passions of love, congratulation, compassion, and natural affection*. These *particular affections* are found in many temperaments where, through a lack of reflection, the *general calm desires* are not found. Indeed, the former may be opposite to the latter, where they are found in the same temperament. We gain command over the *particular passions* principally by strengthening the *general desires* through frequent reflection and making them habitual, so as to obtain a strength superior to the *particular passions*.

Again, the *calm public desires* may be considered as they either regard the *good* of particular *persons* or *societies* presented to our senses; or that of some more abstract or general *community*, such as a *species* or *system*. This latter sort we may call *universal calm benevolence*. Now it is plain that not only *particular kind passions*, but even *calm particular benevolence*, do not always arise from, or necessarily presuppose, *universal benevolence*. Both the former may be found in people of little reflection where the latter is lacking; and the former two may

THIS may let us see that, although it were acknowledged that “men are necessarily determined to pursue their own happiness, and to be influenced by whatever motive appears to be *prepollent* <note>prevailing or outweighing</note>;” yet they might still be proper *subjects* of a *law*; since the very *sanctions* of the law, if they attend to them, may suggest a motive prepollent to all others. In like manner, “errors may be criminal,* where there are sufficient *data* or *objective evidence* for the truth;” since no demonstration can lead to error, and we can suspend our *assent* to probable arguments until we have examined both sides. Yet *human penalties* concerning opinions must be of little consequence, since no penalty can supply the place of *argument* or *probability* to engage our *assent*, however they may as *motives* determine our *election* <note>the act of choosing or deciding</note>.

<margin>Definitions.</margin>**III. T**HE following definitions of certain words used on this subject may shorten our expressions; and the *axioms* subjoined may show the manner of acting from calm *desire*, with an analogy to the *laws of motion*.

<margin>Natural Good and Evil.</margin>1. **N**ATURAL *good* is pleasure; *natural evil* is pain.

2. **N**ATURAL *good objects* are those which are apt, either mediately <note>indirectly</note> or immediately <note>directly</note>, to give pleasure; the former are called *advantageous*. Natural *evil objects* are such as, in like manner, give pain.

<margin>Absolute.</margin>3. **A**BSOLUTE *good* is that which, considered with all its concomitants and consequences, contains more good than what compensates for all its evils.

4. **A**BSOLUTE *evil*, on the contrary, contains evil which outweighs all its good.

<summary>The author defines natural good and evil as pleasure and pain, and explains that in cases of universal benevolence, the choice is determined by the magnitude of the good and the number of people it affects. He also introduces definitions for absolute good and evil based on the net balance of their consequences.</summary>

--- Page 65 ---
<meta>This page continues the definitions of good and evil, moving from absolute definitions to relative ones and categorizing them by the scope of those they affect.</meta>

<page-num>35</page-num>
<header>of the PASSIONS. Sect 2.</header>
<sig>D 2</sig>

<margin>Relative.</margin>
5. **RELATIVE** Good or *Evil* is any particular Good or Evil which does not thus compensate for its contrary accompaniments or consequences. This distinction would have been more exactly expressed by the *Bonum simpliciter* <note>Good simply or absolutely</note> and *secundum quid* <note>Good in a certain respect</note> of the Schoolmen <note>Medieval philosophers and theologians</note>.

*Corollary.* **RELATIVE** Good may be *Absolute Evil*; thus sensual pleasures are often pernicious on the whole. And *Absolute* Good may be *Relative Evil*; thus an unpleasant medicine may recover health.

**GOOD** and *Evil*, according to the *persons* whom they affect, may be divided into *Universal*, *Particular*, and *Private*.

<margin>Universal.</margin>
6. **UNIVERSAL** Good is what tends to the happiness of the whole *system* of *sensitive beings*; and *Universal Evil* is the contrary.

<margin>Particular.</margin>
7. **PARTICULAR** Good is what tends to the happiness of a part of this *system*; *Particular Evil* is the contrary.

<margin>Private.</margin>
8. **PRIVATE** Good or *Evil* is that of the person acting. Each of these three members may be either *absolute* or *relative*.

*Corollary* 1. **PARTICULAR** *or private good may possibly be universal evil: and universal good may be particular or private evil. The punishment of a criminal is an instance of the latter. Of the former, perhaps there are no real instances in the whole administration of nature; but there are some apparent instances, such as the success of an unjust war or the escape of an unrelenting criminal.*

*Corollary* 2. **WHEN** *particular or private goods are entirely innocent toward others, they are universal good.*

<margin>*Compound.*</margin>
9. **COMPOUND** *good objects or events are those that contain the powers of several goods at once. Thus, meat may be both pleasant and healthful; an action may give its author at once the pleasures of the moral sense and of honor. The same is easily applicable to compound evil.*

<margin>*Mixed.*</margin>
10. **A MIXED** **object** *is what contains at once the powers of good and evil: Thus, a virtuous action may give the agent the pleasures of the moral sense and the pains of the external senses. The execution of justice may give the pleasures of the public sense and the pains of compassion toward the sufferer.*

<meta>This page continues the definitions of good and evil, specifically transitioning from the discussion of private versus universal goods to the definitions of the greatest good, moral good, and moral evil.</meta>

<page-num>37</page-num>
<header>of the PASSIONS. Sect. 2.</header>

11. <margin>*Greatest Good.*</margin> THE greatest or most perfect Good is that whole series, or scheme of events, which contains a greater aggregate of happiness in the whole, or more absolute universal good, than any other possible scheme, after subtracting all the evils connected with each of them.

12. <margin>*Moral Good.*</margin> AN action is good, *in a moral sense*, when it flows from benevolent affection or the intention of absolute good to others. Men of much reflection may actually intend *universal absolute good*; but for the common run of men, their virtue consists in intending and pursuing *particular absolute good* that is not inconsistent with *universal good*.

13. <margin>*Moral Evil.*</margin> AN action is *morally evil*, either from the intention of *absolute evil*, whether *universal* or *particular* (* *which is seldom the case with men, except in sudden passions*), or from the pursuit of *private* or *particular relative good*, which they might have known tended toward *universal absolute evil*. For even the lack of a † just degree of benevolence renders an action evil.

<margin>Compound.</margin>
14. COMPOUND *moral goodness* is that in which different *moral types* join together: Thus, the same action may show love for our fellows and gratitude to GOD. We may understand *compound moral evil* in a similar way. We cannot suppose there are *mixed moral actions* ‡.

15. AGENTS are named *morally good or evil* based on their affections and actions, or their attempts to act.

<margin>Axioms, or general laws.</margin>
## IV. AXIOMS, or *natural laws of calm desire*.

1. SELFISH *desires* ultimately pursue only the private good of the agent.

2. BENEVOLENT or *public desires* pursue the good of others, according to the various *systems* to which we extend our attention, but with different degrees of strength.

3. THE strength of either the private or *public desire* for any event is proportional to the imagined *quantity of good* which will result from it for the agent or the beloved person.

4. **MIXED Objects** are pursued or shunned with *Desire* or *Aversion*, proportioned to the perceived *Excess* of Good or Evil.

<margin>Intensity Duration</margin>
7. **IN** computing the *quantities* of good or evil, which we pursue or shun, either for ourselves or others, when the *durations* are equal, the magnitude is as the *intensity*: and when the *intensity* of pleasure is the same, or equal, the magnitude is as the *duration*.

8. **HENCE** the *magnitude* of good in any object is in a compound proportion of the *duration* and *intensity*.

9. **THE** trouble, *pain*, or *danger* incurred by the agent in acquiring or retaining any good is to be subtracted from the *sum* of the good. So the *pleasures* which attend or flow from the means of *prevailing evil* <note>"Prepollent evil" refers to evil that is predominant or outweighs accompanying good.</note> are to be subtracted to find the *absolute quantity*.

10. THE *ratio* of the *risk* of acquiring or retaining any *good* must be multiplied by the *moment* <note>In this context, "moment" refers to the weight, importance, or magnitude of the good or evil.</note> of the *good*; likewise, the *risk* of avoiding any *evil* is to be multiplied by its *moment*, to find its comparative value.

*Corollary. HENCE* it is that the smallest certain *good* may raise stronger *desire* than the greatest *good*, if the *uncertainty* of the latter surpasses that of the former in a greater *ratio* than that of the greater to the less. Thus, people content themselves in all affairs with *smaller*, but *more likely successful pursuits*, quitting those of *greater moment* but *less probability*.

11. TO an *immortal nature*, it makes no difference in what part of its *duration* it enjoys a *good* of limited *duration*, provided its *perception* is equally acute in all parts of its *existence*, and the *enjoyment* of this *good* does not exclude the *enjoyment* of other *goods* at one time more than at another. The same may be applied to the *suffering* of *evil* of limited *duration*.

12. BUT if the *duration* of the *good* is *infinite*, the *earliness* of its *commencement* increases the *moment*, just as a *finite* amount added to an *infinite* one surpasses the *infinite* alone.

15. THE removal of *pain* always carries the notion of good and solicits us more urgently: its moment is computed in the same way by *intensity* and *duration*, and is affected by the risk and by the uncertainty of our existence.

Concerning these latter we may observe:

16. THAT our desires toward the public good are, when other circumstances are equal, proportional to the *moment* <note>In this context, "moment" refers to the weight, importance, or magnitude of the good or evil.</note> of the goods themselves.

17. OUR public desires for any events are proportional to the *number* of persons to whom the good event shall extend, when the *moments* and other circumstances are equal.

18. WHEN the *moments* themselves and the number of recipients are equal, our *desire* is proportional to the *strength* or *nearness* of the *ties* or *attachments* to the persons.

19. WHEN all other circumstances are equal, our desires are proportional to the perceived *moral excellence* of the persons.

20. IN general, the strength of public desire is in a compound ratio of the *quantity* of the *good* itself, and the *number, attachment,* and *dignity* of the persons.

THESE seem to be the general laws according to which our desires arise. Our *Senses*

V. IF it is granted that we have implanted in our nature the various *desires* mentioned above, let us next inquire “into what state we would incline to bring ourselves, given the various accidents which now raise our *passions*; supposing that we had the choice of our own state entirely, and were not, by the frame of our nature, subjected to certain *sensations*, independently of our volition.”

<meta>This page continues the argument that desire is distinct from physical uneasiness, completing a sentence from the previous page that distinguishes between sensations like pain and the desire to avoid them.</meta>

“otherwise it would prove *Color* and *Figure* to be the same, or *Incision* and *Pain*.”

THERE is a middle state of our minds, when we are not in the pursuit of any important *good*, nor are we aware of any great *need* of those we love. In this state, when any smaller positive *good* for ourselves or our friend is perceived to be within our power, we may resolutely *desire* and pursue it, without any significant *sensation* of pain or uneasiness. Some temperaments seem to have as strong *desires* as any, evidenced by the constancy and vigor of their pursuits, whether for public or private *good*; and yet they show little evidence of any *uneasy sensation*. This is observable in some calm men, who seem in no way inferior in strength of desire to others. Indeed, if we consult ourselves, and not the common philosophical systems, we shall perhaps find that “the noblest desire in our nature, that of *universal happiness*, is generally calm and entirely free from any confused uneasy sensation;” except in some passionate temperaments who, by a lively *imagination* and frequent attention to general *ideas*, stir up something like *passion* even toward *universal nature* <note>See *Marcus Aurelius*, in many places.</note>. Furthermore, *desire* may be as strong as possible toward a certain future

<margin>Sect. 2.</margin>

According to the present constitution of our nature, we find that the *modifications* or *passions* of our mind are very different from those which we would choose to bring upon ourselves, upon their several occasions. The prospect of any considerable good for ourselves, or those we love, raises *desire*; and this *desire* is accompanied with *uneasy, confused sensations*, which often occasion *fretfulness*, *anxiety*, and *impatience*. We find *violent motions* in our *bodies*; and are often made unfit for serious deliberation about the means of obtaining the good desired. When it is first obtained, we find violent, confused *sensations* of *joy*, beyond the proportion of the good itself, or its *moment* <note>importance or weight</note> to our *happiness*. If we are disappointed, we feel a sensation of *sorrow* and *dejection*, which is often entirely useless to our present state. Foreseen *evils* are anticipated by painful *sensations* of *fear*; and *reflection*, attended with *sensations* of *sorrow*, gives a tedious *existence* to transitory *misfortunes*. Our public *desires* are in the same manner accompanied with painful *sensations*. The presence or suspense of *good* or *evil* to others is made the occasion of the like confused *sensations*. A little *reflection* will show that none of these *sensations* depend upon our *choice*, but arise from the very *frame* of

<margin>
The necessity for these sensations.
</margin>

VI. Let us then examine "for what purpose our nature was so constituted that sensations necessarily arise in us." Would not those *first* sorts of sensations—by which we perceive good and evil in the objects themselves—have been sufficient, along with our *reason* and pure *desires*, without those sensations that accompany the desires themselves (for which they are called *passions*), or those sensations which accompany our reflection upon the presence, absence, or approach of good or evil?

The common answer—that "they are given to us as useful *incitements* or *spurs* to *action*, by which we are more effectively roused to promote our private good or that of the *public*"—is too general and vague. What need is there for rousing us to *action* more than a calm, pure *desire* for good and *aversion* to evil would do, without these confused sensations? Some say, "we are averse to *labor*; we are prone to be distracted by *curiosity* or *mirth*; we are often so indolent and averse to the vigorous use of our powers that we would neglect our true interest without these soliciting

Sect. 2. serve an equilibrium. But the first question is, “From where did the necessity of such additional incitements on either side arise?”

It must be very difficult for beings of such imperfect knowledge as we are to answer such questions; we know very little of the constitution of *nature*, or what may be necessary for the perfection of the whole. The Author of Nature has probably formed many active beings whose desires are not accompanied by confused sensations, raising them into passions like ours. There is probably an infinite *variety* of beings, of all possible degrees, in which the sum of happiness exceeds that of misery. We know that our state is *absolutely good*, notwithstanding a considerable mixture of evil. The goodness of the great Author of Nature appears even in producing the inferior natures, provided their state as a whole is absolutely good. Since we may probably conclude that there are in the universe as many species of superior natures as was consistent with the most perfect state of the whole. AGAIN, our bodies could not be preserved without a sense of pain associated with *incisions*, *bruises*, or violent *labor*, or whatever else tends to destroy any part of their mechanism; since our knowledge does not extend far enough to judge in time what would be harmful to it. And yet, without a great deal of human labor and many dangers, this Earth could not support even a tenth part of its inhabitants. Our nature, therefore, required a sensation—one that accompanies its desires for the *means of preservation*—capable of overcoming the uneasiness of *labor*. This we have in the pains or uneasiness accompanying the desire for food.

IN like manner, the *propagation of animals* is a mystery to their *reason*, but easy for their *instinct*. The offspring of such creatures as humans are could not be preserved without constant labor and care, which we find could not be expected from the more general ties of benevolence alone. Here again, then, appears the necessity of strengthening the <term>Στοργὴ</term> <note>storge</note>, or *natural affection*, with strong sensations or the pains of desire, which are sufficient to counterbalance the pains of *labor* and the sensations of the

We know, for instance, that the pleasures of the *imagination* tend much to the happiness of mankind; the *desires* for them, therefore, must have similar *sensations* assisting them to prevent our indulging in a nasty, solitary luxury. The happiness of human life cannot be promoted without *society* and *mutual aid*, even beyond a family; our *public affections* must therefore be strengthened as well as the private ones to keep a balance; so must also our *desires* for *virtue* and *honor*. *Anger*, which some have thought a useless *passion*, is really as necessary as the rest; since men's interests often seem to interfere with each other, and they are thereby led by *self-love* to do the worst *injuries* to their fellows. There could not, therefore, be a wiser contrivance to restrain *injuries* than to make every mortal in some way formidable to an unjust *invader* through such a violent *passion*. <margin>*A balance may still be preserved.*</margin>
VII. With this balance of public passions against the private, along with our *passions* for honor and virtue, we find that human nature can be just as truly amiable in its lower sphere as superior beings endowed with higher reason and influenced only by pure *desires*; provided we vigorously exercise the powers we have in maintaining this balance of affections, and checking any passion that grows so violent as to be inconsistent with the public good. If we have selfish passions for our own preservation, we also have *public passions*, which may engage us in vigorous and laborious service to *offspring*, *friends*, *communities*, and *countries*. *Compassion* will lead us to help the distressed, even at our own private loss or danger. An abhorrence of those who cause injury, and love toward those who are injured, combined with a sense of virtue and honor, can make us despise labor, expense, wounds, and death.

<header>The Nature and Conduct Sect. 3.</header>

## SECT. III.

### Particular Divisions of the Affections and Passions.

<margin><unclear>language ... ideas</unclear></margin>
The nature of any language has a considerable influence upon men's reasonings on all subjects, making them often take all those ideas which are denoted by the same word to be the same; and on the other hand, to look upon different words as denoting different ideas. We shall find that this identity of names has occasioned much confusion in treatises of the *passions*; while some have made larger, and some smaller collections of names, and have given the explanations of them as an account of the *passions*.

<margin>The division of the Stoics.</margin>
CICERO, in the fourth book of *Tusculan Questions*, gives from the *Stoics* this general division of the *passions*: First, into *love* and *hatred*, according to whether the object is good or evil; and then subdivides each, according to whether the object is present or expected. Regarding *good*, we have these two: *Libido* and *Lætitia*, *desire* and *joy*. Regarding *evil*, we have likewise two: *Metus* and *Ægritudo*, *fear* and *sorrow*. To this general division he subjoins many

<meta>catchword: Sub-</meta>

<summary>The author argues that language often confuses our understanding of the passions and introduces the Stoic classification of emotions into desire, joy, fear, and sorrow as cited by Cicero.</summary>
<keywords>language and thought, Cicero, Stoics, Tusculan Questions, classification of passions, Libido, Lætitia, Metus, Ægritudo, desire, joy, fear, sorrow</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the classification of the passions, focusing on the terminology used in Latin and by the Schoolmen, and critiques Malebranche's distinction between desire and sensation.</meta>

<page-num>59</page-num>
<header>of the PASSIONS.</header>

<margin>Sect. 3.</margin>
Subdivisions of each of these four passions; according as in the *Latin* tongue they had different names for the several degrees of these passions, or for the same passion employed upon different objects. A writer of *lexicons* would probably find the most precise meanings of the *Latin* names in that book; nor would it be useless in considering the nature of them.

The *Schoolmen*, as their store of language was much smaller, do not have such full enumerations of them, going no further than their admired *Aristotle*.

II. It is strange that the thoughtful **MALEBRANCHE** did not consider that " *desire* and *aversion* are obviously different " from the other modifications called *passions*; that these two directly lead to " action, or the volition of motion, " and are wholly distinct from every sort of " sensation. " Whereas joy and sorrow are only a sort of sensation; and other affections differ from sensations only by including desire or aversion, or their corresponding propensities: so that *desire* and *aversion* are the only pure affections in the strictest sense.

<margin>Passion.</margin>
**W**HEN more violent *confused sensations* arise with the *affection*, and are attended with, or prolonged by bodily motions, we call the whole by the name of *passion*, especially when accompanied with some *natural propensities*, which will be explained hereafter.

<margin>Division by Malebranche.</margin>
**I**F this use of these words is allowed, the division of **MALEBRANCHE** is very natural. Good objects excite *love*; evil objects *hatred*: each of these is subdivided, depending on whether the object is *present* and *certain*, or *doubtfully expected*, or *certainly removed*. To these three circumstances correspond three modifications of the original affections; namely, *joy, desire* and *sorrow*. Good *present*, raises *joy* of *love*, or *love* of *joy*: Good in *suspense*, the *love* of *desire*; Good *lost*, *love* of *sorrow*. Evil present,

<margin>Desire and Aversion.</margin>
<margin>Joy and Sorrow.</margin>
**P**ERHAPS it may be easier to conceive of our *affections* and passions in this manner. The apprehension of good, either to ourselves or others, as something attainable, raises *desire*. A similar apprehension of evil, or of the loss of good, raises *aversion*, or the desire to remove or prevent it. These two are the proper *affections*, distinct from all *sensation*; we may call both *desires* if we wish. The reflection upon the presence or certain future occurrence of any good raises the sensation of joy, which is distinct from those immediate sensations that arise from the object itself. A similar sensation is raised when we reflect upon the removal or prevention of evil that once threatened ourselves or others. The *reflection* upon the presence of evil, the certain prospect of it, or the loss of good, is the cause of the sensation of sorrow, distinct from those

<meta>This page continues the discussion of sensations and affections, specifically differentiating between "pure" spiritual affections and "passions" that involve bodily sensations, while also introducing the concept of instinctive propensities.</meta>

Sect. 3. those immediate sensations arising from the objects or events themselves.

<margin>*Affections may be distinguished from passions.*</margin>
These affections—namely *desire*, *aversion*, *joy*, and *sorrow*—we may, following MALEBRANCHE, call *spiritual* or *pure affections*; because the purest spirit, if it were subject to any evil, would be capable of them. But besides these affections, which seem to arise necessarily from a rational apprehension of good or evil, there are in our nature violent, *confused sensations* connected with bodily motions, because of which our *affections* are called *passions*.

<margin>*Affections attended with undesigning propensities.*</margin>
We may further observe something in our nature that very frequently determines us to action, which is distinct from both *sensation* and *desire*—if by *desire* we mean a distinct inclination toward something understood as good, whether public or private, or as a means of avoiding evil. That is, there is a certain *propensity of instinct* toward objects and actions without any conception of them as good or as a means of preventing evil. These objects or actions are generally, though not always, actually the means of some good; but we are determined toward them even without this conception of them. Thus, as we observed above <note>Section 1, near the end.</note>, the propensity for fame

Our particular affections generally have some of these *propensities* accompanying them; but these *propensities* are sometimes present without the affections or distinct desires, and have a stronger influence upon the generality of men than the affections could have alone. Thus in anger, besides the intention of removing the uneasy sensation from the injury received, and besides the desire of obtaining a reparation for it and security for the future—which are types of goods intended by men when they are calm as well as during the passion—there is in the passionate person a propensity to cause *misery* to the offender, a determination toward *violence*, even where there is no *intention* of any good to be obtained or evil avoided by this violence. And it is principally this propensity which we denote by the name *Anger*, though other <margin>*Anger.*</margin> desires often accompany it.

This part of our *constitution* is as intelligible as many others universally observed and acknowledged; such as these: the danger of falling makes us stretch out our arms; noise makes us wink; a child is instinctively driven to suck; many other animals are driven to rise up and walk; and some to run into water, before they can have any notion of good to be obtained or evil avoided by these means.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of definitions regarding sentiments directed toward others, moving from the definition of hatred to benevolence and malice, then categorizing envy, fear, and hope as specific passions or affections.</meta>

<margin>Sect. 3.</margin>

<margin>*Envy.*</margin>
The propensities of *anger* and *envy* have some resemblance to it; yet *envy* is not an ultimate desire for another’s misery, but only a subordinate desire for it, as the means of advancing ourselves, or some person more beloved than the person envied.

<margin>*Fear.*</margin>
**Fear**, as far as it is an *affection*, and not an unintentional propensity, is “a mixture of *sorrow* and *aversion* when we perceive the probability of evil, or the loss of good happening to ourselves, or those we love.” There is more or less *sorrow* according to the perceived degrees of probability. 
<margin>*Hope.*</margin>
**Hope**, if it be in any way an *affection* and not an opinion, is “a mixture of *desire* and *joy* upon the probability of obtaining good and avoiding evil.” Both these passions may have some propensities and sensations attending them, distinct from those of the other affections.

<margin>Sect. 3.</margin> some of the most important distinctions of our affections and passions to be overlooked. No states of mind can be more different from each other than a *private desire* and a *public* one; yet both are called *Love*. The *love of money*, for instance, and the *love of a generous character*, or a *friend*: the *love of a fine estate*, and the *love of a child*. In like manner, what can be more different than the *sorrow for a loss befallen ourselves*, and *sorrow for the death of a friend*? Of this, men must convince themselves by reflection.

**There** is also a considerable difference even among the *selfish passions*, which bear the same general name, according to the different *senses* which constitute the objects as good or evil. Thus the *desire for honor* and the *desire for wealth* are certainly very different kinds of affections, and accompanied by different sensations. In like manner, *sorrow* for our *loss by a shipwreck*, and our *sorrow* for having done a *base action*, or *remorse*; *sorrow* for our being subject to *gout* or the *stone* <note>Kidney or bladder stones.</note>, and *sorrow* for our being *despised* and *condemned*, or *shame*; *sorrow* for the *damage* done by a *fire*, and that *sorrow* which arises upon a perceived *injury* from a *partner*, or any other of our fellows, which we call *anger*. WE might enumerate six other passions from the sense of *honor*, according to how we perceive our actions, or any other circumstances, shall affect the *opinions* which others form concerning us. When any action or circumstance occurs from which we imagine honor would arise, we feel *desire*; when we attain it, *joy*; when we are disappointed, *sorrow*. When we first perceive any action or circumstance as *dishonorable*, we feel *aversion* arising; if we perceive ourselves involved in it, or in danger of being tempted to it, we feel 
<margin>*Modesty. Shame.*</margin>
a passion we may call *modesty* or *shame*; when we escape or resist such temptations, or avoid what is dishonorable, we feel a *joy* for which there is no special name.

<margin>*Ambition.*</margin>
WE give the name *ambition* to a violent *desire* for honor, but generally in a bad sense, when it would lead the agent into *immoral means* to gratify it. The same word often denotes the *desire for power*.

<margin>*Pride.*</margin>
*Pride* sometimes denotes the same desires for honor and power, with aversion to their contraries; sometimes *pride* denotes joy upon any perceived *right* or *claim* to honor; generally it is taken in a bad sense, when one claims that to which he has no right.

The success of such a character raises all the contrary affections of *joy* and satisfaction with Providence, and *security* in virtue. <margin>*Which of them fit for the Drama.*</margin>
**HENCE** we see how unsuitable such representations are in *tragedy* as make the perfectly virtuous miserable to the highest degree. They can only lead the spectators into *distrust* of Providence and *diffidence* in virtue, and into such sentiments as some authors (who probably mistake his meaning) tell us *Brutus* expressed at his death: "The virtue he had pursued as a solid good proved to be but an empty name." But we must remember here that, despite all the frightening ideas of the *King of Terrors* <note>A personification of Death.</note> that have been instilled in us, an honorable *death* is far from appearing to a generous mind as the greatest of evils. The *ruin* of a *free state*, the *slavery* of a generous *spirit*, and a *life* lived on *shameful terms* still appear to be vastly greater evils—besides many other intense distresses of a more private nature, in comparison to which an honorable death befalling a favorite character is seen as a deliverance.

directly malicious, and aiming for the misery of others without any further intention. While the heat of this passion continues, we seem naturally to pursue the misery of the wrongdoer until they relent and convince us of their better intentions by expressing their awareness of the injury and offering reparation for the damage, along with security against future offenses.

Now, as it is plainly necessary in a system of agents capable of injuring each other that everyone should be made *formidable* to an invader by such a violent passion—until the invader shows a reformation of character, as described above, and no longer—we find it is thus ordered in our constitution. Upon these evidences of reformation in the invader, our passion naturally abates; or if in any perverse disposition it does not, the *sense* of mankind turns against the angry person, and he is looked upon as cruel and inhumane.

IN considering more fully the passions regarding the fortunes of evil characters—distinct from *anger*, which arises from a fresh injury—we may first consider evil agents as a sudden view sometimes represents them: *directly evil and malicious*. Then, we can make proper adjustments for *how* even the worst of men fall short of this completely evil disposition. As mathe-

<page-num>77</page-num>
<header>of the PASSIONS.</header>

Sect. 3.
<image-desc>A small decorative horizontal flourish.</image-desc>

Let anyone reflect on this class of passions, especially as they arise upon occasions which do not affect himself, and he will see how little *self-love* there is in them; and yet they are frequently as violent as any passions whatsoever. We seem conscious of some *dignity* in these passions above the selfish ones, and therefore never conceal them, nor are we ashamed of them. These complicated *passions* the philosophers have confusedly mentioned under some general names, along with the simple selfish passions. The *poets* and *critics* have sufficiently shown that they felt these differences, however it did not concern them to explain them. We may find instances of them in all dramatic performances, both ancient and modern.

<margin>
Passions regarding mixed characters.
</margin>
The *reductions* to be made for how human nature falls short of the highest degrees of either virtue or vice may be conceived thus: When the good in any mixed *character* surpasses the evil, the passions arise as toward the good; where the evil surpasses the good, the passions arise as toward the *evil*, only in both cases with less violence. And further, the passions in both cases are either *stopped*, or turned the contrary way, by a lack of due proportion between the state and *character*. Thus an imperfectly good character,

An imperfectly evil character, threatened by an evil greater than is necessary to make him relent and reform, or by a great calamity which has no direct tendency to reform him, instead of raising *desire* toward the event, raises *aversion*; his escaping it raises *joy*,

<margin>The best plots in tragedy.</margin>
THERE is another circumstance which exceedingly varies our passions of this class: when the agents themselves, by their *own conduct*, bring about their *misery*. When an imperfectly good character, by an evil *action*, brings the greatest *misery* upon himself, this raises these complicated passions: *pity* toward the sufferer, *sorrow* for the situation, *abhorrence* of *vice*, and *awe* and *admiration* of *Providence*, as it maintains strict measures of *sanctity* and *justice*. These passions we may all feel in reading the *Oedipus* of *Sophocles*, when we see the distress of that prince, occasioned by his superstitious curiosity about his future fortunes, his rash violence of temper in fighting a duel without provocation, and his pronouncing of curses on unknown persons.

It highlights that the most effective tragic plots involve characters whose own actions and flaws, such as those of Sophocles' Oedipus, lead to their downfall.</summary>

<page-num>79</page-num>
<header>of the PASSIONS.</header>

<margin>Sect. 3.</margin>
We feel similar passions from the fortunes of *Creon* in the *Antigone*; or from the fates of *Pyrrhus* and *Orestes*, in the *Andromache* of *Racine*; or our *Distressed Mother*. We heartily pity these characters, but without resenting Providence; their misery is the fruit of their own actions. It is with the most just reason that *Aristotle* <note>Aristotle's Poetics, Chapter 13.</note> prefers such plots to all others for tragedy, since these characters come nearest to those of the spectators, and consequently will have the strongest influence on them. We are generally conscious of some good dispositions, mixed with many weaknesses: few imagine themselves capable of attaining the *height* of perfectly good characters, or arriving at their high degrees of happiness; and fewer imagine themselves capable of sinking into the baseness of perfectly evil tempers, and therefore few dread the calamities which befall them.

<margin>4. Public Passions and the Relationships of Agents.</margin>
VI. The passions of the fourth class arise from the same *moral sense* and *public affections* upon observing the actions of agents who are somehow attached to each other, whether by prior ties of nature or good deeds, or separated by prior injuries. When these *relationships* are known, the moral qualities of the actions appear considerably different, and our passions are much diversified by them; there is also a great complexity of different passions, and a sort of *contrast*, <margin>Contrasts and complexities of passions.</margin> or collection of opposite passions toward the various persons concerned. The most moving *peripeties* <note>sudden reversals of fortune</note> and *remembrances* <note>recognition scenes</note> in epic and dramatic poetry are designed to raise these complicated passions; and in oratory, we study to do the same.

Thus, when strong sentiments of gratitude and vigorous returns of good deeds are observed, they raise in the spectator the highest *love* and *esteem* toward both the benefactor and even the person who is indebted to them, along with a sense of security and delight in virtue. Ingratitude, or intentionally returning bad deeds for good ones, raises the greatest detestation a-

<margin>5. Public passions joined with the selfish.</margin>
VII. The passions of the last class are those in which any of the previous kinds are combined with *selfish passions*, when our own interest is concerned. It is unnecessary to repeat them all here; only this may be noted in general: just as the combination of selfish passions will greatly increase the agitation of the mind, so the opposition of any *selfish interests* which appear to be of great importance will often overcome the *public desires* or *aversions*, or those based on the sense of *virtue* or *honor*; and this is the case in immoral actions committed against *conscience*.

These combinations of passions are often not reflected upon by the person who is driven by them during their peak of intensity. However, a discerning observer may find them by reflecting upon himself or by observing others; and the depiction of them never fails to affect us in the most vivid manner.

## SECT. IV.

### How far our various Affections and Passions are under our Power, either to govern them when raised, or to prevent them from arising: with some general Observations about their Objects.

<margin>
Affections and passions depend greatly on opinions.
</margin>

I. FROM what was said above, it appears that our passions are not as much in our power as some seem to imagine, judging by the *topics* used either to stir them up or calm them down. We are so constituted by nature that as soon as we form the *idea* of certain objects or events, our *desire* or *aversion* will arise toward them; and consequently, our *affections* must depend very much upon the *opinions* we form concerning anything that occurs to our mind—its *qualities*, *tendencies*, or *effects*. Thus, the happiness of every sentient nature is desired as soon as we remove all opinion or *perception* of a conflict of interest between this being and others. The *perception* of morally good qualities is the necessary cause of approval, by our <term>moral Sense</term>, and of stronger love. The cause of *hatred* is the per-

<meta>catchword: ception</meta>

<summary>This section argues that human passions are not directly controlled by the will but are involuntary responses to the opinions and perceptions we hold about objects and events. It explains that our feelings of desire, love, or hatred naturally follow from how we perceive the qualities and interests of others.</summary>
<keywords>passions, affections, self-control, opinions, sentient nature, moral sense, perception, desire, aversion</keywords>

Our *selfish passions* do not differ from our *public ones* in this regard.

THIS may show us some inconsistency in the topics of argument often used to instill *piety* and *virtue*.
