He will perhaps not always be aware that it is the absence of company which causes his uneasiness: A painful sensation dictates nothing of itself; it must therefore be some *reflection* or *instinct*, distinct from the pain, which suggests the remedy. Our benevolence and compassion indeed presuppose some *knowledge* of other sensitive beings, and of what is good or evil for them: But they do not arise from any previous *opinion* that “the good of others tends to the good of the agent.” They are *determinations of our nature*, prior to our choice based on self-interest, which excite us to action as soon as we know other sensitive or rational beings, and have any understanding of their happiness or misery.

In other desires, the case is different. No man is distressed for want of *fine smells, harmonious sounds, beautiful objects, wealth, power, or grandeur* prior to some opinion formed of these things as good, or some prior sensation of their pleasures. In like manner, *virtue* and *honour* as necessarily give us pleasure when they occur to us as *vice* and *contempt* give us pain; but, prior to some experience or opinion of this pleasure, there is no previous uneasy sensation in their absence, as there is in the absence of the *objects of appetite*. <page-num>92</page-num>
<header>The Nature and Conduct</header>

<margin>Section 4.</margin> of these *sensations* preceding our *appetites* has been considered already <note>Section 2, Article 6</note>. The *sensations* accompanying or following our other *desires*, by which they are named *passions*, keep them in a just *balance* with our *appetites*, as was previously observed.

But this holds true in general concerning all our *desires* or *aversions*: that according to the *opinion* or *apprehension* of *good* or *evil*, the *desire* or *aversion* is increased or diminished. Every *gratification* of any *desire* initially gives *pleasure*, and *disappointment* gives *pain*, in proportion to the *intensity* of the *desire*. In the same way, escaping any *object* of *aversion*, though it makes no permanent addition to our *happiness*, initially gives a pleasant *sensation* and relieves us from *misery*, in proportion to the degree of *aversion* or *fear*. So when any *event* to which we had an *aversion* befalls us, we initially experience *misery* in proportion to the degree of *aversion*. Thus, some *pain* follows all *frustration* of *desire* or *aversion*, but it is prior only to those *desires* which are called *appetites*.

III. FROM THIS we see how impossible it is for one to judge the *degrees* of *happiness* or *misery* in others, unless he knows their *opinions*, their *associations* of ideas, and the *degrees* of their *desires* and *aversions*. We see also how much *consequence* our *associations* of ideas and *opinions* have for our *happiness* or *misery*, and for the *command* of our *passions*.

<margin>Associations of ideas and opinions increase or diminish the strength of our desires.</margin>
FOR though in our *appetites* there are uneasy sensations prior to any *opinion*, yet our very *appetites* may be strengthened or weakened, and variously altered by *opinion* or *associations* of ideas. Before their intervention, the bodily appetites are easily satisfied; nature has put it in almost everyone’s power to gratify them enough to support the body and remove pain. But when *opinion*, *confused ideas*, or *fancy* enter in and represent some particular kinds of gratifications, or a great variety of them, as being of great importance; when ideas of *dignity*, *grandeur*, *magnificence*, *generosity*, or any other *moral species* <note>A category or type of moral quality</note> are joined to the objects of appetites, they may provide us with endless labor, vexation, and misery of every kind.

Instead, the constant *indulgence* of any *desire*, its frequent *repetition*, the diverting of our minds from all other *pursuits*, the tone of *conversation* among men of the same *temper* (who often associate together), and the *contagion* found in the very air and facial expressions of the *passionate*, create such wild *associations* of *ideas* that a sudden *conviction* of *reason* will not stop the *desire* or *aversion*. This is no more effective than an argument trying to overcome the loathing or aversions acquired against certain foods or drinks through overindulgence or emetic preparations.

**THIS** is probably the case in those affections which some suppose *natural*, or at least incident to our natures, and yet *absolutely evil*: such as *rancor*, or *disinterested malice*, *revenge*, and *misanthropy*.

We indeed find our nature determined to disapprove of an *Agent* perceived as evil or malicious through *direct intention*; we must desire the destruction of such a being, not only from *self-love*, but from our *benevolence* toward others. Now, when we rashly form opinions of *sects* or *nations* as absolutely evil; or acquire associated ideas of *impiety*, *cruelty*, and *profaneness* that recur every time they are mentioned: when, by repeated reflection upon injuries received, we strengthen our dislike into a stubborn *aversion* and conceive that those who injured us are *directly malicious*; we may be led to act in such a manner that *spectators*—who are unacquainted with our *secret opinions* or *confused apprehensions* of others—may think we have *pure disinterested malice* in our nature, a *very instinct* toward the misery of others. In reality, it is only the overgrowth of a just, natural affection based upon false opinions or confused ideas; just as our *appetites*, upon which our natural life depends, may acquire accidental *loathings* for the most wholesome food.

Our ideas and opinions of mankind are often very rashly formed, but our *affec-*

When our *Ideas* and *Opinions* of the moral qualities of others are just, our affections are generally regular and good; but when we give free rein to our *imagination* and *opinion*, our affections must follow them into all extravagance and folly. <margin>1. Regarding external pleasures.</margin>
THE pleasures and pains of the external senses must certainly be perceived by everyone who enters the world; the former raising some degree of desire, and the latter, aversion. The pains of appetites arise even more certainly than others and occur prior to any opinion. But then, it is largely within our power to keep these sensations pure and unmixed with any foreign ideas; so that the simplest food and clothing, if sufficiently nutritious and healthy, may keep us comfortable just as well as the rarest or most costly. Indeed, when the body is accustomed to simpler things, it is most at ease with them. We are raised to a higher degree of cheerfulness by a small improvement in our table than it is possible to bring a pampered body into by any of the productions of nature. Whatever the body is once accustomed to produces no significant change in it.

<margin>2. Desires for the pleasures of the imagination.</margin>
THE pleasures of the imagination, or of the internal sense of beauty, decency, and harmony, must also be perceived by us. Regularity, proportion, and order in external forms will as necessarily strike the mind as any perceptions of the external senses. But then, as we have no uneasiness of appetite prior to the

AGAIN, the sense and desire of beauty of several kinds is entirely independent of *possession* or *property*; so that the finest *relish* of this kind, and the strongest subsequent *desires*, if we admit no foolish combinations of ideas, may almost everywhere be gratified with the prospects of *nature*, and with the contemplation of the more curious *works of art*, which the owners generally allow to others without restraint. But if this sense or desire of beauty itself is accompanied with the desire for *possession* or *property*; if we let it be guided by *custom*, and receive *associations* of foreign ideas in our imagination regarding *dress*, *equipage*, *furniture*, *retinue*; if we relish only the fashions of the great, or the marks of *distinction* as beautiful; if we let such desires grow strong, we must be very great indeed before we can have any pleasure by this sense: and every disappointment or change of fortune must make us miserable. The same fate may attend the pursuit of *theoretical sciences*, *poetry*, *music*, or *painting*; to excel in these things is granted to but few.

<margin>Sect. 4.</margin>
# A violent *Desire* of *Distinction* and *Eminence* may bring on *Vexation* and *Sorrow* for the longest Life.

<margin>3. The public desires.</margin>
The *pleasures* and *pains* of the *public sense* will also necessarily arise in us. Men cannot live without the society of others and their *good offices*; they must observe both the *happiness* and *misery*, the *pleasures* and *pains* of their fellows: *desire* and *aversion* must arise in the observer. Furthermore, as we cannot avoid closer attachments of *love*, whether from the *instinct* between the sexes, or that toward *offspring*, or from the observation of the *benevolent tempers* of others, or their *particular virtues* and *good offices*, we must feel the sensations of *joy* and *sorrow* from the state of others even in stronger degrees, and have *public desires* to a greater height. All we can do to prevent the *pains* of general benevolence will equally lessen its *pleasures*. If we restrain our *public affection* from growing strong, we diminish our *pleasures* from the good success of others as much as we lessen our compassion for their misfortunes: If we confine our desires to a small circle of acquaintances, or to a cabal or *faction*, we contract our pleasures as much as we do our pains. (The distinction of pleasures and pains into *real* and *imaginary*, or rather into *necessary* and *voluntary*,

**Sect. 4.** would be of some use if we could correct the *imaginations* of others as well as our own; but if we cannot, we are certain that whoever thinks himself *miserable* is really so, however he might possibly have prevented this *misery* through a better *conduct* of his *imagination*. All we can do in this matter is to enjoy a great share of the *pleasures* of the *stronger ties* with fewer *pains* from them, by confining the *stronger degrees* of *love*, or our *friendships*, to persons of *corrected imaginations*, for whom as few of the uncertain objects of *desire* are necessary for *happiness* as possible.] Our *friendship* with such persons may probably be a much greater source of *happiness* to us than of *misery*, since the *happiness* of such persons is more probable than the contrary.

Since there is nothing in our *nature* that determines us to feel *disinterested hatred* toward any person, we may be secure against all the *pains* of *malice* by preventing *false opinions* of our fellows as being absolutely evil, or by guarding against *habitual anger* and *rash aversions*.

our own affections, and we cannot avoid reflecting upon them sometimes: kind and generous affections will appear amiable, and any appearance of cruelty, malice, or even very selfish affections will be disapproved and appear odious. Our *own temper*, as well as that of others, will appear to our moral sense as either lovely or deformed, and will be the occasion of either pleasure or uneasiness. We do not have any specific *appetite* toward virtue that makes us uneasy even before that lovely form appears; but as soon as it appears to any person, as it certainly must very early in life, it never fails to raise *desire*, just as vice raises *aversion*. This is so rooted in our nature that no *education*, *false principles*, *depraved habits*, or even affectation itself can entirely root it out. **LUCRETIUS** and **HOBBES** show themselves in innumerable instances to be struck by some *moral species* <note>moral types or forms of virtue</note>; they are full of expressions of *admiration*, *gratitude*, *praise*, and the *desire* to do good, as well as *censure*, *disapprobation*, and *aversion* toward some forms of *vice*.

**SINCE** then there is no avoiding these desires and perceptions of *morality*, all we can do to secure ourselves in the possession of pleasures of this kind, without pain, consists in “a vigorous use of our reason to discern what actions really tend

<meta>catchword: tend</meta>

<summary>The author argues that humans possess an innate moral sense that naturally admires virtue and finds vice odious, a trait so fundamental that even cynical philosophers like Lucretius and Hobbes cannot help but express moral judgments. To maintain the pleasure derived from this moral sense, one must use reason to identify which actions truly align with virtuous ends.</summary>

WHAT most deeply affects our happiness or misery are the dispositions of those persons with whom we voluntarily contract some nearer *intimacies* of friendship: If we act wisely in this point, we may secure to ourselves the greatest pleasures with the fewest pains by attaching ourselves to persons of real goodness; good offices toward whom are useful to the world. The ties of *blood* are generally very strong, especially toward *offspring*; they need the bridle rather than the spur in all cases where the object is not recommended to a singular love by his good qualities. We may, in a considerable measure, restrain our *natural affection* toward a worthless offspring by setting our *public affections* and our *moral sense* against it, through frequent contemplation of their vices, and of the mischief which may arise to persons of more worth from them if we give them any countenance in their vices.

<summary>The author explains that irrational attachments and prejudices lead to self-condemnation, whereas choosing friends based on true virtue ensures lasting happiness. ### Sect. 4.

REGULATING our **perceptions** of the **actions** of others is of very great **importance**, so that we may not imagine **mankind** to be worse than they really are, and thereby bring upon ourselves a **temperament** full of *suspicion*, *hatred*, *anger*, and *contempt* toward others; which is a constant **state** of **misery**, much worse than all the **evils** to be feared from *gullibility*. If we examine the true *sources* of human *action*, we shall seldom find their **motives** to be worse than *self-love*. **Men** are often subject to *anger*, and upon sudden *provocations* do **injuries** to each other, and that only from **self-love**, without **malice**; but the greatest part of their **lives** is employed in **acts** of *natural affection*, *friendship*, innocent *self-love*, or *love of country*. Petty **party prejudices** are generally founded upon **ignorance** or false **opinions**, and are more likely to move us to *pity* than *hatred*. Such **considerations** are the best **safeguard** against *anger*, *malice*, and *discontent* of **mind** regarding the **order** of **nature**.

WHEN the *moral sense* is thus assisted by a sound *understanding* and *application*, our own *actions* may be a constant source of solid *pleasure*, along with the *pleasures* of *benevolence*, in the highest degree which our nature will admit, and with as few of its pains as possible.

<margin>How far our *sense* of *honor* is in our power.</margin>
As to the *desires of honor*, since we cannot avoid observing or hearing of the *sentiments* of others concerning our *conduct*, we must feel the *desire* for the *good opinions* of others, and *aversion* to their *censures* or *condemnation*: since the one necessarily gives us *pleasure*, and the other *pain*. Now it is impossible to bring all men into the same *opinions* of particular *actions*, because of their different *opinions* of *public good*, and of the *means* of promoting it; and because of *opposite interests*; so that it is often impossible to be secure against all *censure* or *dishonor* from some of our fellows. No one is so much master of *external things*, as to make his honorable *intentions* successful; and yet

<header>The Nature and Conduct</header>

Sect. 4. *Success* is a mark by which many judge the goodness of attempts. Therefore, whoever allows his desire for honor or *applause* to become excessive, without distinguishing between the *people* to whose judgment he submits, runs a great risk of misery. But our natural desire for praise—to speak in a mathematical style—is a compound proportion of the number of those who applaud and their *dignity*.

> “He, therefore, who justly distinguishes between people and acts wisely for the *public good*, may protect himself from much distress caused by unwise criticism, and may obtain the approval of those whose esteem alone is valuable—or at least far outweighs the criticism of others.”

<margin>*The Desire for Wealth and Power.*</margin>
The *desire for wealth* must be as necessary as any other desire of our nature as soon as we realize the usefulness of wealth in satisfying all other desires. While it is desired as the *means* to something else, the desire contributes to our happiness in proportion to the good regulation of the *primary desires* to which it is made subservient. It is within everyone’s power, through a little reflection, to prevent the madness and delusion with which wealth is insatiably pursued for its own sake, without any direct intention of using it. The consideration of the small addition often made

POWER, in the same way, is desired as the means of gratifying other *original desires*; nor can this desire be avoided by those who perceive its usefulness. It is easy to prevent the extravagance of this desire, and many of its resulting pains, by considering "the *danger* of striving for it through harmful means, supporting it by force without the consent of the subject, and employing it for *private interest* in opposition to the *public good*." No mortal is at ease under such subjection; every slave to such a power is an enemy. The possessor must be in a continual state of fear, *suspicion, and hatred*.

<margin>The occasion of fantastic desires.</margin>
THERE is nothing in our nature leading us necessarily into *fantastic desires*; they arise entirely through our *ignorance* and *negligence*. When, through a lack of thought, we allow foolish *associations* of ideas to be formed and imagine certain trifling circumstances to contain something honorable and excellent simply because they are practiced by people of *distinction*. We know how the *inadvertences, negligences, infirmities*, and even *vices* of either great or talented men have been imitated,

V. **THE** goal of all these considerations is to find the most effective way of advancing the happiness of mankind. In order to do this, these points may perhaps appear to be of considerable consequence, since happiness consists in “the highest and most durable gratifications of either all our *desires*, or, if all cannot be gratified at once, of those which tend toward the greatest and most durable *pleasures*, with exemption either from all *pains* and objects of *aversion*, or at least from those which are the most grievous.” The following general observations may be stated beforehand concerning their objects.

<summary>The author explains that failing to cultivate higher interests leads to a cycle of fickle desires for trivial things and constant dissatisfaction. He then defines happiness as the fulfillment of our most durable desires and the avoidance of significant pain, setting the stage for an inquiry into human well-being.</summary>

<page-num>114</page-num>
<header>The Nature and Conduct</header>

<margin>
Sect. 4.
The full pursuit of all kinds of pleasure is impossible.
</margin>

> I. "It is plainly impossible that any man should pursue the gratifications of all these *desires* at once, with sufficient prudence, diligence, and vigor to obtain the highest pleasures of each kind and to avoid their opposite pains."

For, not to mention the *narrowness* of our minds' powers, which makes them incapable of a multiplicity of pursuits at once, the very *methods* of obtaining the highest gratification of the various senses and desires are directly inconsistent with each other. For example, the intense pursuit of the pleasures of the *external senses*, or *sensuality*, is opposite to the pleasures of the imagination and to the study of the creative *arts*, which tend toward the ornament of life: these require labor and application, which are inconsistent with the *voluptuousness* of the external senses; that sensuality, by itself, would engross the entire application of our minds through vain associations of ideas.

AGAIN: The intense pursuit of either of the former kinds of pleasures is often directly inconsistent with *public affections*, and with our *moral sense* and *sense of honor*. These pleasures require a quite different temperament: a mind little set upon selfish pleasures, and strongly possessed with love for others and concern for their inte-

However our desire for **Honor** may be truly *selfish*, yet we know it is never acquired by actions that appear selfish; but rather by those that appear public-spirited, involving a neglect of the pleasures of the external senses and wealth. *Selfishness* is generally accompanied by *Shame*;* and for this reason, we conceal even our *desire for honor* itself, and are ashamed of *praise* in our own presence, even when we are performing beneficent actions with the intention of obtaining it. The pursuits of *wealth* and *power* are often directly opposite to pleasures of all other kinds—at least for the present—however much they may be intended for the future enjoyment of them.

that our public desires may often be disappointed; and every disappointment is uneasy, in proportion to the degree of desire. And therefore, however the *admiration* and *fixed pursuit of virtue* may always secure one stable and constant pleasure of *self-approbation*, yet this enjoyment presupposes a *desire* for the *public good*, which is subject to frequent disappointments; these will be attended with an uneasiness proportioned to the degree of public desire, or the *virtue* upon which we reflect. There seems, therefore, no possibility of securing for ourselves, in our present state, an *unmixed happiness* independent of all other beings. Every apprehension of *good* raises desire, and every *disappointment* of *desire* is uneasy; every object of *desire* is uncertain except *virtue*, but the *enjoyment* of *virtue* presupposes the *desire* for an uncertain object, namely, the *public happiness*. To secure, therefore, independently of all other beings, an invariable and pure happiness, it would be necessary either to have the power of directing all events in the universe, or to root out all *sense* of *evil*, or *aversion* to it, while we retained our *sense* of *good*, but without previous *desire*, the *disappointment* of which could give pain. The rooting out of all *senses* and *desires*, were it practicable, would cut off all *happiness* as well as *misery*: The removing or stopping a part of them might indeed be

But it is plain that we do not have in our power the modeling of our *senses* or *desires* to form them for a private interest: they are fixed for us by the Author of our Nature, subservient to the interest of the *system*; so that each *individual* is made, prior to his own choice, a member of a *great body*, and affected by the fortunes of the *whole*, or at least of many *parts* of it; nor can he break himself off at his pleasure.

<margin>The mistakes of the Stoics about complete happiness.</margin>
This may show the vanity of some of the lower rank of philosophers of the *Stoic* sect, in boasting of an undisturbed happiness and serenity—independent even of the Deity, as well as of their *fellow-creatures*—which is wholly inconsistent with the *order of nature*, as well as with the principles of some of their great leaders; for this, men of wit in their own age did not fail to ridicule them.

That must be a very fantastic scheme of virtue which represents it as a private, *sublimely selfish discipline*, to keep ourselves wholly unconcerned, not only in the changes of fortune regarding our *wealth* or *poverty*, *liberty* or *slavery*, *ease* or *pain*, but even in all external events

If there is anything amiable in human nature, the reflection on which can give us pleasure, it must be kind *disinterested affections* toward our fellows, or toward the *whole*, and its Author and Cause. These affections, when reflected upon, must be one constant source of pleasure in *self-approbation*. But some of these very affections, being directed toward an uncertain object, must occasion pain and directly produce one sort of misery for the virtuous in this life. It is true indeed, it would be a much greater misery to lack such an amiable temper, which alone secures us from the basest and most detestable state of *self-condemnation* and *abhorrence*. But, allowing such a temper to be the necessary cause of one sort of happiness—even the greatest we are capable of—yet it may also be the cause of no inconsiderable pains in this life.

That this affectionate temper is true virtue, and not that *undisturbed selfishness* (were it attainable), everyone who saw them both in practice would readily admit. Would any honest heart relish a speech like this from a *Cato* or an *Aemilius Paulus*?

<margin>irregular imagination</margin>
FURTHERMORE, people of *irregular imaginations* are not easily reformed, nor are their *associations of ideas* broken by every *experience* of the *smallness* of the *good* in the *admired object*. They are often instead set upon *new pursuits* of the same kind, or a greater *variety* of similar *objects*. So their experience of *disappointment*, or of contrary *evils*, does not soon correct their *imaginations* about the *degrees* of *good* or *evil*. The *loss* of *good*, or the *pressure* of any *calamity*, will continue to torment them through their vain *notions* of these *events*, and make them insensitive to the *real good* which they might still enjoy in their present *state*. Thus the *covetous* have smaller *pleasure* in any given *degree* of *wealth*; the *luxurious* from a *splendid table*; the *ambitious* from any given

# SECTION V.

## *A Comparison of the Pleasures and Pains of the several Senses, as to Intensity and Duration.*

I. HAVING considered how far these desires must necessarily affect us, and when they cause pleasure or pain; since, according to the first general *observation*, the pursuit of their various pleasures and the avoidance of their various pains may often be inconsistent with each other; let us next examine which of these various pleasures are *the most valuable*, so as to deserve our pursuit even if it means neglecting the others; and which of these pains are *the most distressing*, so as to be avoided even by enduring other pains if necessary.

> " THE *value* of any pleasure, and the
> " *quantity* or *importance* of any pain, is in
> " a compound proportion of its *intensity*
> " and *duration*." In examining the duration of pleasure, we must include not only the stability of the *object*, but even of our *fancy* <note>imagination, taste, or inclination</note>; for a change in either of these will put an end to it.

<summary>This section begins an investigation into how to evaluate and compare different pleasures and pains based on their intensity and duration. The author notes that the value of a pleasure is determined not just by the object itself, but also by the consistency of one's own imagination or taste.</summary>
<keywords>pleasure, pain, senses, intensity, duration, fancy, value, observation</keywords>

<page-num>127</page-num>
<header>of the PASSIONS. Sect. 5.</header>

<margin>The difficulty of comparing the various pleasures regarding intensity.</margin>
To compare these various Pleasures and Pains regarding their *Intensity* seems difficult because of the *Diversity of Tastes* or *Variations of Temperament* produced by *Custom* and *Education*, which create strange *Associations of Ideas* and form *Habits*. From this, it happens that, though all the different kinds of original Senses and Desires seem equally natural, some people are led into a constant Pursuit of the Pleasures of one kind as the only Enjoyment of Life and are indifferent toward others. Some pursue, or seem to pursue, only the Pleasures of the *external Senses*, and all other Pursuits are made subservient to them. Others are chiefly focused on the Pleasures of the *Imagination* or *internal Senses*. *Social* and *kind Affections* occupy another group, who seem indifferent to all private Pleasure. This last Temperament generally has joined with it a high *moral Sense* and *Love of Honor*. We may sometimes find a high Sense of *Honor* and desire for *Applause* where there is indeed a *moral Sense*, but a very weak one, which is very much perverted so as to be influenced by *popular Opinion* and made subservient to it. In this Character, the Pleasures of the external Senses, or even of the Imagination, have little room, except in so far as they may procure *Distinction*. Now, upon comparing the various Pleasures, perhaps the Judgment of the *Luxurious* would be quite opposite

The *Miser* would pride himself on his wealth above them all. Is there, therefore, no disputing about tastes? Are all people equally happy when they obtain the various enjoyments for which they have a taste? If they are, the dispute is at an end: A fly or maggot in its proper environment is as happy as a *hero*, *patriot*, or *friend* who has just delivered his *country* or *friend* and is surrounded by their grateful *praises*. The *fly* or *maggot* may think so of itself; but who will rely on its judgment when we are certain that it has experienced only one kind of pleasure and is a stranger to the others? May we not, in the same way, find reasons to appeal against the judgment of certain men? Or might some *characters* be found among men who alone are capable of judging in this matter?

<margin>The pleasures of a moral kind are proved superior by the testimony of the virtuous.</margin>

<margin>By the testimony of the vicious.</margin>
Moreover, we need not confine our evidence to the testimony of the *perfectly Virtuous*. The vicious man, though he would be no fit judge if he were entirely abandoned—since he loses his *Sense* of the pleasures of the *moral Kind*, or at least has not experienced them fully—yet he generally retains enough of human nature, and of the *Senses* and *Affections* of our kind, as to sometimes experience even *moral Pleasures*. There is scarcely any mortal who is wholly insensible to all species of *Morality*.

<summary>The author argues that virtue and temperance do not impair physical senses but rather enhance life, as virtuous people testify that moral pleasures far exceed sensory ones. A luxurious *debauchee* has perhaps never felt the *pleasures* of a wise, public-spirited conduct—of an entirely upright, generous, social, and affectionate life, with the sense of his own *moral worth* and merited *esteem* and *love*. Because this course of life is unknown to him, he may despise it in comparison to his *pleasures*. But if in any particular affair a *moral species* <note>In this context, "species" refers to the appearance or specific kind of a moral quality.</note> or *point of honor* has affected him, he will soon despise his sensual pleasures in comparison to the moral ones. If he has a person whom he calls his *friend*, whom he loves for whatever whimsical reasons, he can quit his *debauchery* to serve him—nay, he can run the risk of *wounds* and *death* to rescue him from danger. If his honor is concerned with resenting an *affront*, will he not quit his pleasures and run the risk of the greatest bodily pain to shun the imputation of cowardice or falsehood? He will scorn anyone who tells him that "a *liar* or a coward may be happy enough as long as he has everything necessary for *luxury*." It is in vain to allege that "there is no disputing about *tastes*." For every nature, there are certain *tastes* assigned by the great AUTHOR of all. To the human race, there are assigned a *public taste*, a *moral* one, and a *taste* for *honor*. They cannot root out these senses any more than they can their external *senses*:

The happiness of an *insect* or *brute* will only make an *insect* or *brute* happy. But a nature with further *powers* must have further *enjoyments*.

Nay, let us consider the different *ages* in our own species. We once knew the time when a *hobby-horse*, a *top*, or a *rattle* was sufficient pleasure for us. We grow up; we now relish *friendships*, *honor*, good *deeds*, *marriage*, *offspring*, and serving a *community* or *country*. Is there no difference in these *tastes*? We were happy before; are we no happier now? If not, we have made a foolish change of fancy. A *hobby-horse* is more easily procured than an *employment*; a *rattle* is kept in order with less trouble than a *friend*; and a *top* than a *son*. But this change of fancy does not depend upon our *will*. 

“Our *nature* determines us to certain pursuits in our several stages; and following her dictates is the only way to our happiness. Two states may both be happy, and yet the one is infinitely preferable to the other: Two species may both be content, and yet the pleasures of the one are greater beyond all comparison than those of the other.” The *virtuous man*, who has

III. Again, we see in fact, that in the *virtuous man*, *public affections*, a *moral sense*, and a *sense of honor* actually overcome all other desires or senses, even in their full strength. Here there is the fairest combat, and the success is on the side of *virtue*.

There is indeed an obvious exception against this argument. “Do we not see,” in many instances, “the external senses overcome the *moral*?” But the reply is easy. A constant pursuit of the pleasures of the external senses can never become agreeable without an opinion of *innocence*, or the *absence* of *moral evil*; so that here the *moral sense* is not engaged in the combat. Whereas when a *point of honor*, or a *moral species* <note>the appearance or form of moral goodness</note>, makes anyone despise the pleasures or pains of the external senses, there can be no question made of a real *victory*. The external senses represent these objects in the same manner, even when they are conquered. No one denies to the *virtuous* their *sense of pain, toil*, or *wounds*. They are allowed as lively a sense as others of all *external pleasure* of every kind. The costs of *generosity, humanity, charity*, and *compassion* are acknowledged, even when yielded to *virtue*, to be known to the full. But the *moral sense*, weak as it often is, does not yield even to known *external pleasure, ease*, or *advantage*; but, where there is a depraved *taste* and a weak *understanding*, *private advantage*, or the avoiding of some external evil, may make actions appear innocent which are not; and then the *moral sense* gives no opposition. All the victory on such occasions is only this: that *private external advantage* surmounts our aversion to *dishonor* by making us do actions which *others* will censure, but we esteem innocent. ### Sect. 5. by some *moral Species*, <note>"Species" here refers to the mental representation, form, or appearance of a moral quality.</note> raised by a false *Imagination*.

---

SET before men in the clearest light all external pleasures, but strip them of their borrowed notions of *dignity*, *hospitality*, *friendship*, *generosity*, *liberality*, and the *sharing of pleasure*; let no regard be given to the *opinions* of others, to *reputation*, to avoiding *reproach*, or to *company*. Separate from the pursuit of wealth all thoughts of a *family*, *friends*, *relations*, or *acquaintances*; let *wealth* be regarded only as the means of private pleasure for the external senses, or for the imagination, to the possessor alone. Let us divide our confused ideas and consider things simply and apart from each other; and in opposition to these desires, set even the weakest *moral species*, and see if they can prevail over it. On the other hand, let us examine as much as we please a *friendly*, *generous*, *grateful*, or *public-spirited ac-* <margin>part</margin> *tion*; strip it of all external pleasure, and it will still appear all the more beautiful; the longer we focus our attention on it, the more we admire it. What is it that we feel in our own hearts, determining, as it were, our fate regarding happiness or misery? What sort of sensations are the most lively and delightful? In what sort of possessions does the highest joy and self-satisfaction consist? Who has ever felt the pleasure of a generous

<meta>catchword: friendly</meta>

<summary>The author argues that external pleasures and wealth only seem valuable because they are associated with moral concepts like friendship and generosity; once these associations are removed, even a small moral virtue is more appealing than pure sensory indulgence. He suggests that true happiness and self-satisfaction are found in moral, public-spirited actions rather than in private, external sensations.</summary>

Some crimes appear so horrid, and some actions so cruel and detestable, that there is hardly any man who would not rather suffer death than be conscious of having done them.

<margin>Section 5.</margin> THE second cause of mistake in this matter is this: "Avoiding moral evil by enduring *external pain* does not diminish the *sense* of the pain; but on the other hand, the *motive* of avoiding grievous pain really diminishes the *moral evil* of the action performed with that intent." So, in such instances, we compare *external pain* in its full strength with a *moral pain* of a lighter sort, which has been alleviated by the greatness of the temptation<note>Treatise 2. Section 7, 9. Corollary 3.</note>. To make a just comparison, it should be phrased this way: "Which would a man choose: to be tortured to death, or—without any temptation or necessity—to have tortured another person, or a dear friend, or a child to death?" The question is not whether a man will betray his friend or country for fear of torture, but "whether it is better to voluntarily, and under no fear, betray a friend or our country, than to suffer torture, or the pain of gout or kidney stones equal to torture?" Upon making such comparisons as these, we should find certain other pains and miseries superior to any *external pain*. When we judge the state of others, we would not be long in suspense as to which of these evils to

VII. We have so far only compared, on one side, the *public* and *moral sense* and the *sense of honor* together, with the *external senses*, the *pleasures of imagination*, and *external advantage* or *disadvantage* on the other. The reason for joining them in this way must be obvious: to a mind not biased by any false *apprehensions* of things, those first three *senses* and *desires* actually work together to encourage the same *course of action*. For promoting the *public good* can never be opposite to *private virtue*, nor can the *desire for virtue* ever lead to anything harmful to the *public*. If men also held true *opinions*, *honor* could only be obtained through *virtue* or by *serving the public*.

But it is enough for some *writers*—who pretend to be wonderfully shrewd in their observations on human nature and are fond of making the whole world, as well as themselves, a *selfish* generation, incapable of any real *excellence* or *virtue*, and without any natural *disposition* toward a *public interest* or toward any *moral species*—to simply use a “set of different *words* from those commonly used, yet including the same *natural dispositions* <note>Ibid.</note> or presupposing them,” even if an inattentive reader might not notice it. They are then sufficiently equipped to show that there is no real *virtue*, and that everything is merely *hypocrisy*, *disguise*, *artifice*, or *self-interest*. “To be honored, highly esteemed, valued, praised, or, on the contrary, to be despised, undervalued, censured, or condemned; to be proud or ashamed, are *words* without any meaning if we take away a *moral sense*.” Let this sense be as capricious, inconstant, and different in various people as they please to allege; “there must be a *sense of morality*, and it must be natural, if the *desire for esteem*, *parental pride*, or *shame* are natural.”

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the moral sense and the sense of honor, specifically comparing the strength of different motivations and the prioritization of avoiding evil over seeking positive good.</meta>

<page-num>148</page-num>
<header>The Nature and Conduct</header>

<margin>Section 5.</margin>
---

To make this comparison between the *public* and *moral senses* on the one hand, and that of *honor* on the other, it is to be observed that all *aversion* to *evil* is stronger than the *desire* for *positive good*. There are many sorts of *positive good* without which anyone may be at ease and enjoy others of a different kind; but *evil* of almost any kind, in a high degree, may make life intolerable. The *avoiding* of *evil* is always allowed as a more extenuating circumstance in a *crime* than the *prospect* of *positive good*. Therefore, to make just comparisons of the prevalence of several desires or senses, their various goods should be opposed to each other, and their *evils* to each other, and the *pleasures* of one should not be compared with the *pains* of another.

*PUBLIC affections*, in their closer ties, frequently overcome not only the *pleasures* of *honor* but even the *pains* of *shame*. This is the most common event in life: that for some perceived interest of *offspring*, *families*, or *friends*, *men* should neglect opportunities for gaining *honor* and even incur *shame* and *contempt*. In *actions* done for the service of a *party*, there can be no comparison, for *honor* is often a motive on both sides.

<meta>catchword: It is</meta>

<summary>The author explains that the aversion to evil is a stronger motivator than the desire for positive good and notes that personal ties to family and friends often outweigh the desire for honor or the fear of shame.</summary>
<keywords>moral sense, public sense, honor, shame, aversion to evil, positive good, public affections, family, social ties</keywords>

--- Page 179 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the relative strength of different human motivations, specifically contrasting the desire for honor and the fear of shame with other natural affections and the pursuit of virtue.</meta>

<page-num>149</page-num>
<header>of the PASSIONS. Sect. 5.</header>

It is also certain that the *fear of shame*, in some instances, will overcome all other *desires* whatsoever, even natural *affection*, love of *pleasure*, *virtue*, *wealth*, and even *life* itself. This *fear* has excited parents to the murder of their offspring; has persuaded men to the most dangerous enterprises; to squander their fortunes, to counteract their *duty*, and even to throw away their *lives*. The distraction and convulsion of mind observable in these *conflicts* of honor with virtue and public *affection* shows how *unnatural* that state is, wherein the strongest *principles of action*, naturally designed to cooperate and assist each other, are thus set in opposition.

<margin>Honor and Virtue</margin>
It is perhaps impossible to state anything universally concerning the superiority of the *desire for honor* on the one hand, or that of the *desire for virtue* and public *good* on the other. *Habits* or *custom* may perhaps determine the victory on either side. Men in high stations, who have long indulged the *desire for honor* and have formed the most frightful apprehensions of *contempt* as the worst of evils; or even those in lower stations, who have been long accustomed to value *reputation* in any particular area and dread *dishonor* in that point, may have a *fear of shame* superior

rior to all aversions. On the contrary, men who have much indulged their good nature, or reflected much upon the excellence of *virtue* itself, independent of *honor*, may find affections of this kind more prevalent than the fear of *shame*.

To compare the *moral sense* with the *sense of honor*, we must find cases where the agent condemns an action, with all its present circumstances, as evil, and yet fears *infamy* for omitting it, without any other unequal motives on either side. Or, we must find cases where one may obtain *praise* through an action, even though omitting that action would appear to himself as significant a virtue as the expected *praise* would represent the action to be. The common examples, in which some who claim a deep knowledge of *human nature* take great pride, do not have these necessary circumstances.

<margin>Duels are not proper examples.</margin>
When a man condemns *dueling* in his private sentiments and yet practices it, we have indeed significant evidence of the strength of this *desire for honor* or *aversion to shame*, since it surpasses the fear of death. But here, on the one hand, besides the fear of *shame*, there is the fear of *constant insults*, of losing all the advantages that depend on a reputation for courage, and sometimes even some form of *virtue* and the public good, in restraining an insolent

The *moral sense* is seldom much concerned: for however *men* may condemn *voluntary dueling*; however they may blame the *age* for the custom, or censure the *laws* as defective, yet generally, in their present case, *dueling* appears to be a necessary piece of *self-defense* against insulting injuries and affronts for which the *law* has provided no redress, and consequently leaves *men* to the natural rights of *self-defense* and the *prosecution of injuries*. The case seems to them the same as that of thieves and *night-robbers*, who may be put to death by private persons when there is no hope of catching them by the *law*. These are certainly the notions of those who condemn *dueling* and yet practice it.

It is foreign to our present purpose to expose the fallacy of these arguments in defense of *duels* as they are commonly practiced among us; when *men*, from a sudden *anger* over some trifling or imaginary *affronts*—the ignoring of which would appear honorable in every wise *man's* eyes—expose themselves, and often their dearest friends, to death, and risk the ruin of their own families as well as that of their *adversary*; though *success* in such attempts can have no tendency to justify them

### Sect. 5. Against the dishonorable charge, or to procure any honor from men of worth.

<margin>
Nor the case of Lucretia.
</margin>

The celebrated instance of Lucretia <note>Livy, Book 1, chapter 57.</note> is even less to our purpose. Some speak as if “she indeed would rather have died than consented to the crime; but the crime did not appear as great an evil as the *dishonor*; and she submitted to the guilt to avoid the *shame*.” Let us consider this renowned argument. Was there then no motive on either side except fear of shame and a *sense of duty*? If we look into the story, we shall find that to persuade her to consent, there conspired—besides the fear of shame and of death, which she little regarded—the hope of noble *revenge*, or rather of *justice* against the ravisher and the entire family of the tyrant; moreover, the hopes of a nobler fame through her future *conduct*; and the fear of suffering that insult by force, to which she was tempted to consent, and in such a manner that she could have had no redress. All these considerations combined to make her consent. On the other side, there was only the *moral sense* of a crime thus lessened by the most grievous necessity, and by the hope of doing *justice* to her husband’s honor and rescuing her country: Nay,

Could she not have saved both her *reputation* and her *life* at once by consenting; since in that virtuous age she might have expected secrecy from the prince, as boasting of such attempts would have been dangerous even to the most powerful man in *Rome*?

It is not easy to find appropriate room for a comparison, even in fictitious cases, between these two *principles*. If there were a person who had no belief in any deity, or of any reality in religion, in a country where his *worldly interest* would not suffer from a reputation for *atheism*; and yet he knew that the profession of zealous devotion would lead to his *honor*: if such a person could have any sense of morality, particularly an aversion to *hypocrisy*, then his profession of religion would demonstrate the superiority of the *sense of honor*; and his disclosure of his true sentiments, or *neglect* of religion, would demonstrate the balance to be on the other side. I presume that in *England* and *Holland*, we have more instances of the latter than the former. It is true that our gentlemen who adopt the name of *freedom* <note>Referring to the "freethinkers" of the 18th century who challenged traditional religious authority.</note> may now have their hopes of *honor* from their own *party*, as well as from others.

<margin>
The pleasures of imagination are greater than those of external senses.

IX. THE pleasures of the *internal senses*, or of the *imagination*, are acknowledged by all who have any tolerable taste for them to be a much superior happiness to those of the *external senses*, even if those were enjoyed to the full.

OTHER comparisons might be made, but with less use or certainty in any general conclusions that might be drawn from them.

THE pleasures of *wealth* or *power* are proportioned to the gratifications of the *desires* or *senses* which the agent intends to satisfy by them. So that, for the reasons offered above, wealth and power give greater happiness to the *virtuous* than to those who consult only *luxury* or *external splendor*. If these *desires* have grown *enthusiastic* <note>In this context, "enthusiastic" refers to an irrational, excessive, or obsessive passion.</note> and habitual, without regard to any other end than *possession*, they are an endless source of vexation without any real *enjoyment*; a perpetual *craving* without *nourishment* or *digestion*; and they may surmount all other *affections*,

3. *Public happiness* is indeed, as to external *appearance*, a very uncertain *object*; nor is it often in our power to remedy it by changing the course of *events*. There are perpetual changes in mankind from pleasures to pains, and often from virtue to vice. Our *public desires* must therefore frequently subject us to *sorrow*; and the pleasures of the *public sense* must be very inconstant. It is true indeed that a general *goodwill* toward our kind is the most constant inclination of the mind, which grows upon us by indulgence; nor are we ever dissatisfied with the *thought*: the *uncertainty* therefore is wholly due to the *objects*. If any considerations can be found to make it probable that, in the whole, all events tend toward happiness, this implicit hope indeed may make our *public affections* the greatest and most constant source of pleasure. Frequent *reflection* on this is the best support under the sorrow arising from particular evils befalling our *fellow creatures*. In our closer *attachments* brought upon ourselves, we may procure for ourselves the greatest *enjoyments* of this kind, with considerable *security* and *constancy*, by choosing for our *friends*, or dearest *favorites*, persons of just apprehensions of things, who are subjected only to the *necessary evils* of life, and can enjoy all the

<margin>The duration of the various pains considered.</margin>
XI. **W**ere we to consider the *duration* of the various *pains*, we may find it generally corresponds to the *duration* of their *pleasures*. As to the external *senses*, the old *Epicurean* consolation is generally just:

> "Where the *pain* is violent, it shortens our
> " *lifespan*; when it does not shorten
> " our *lifespan*, it is generally either *tolerable*, or admits of frequent *intermissions*;"

and then, when the external pain is once past, no mortal is the worse for having endured it. There is nothing uneasy in the *reflection*, when we have no present *pain*, or fear no *return* of it.

THE pains of the *moral sense* and *sense of honor* are almost perpetual. *Time*, the refuge of other sorrows, gives us no *relief* from these. All other pleasures are made insipid by these pains, and life itself an uneasy *burden*. Our very *self*, our *nature*, is disagreeable to us. It is true, we do not always observe the *vicious* to be uneasy. The *deformity* of *vice* often does not appear to those who continue in a *course* of it. Their *actions* are under some *disguise* of *innocence*, or even of *virtue* itself. When this *mask* is pulled off—as it often happens, nor can any vicious man prevent its happening—*vice* will appear as a *fury*, whose *aspect* no *mortal* can bear. This we may see in one *vice*, which perhaps has had fewer false or fantastic associations of favorable ideas than any, namely, *cowardice*, or such a selfish love of life and *aversion* to *death*, or to the very *hazard* of it, as hinders a man from serving his *country* or his *friend*, or supporting his own *reputation*. How few of our gay gentlemen can bear to be reputed *cowards*, or even secretly to imagine themselves void of *courage*? This is not tolerable to any, however negligent they may be about other points of *morality*. Other *vices* would appear equally odious and despicable, and bear as horrid an *aspect*, were they equally stripped of the *disguises* of

*Virtue*. A vicious man has no other security against the appearance of this terrifying *form* than *ignorance* or *inadvertence*. If *truth* breaks in upon him, as it often must when any *adversity* stops his intoxicating pleasures, or when *observers* use *freedom* in criticizing his conduct, he is rendered perpetually miserable, or must fly to the only remedy that *reason* would suggest: making all possible reparation for injuries and starting a new course of life. The necessity of this is not replaced by any remedy suggested by the *Christian revelation*.

THE pains of the public *sense* are very lasting. The *misery* of others, whether in past or present ages, is a matter of very uneasy *reflection*, and must continue to be so if their state appears as a whole to be absolutely *evil*. ## SECT. VI.

### Some general Conclusions concerning the best Management of our Desires. With some Principles necessary to Happiness.

WE see therefore, upon comparing the several kinds of Pleasures and Pains, both as to *intensity* and *duration*, that “the whole sum of *interest* lies on the side of *virtue*, *public spirit*, and *honor*. That to forfeit these pleasures in whole or in part for any other *enjoyment* is the most foolish bargain; and on the contrary, to secure them by sacrificing all others is the *truest gain*.”

<margin>Constant discipline is necessary.</margin>
THERE is one general observation to be stated first, which appears to be of the greatest necessity for the just management of all our *desires*: namely, that we should, as much as possible in all affairs of importance to ourselves or others, prevent the *violence* of their *confused sensation*, and stop their *propensities* from breaking out into *action*, until we have fully examined the real *importance* of the object of our *desires*

<sig>M 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: or</meta>

<summary>The author concludes that true self-interest aligns with virtue and public spirit, arguing that sacrificing these for other enjoyments is a poor bargain. He emphasizes the necessity of discipline to restrain impulsive desires until their true importance can be rationally evaluated.</summary>
<keywords>virtue, public spirit, honor, management of desires, happiness, self-discipline, intensity and duration, moral interest</keywords>

<margin>Resignation of sensual pleasures.</margin>
To make the necessary *resignation* of other pleasures easier, we must frequently suggest these previously mentioned considerations to ourselves:
> " *External pleasures are short and transitory, leave no agreeable reflection, and are of no advantage to us once they have passed; we are no better off than if we had lacked them altogether.* "

In the same way,
> " *past pains give us no unpleasant reflection, nor are we the worse for having endured them. If they are violent, our existence will probably be short; if not, they are tolerable, or allow long intervals of ease.* "
Let us add to these a *stoical consideration*:
> " *that external pains give us a noble opportunity for moral pleasures in fortitude and submission to the order of the whole, if we bear them resolutely; but if we fret under them, we do not alleviate the suffering, but rather increase it through discontent or sullenness.* "
When external pains must be endured voluntarily to avoid *moral evil*, we must, as much as possible, present to ourselves

We cannot expect any gratitude for what was done only to please our own *vanity*: the *indigent* easily see this and justly consider, upon the whole, how much they have profited.

A **WISE** man in the management of his affairs must do much even out of deference to the *follies* of others, as well as for his own *convenience*, to support that general good *opinion* which must be maintained by those who would be publicly useful. His *expenses* must be in some way suited to his *fortune*, to avoid the accusation of *avarice*. If indeed what is saved in *private expenses* is employed in *generous acts*, there is little danger of this charge. Such a *middle ground* may be kept as to be above *censure*, and yet below any *pretense* of *honor* or *distinction* in these matters. If one corrects his own *imagination* in these things, he will be in no danger of doing anything harmful to please others. He is still in a state fit to judge the real *importance* of everything which occurs to him, and will gratify the false *tastes* of others no further than it is consistent with, and subservient to, *some nobler views*.

<margin>Sect. 6.</margin>
Where the *desire for distinction* is not corrected, our equals become our adversaries: the grandeur of another is our misery, and makes our own enjoyments tasteless. There is only one way of making this disposition tolerable, but this way is almost inconsistent with the *inclination* itself: namely, "continually to associate with our inferiors and compare ourselves with them." But if inconstant *Fortune* or their own merit raises any of them to equal us, our pleasure is lost; or we must lower ourselves to those who are still inferior and abandon the society of every person whose *skill* or *merit* raises them. How poor a thought is this!

The pursuits of the *learned* often have as much folly in them as any others, when studies are not valued according to their *use in life* or the real pleasures they contain, but for their *difficulty* and *obscurity*, and consequently their *rarity* and *distinction*. Indeed, an abuse may be made of the most noble and manly studies—even of *morals*, *politics*, and *religion* itself—if our admiration and desire end with the knowledge itself and not upon

<margin>Ideas of Divinity arise from the internal senses.</margin>

### III. **UNDER** this heading of our internal sense, we must observe one natural effect of it: that it leads us to apprehensions of a **DEITY**. Grandeur, beauty, order, and harmony, wherever they occur, raise a belief in a **MIND**, of *design*, and *wisdom*. Everything great, regular, or proportioned excites veneration, either toward itself, if we imagine it animated, or, if not animated, toward some perceived cause. No inclination of our mind is more natural than this, no effect more universal. One has better reason to deny the inclination between the sexes to be natural than a disposition in mankind toward religion.

**WE** cannot open our eyes without discerning *grandeur* and *beauty* everywhere. Whoever receives these ideas feels an inward *veneration* arise. We may fall into a thousand vain reasonings: foolish, limited notions of **DIVINITY** may be formed, as being attached to particular places

After what has been observed by so many ingenious authors, both *ancient* and *modern*, one cannot be at a loss as to which opinion to choose. We may, on this occasion, only consider the evidence of divine **goodness** appearing in the *structure of our own nature*, and in the order of our *passions and senses*.

<image-desc>A horizontal calligraphic flourish at the beginning of the line.</image-desc> they all aim at good, either private or public: and by them each particular agent is made, in a great measure, subservient to the good of the whole. Mankind are thus insensibly linked together, and make one great *System*, by an invisible union. He who voluntarily continues in this union, and delights in employing his power for his kind, makes himself happy: He who does not continue this union freely, but attempts to break it, makes himself wretched; nor yet can he break the *Bonds of Nature*. His *public sense*, his *love of honor*, and the very *necessities of his nature*, will continue to make him depend upon his *system*, and engage him to serve it, whether he inclines to it or not. Thus we are formed with a view to a general *good end*; and may in our own nature discern a universal mind watchful for the whole.

The same is observable in the order of our *external senses*. The simple productions of nature, which are useful to any species of animals, are also pleasing to them; and the harmful or useless objects are made disagreeable. Our external sensations are no doubt often *painful*, when our bodies are in a dangerous state; when they lack supplies of nourishment; when anything external would be injurious to them. But if it appears, “that the general *laws*

Now, it must appear that there is no room for complaint that “our external sense of pain is made too acute,” as is evident from the multitudes we see daily who are so careless about preserving the blessing of *health*. many are so extravagant as to waste it and expose themselves to external *pains* for very trifling reasons. Can we then complain about the friendly *admonitions* of *Nature*, even when joined with some *severity*, when we see that they are barely sufficient to restrain us from ruin? The same may be said of other kinds of *pains*. *Shame* and *remorse* are never to be called too severe, while so many people are not sufficiently restrained by them. Our *compassion* and friendly *sense* of *sorrow*—what are they but the alarms and exhortations of a kind, impartial *Father*, intended to engage his children to relieve a distressed *brother*? Our *anger* itself is a necessary piece of management, by which every harmful attempt is made dangerous to its author.

<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous section, providing a "reductio ad absurdum" by imagining how a malicious creator would have designed humans, thereby proving the benevolence of the actual Creator through the absence of such designs.</meta>

<page-num>180</page-num>
<header>The Nature and Conduct</header>

### Sect. 6.

<image-desc>A decorative pen flourish, resembling a series of waves or a horizontal scroll, precedes the first paragraph.</image-desc>

If we allowed our imagination room to conceive what sort of *mechanism*, what *constitutions* of *senses* or *affections* a malicious, powerful being might have formed, we would soon see how little evidence there is for any such fear concerning the AUTHOR of this world. Our *mechanism*, as far as we have ever yet discovered, is entirely designed for good. No cruel device, no *art* or *contrivance* exists to produce evil: no such goal or purpose seems ever to be intended. How easy would it have been to have designed some necessary engines of *misery* that serve no purpose; some limb with no other function but to be a source of torment; *senses* incapable of experiencing surrounding objects without pain; eyes pained by *light*; a palate offended by the *fruits of the earth*; a skin as sensitive as the membranes of the eye, and yet some more intense pain forcing us to endure these torments? Human society might have been made as uncomfortable as the company of enemies, and yet a constant, more violent motive of fear might have forced us to endure it. Malice, resentment, and distrust might have been our natural temperament. Our honor and *self-approbation* might have depended upon committing *injuries*; and the torments of others could have been made our delight—which we still could not have enjoyed because of constant fear. Many such contrivances we

<meta>catchword: may</meta>

<summary>Hutcheson argues that human anatomy and psychology are designed for well-being, noting that a malicious creator could have easily designed us for constant misery. He points out that our physical senses and social instincts are naturally oriented toward goodness and cooperation rather than pain.</summary>
<keywords>benevolence, divine design, malice, mechanism, senses, human society, self-approbation, torment, nature of the passions</keywords>

ficient to form Ideas of *Rights*, *Laws*,
*Constitutions*; so as to preserve large So-
cieties in Peace and Prosperity, and promote
a *general Good* amidst all the *private In-*
*terests.*

<page-num>182</page-num>
<header>The Nature and Conduct</header>

### Sect. 6.

<image-desc>A horizontal wavy decorative line.</image-desc>

IF from the present order of nature, in which *good* appears far superior to *evil*, we have just reason to conclude the DEITY is benevolent, it is inconceivable “that any Being, who desires the happiness of others, should not desire a greater degree of happiness for them rather than a less; and that consequently the whole series of events is the best possible, and contains in the whole the greatest possible *absolute good*:” especially since we have no reason to assume any private *interest* which an universal MIND could have in view in opposition to the greatest good of the whole. Nor are the particular evils occurring to our observation any just objection against the perfect goodness of the universal PROVIDENCE toward us, who cannot know how far these evils may be necessarily connected with the means of the greatest possible absolute good.

<margin>The conduct of our public sense and affections.</margin>
IV. IN managing our *public sense* of the state of others, we must beware of one common mistake, namely: “thinking every person to be miserable in those circumstances which we imagine would make ourselves miserable.” We may easily find that the *lower rank* of mankind, whose only income is their bodily labor, enjoy as much *cheerfulness, contentment,*

<meta>catchword: contentment,</meta>

<summary>Hutcheson argues that a benevolent Deity would naturally intend the greatest possible good for the world, suggesting that observable evils are necessary parts of a larger, perfect system. He also warns against the error of assuming that people in lower social stations are miserable simply because we would find their conditions difficult.</summary>
<keywords>Deity, benevolence, absolute good, Providence, public sense, universal Mind, happiness, social class, contentment</keywords>

contentment, health, and *gaiety*, in their own way, as any in the highest station of life. Both their minds and bodies are soon adapted to their state. The farmer and laborer, when they enjoy the bare necessities of life, are at ease. Through necessity and experience, they often have more correct imaginations than others can acquire through *philosophy*. This thought is indeed a poor excuse for a base, selfish oppressor who, imagining poverty to be a great misery, bears down hard upon those in a low station of life and deprives them of their natural conveniences, or even of bare necessities. But this consideration may support a compassionate heart that is too deeply touched by perceived miseries of which the sufferers themselves are unaware.

THE pains of this *sense* are not easily removed. They are not lessened by the distinction of pains into *real* and *imaginary*. Much less will it remove them to consider how much of human misery is due to their own *folly* and *vice*. *Folly* and *vice* are themselves the most pitiable evils. ### Sect. 6.
Malice may seem to have a place in our constitution; but how rare and how brief it is, in comparison to the years spent in steady, kind pursuits for the good of a *family*, a *party*, or a *country*? How great a part of human actions flow directly from *humanity* and *kind affection*? How many censurable actions are due to the same source, only to be blamed on *inadvertence*, or an attachment to too narrow a *system*? How few are due to anything worse than *selfish passions* that exceed their proper proportion?

In this, people are prone to let their imaginations dwell on all the *robberies*, *piracies*, *murders*, *perjuries*, *frauds*, *massacres*, and *assassinations* they have ever heard of or read in history, concluding from this that all mankind is very wicked. This is as if a *court of justice* were the proper place to make an estimate of the *morals* of mankind, or a *hospital* to judge the *healthfulness* of a climate. <margin>Section 6.</margin>
domestic actions are overlooked only because they are so common; just as one great danger, or one month’s sickness, shall become a frequently repeated story during a long life of health and safety.

THE pains of the external senses are quite frequent, but how short in comparison to the long stretches of health, ease, and pleasure? How rare is the instance of a life with one-tenth spent in violent pain? How few lack absolute necessities; indeed, do they not even have something to spend on gaiety and ornament? The pleasures of *beauty* are exposed to everyone to some degree. Those kinds of beauty which require *property* for their full enjoyment are not ardently desired by many. The good of every kind in the universe is plainly superior to the evil. How few would accept *annihilation* rather than a continuance in life in the middle state of age, health, and fortune? Or what departed spirit, who had considered human life, would not, rather than perish, take the hazard of it again by returning into a body in the state of infancy?

> —— *Who would lose,*
> *For fear of pain, this intellectual being,*
> *These thoughts which wander through eternity,*
> *To perish rather, swallowed up and lost*
> *In the wide womb of uncreated night,*
> *Devoid of sense and motion —— ?*
->Milton’s *Paradise Lost*, Book 2.<-

### Sect. 6.

<image-desc>A small decorative flourish consisting of a wavy horizontal line with two symmetrical loops.</image-desc>

THESE thoughts plainly show a prevalence of good in the world. But still our public sense finds much matter for compassionate sorrow among men. The many are in a tolerably good state; but who can be unconcerned for the distressed *few*? They are few in comparison to the whole, and yet they are a great *multitude*.

WHAT parent would be much concerned at the pains of *teething*, if they were sure the pains would be short and end well? Or at the pain of a medicine, or an incision, which was necessary for a cure and would certainly accomplish it? Is there then no *parent* in nature, no *physician* who sees what is necessary for the whole, and for the good of each individual throughout their entire existence, as far as is consistent with the general good? <margin><note>Red ink bracket and checkmark in the margin</note></margin> Can we expect, in this our childhood of existence, to understand all the design and art of this parent and physician of nature? May not some harsh discipline be necessary for <insert><note>Word enclosed in a red ink box</note>good?</insert> May not many natural evils be necessary to prevent future moral evils, and to correct the temperaments of the agents—indeed, even to introduce moral good? Are not *suffering* and *distress* required before there can be room for generous compassion, relief, and liberality? Can there be *forgiveness* or *returning good for evil*,

<margin>Section 6.</margin>
unless there is some *moral evil*? Must the *whole* lack the eternally delightful *consciousness* of such *actions* and *dispositions* to prevent a few transient *sensations* of pain or natural evil? Might there not be some unseen necessity for the greatest universal good, that there should be an *order of beings* no more perfect than we are, who are sometimes subject to error and wrong *affections*? May not all the present disorders that accompany this state of prevalent *order* be rectified by *directing providence* in a future part of our *existence*? This belief in a <term>DEITY</term>, a <term>PROVIDENCE</term>, and a future state are the only sure supports for a good mind. Let us then acquire and strengthen our love and concern for this *whole*, and acquiesce in what the governing <term>MIND</term>, who presides over it, is ordering in the wisest manner for its most universal good, though it is not yet fully known to us.

<margin>The necessity of believing in a future state.</margin>

We should often find those *phantoms* of *vice* and *corruption* which torment the *jealous*, vanishing before the bright warmth of a *thoroughly* good *temper*, resolved to search for everything lovely and good, and averse to thinking any evil.

<margin>
Conduct of the moral sense and sense of honor.
</margin>
V. In governing our *moral sense* and *desires* for *virtue*, nothing is more necessary than to study the *nature* and *tendency* of human *actions*; and to extend our views to the *whole species*, or to all *sentient natures*, as far as they can be affected by our *conduct*. Our *moral sense* thus regulated, and constantly followed in our *actions*, may be the most constant source of the most *stable pleasure*. The same *conduct* is always the most probable means of obtaining the *pleasures* of *honor*. If there is a distinction between *truth* and *falsehood*, *truth* must be stronger than *falsehood*: It must be more probable that *truth* will generally prevail; that the real *good tendency* of our *actions*, and the *wisdom* of our *intentions* will be known; and *misrepresentations* or *partial views* will vanish. Our *desire for honor* is not confined to our present state. The *prospect of future glory* is a strong motive for *action*. And thus the *time*, in which our *character* may have the chance of obtaining *justice*, has no other limits

<summary>A positive disposition can dispel the suspicions of jealousy and turn rivals into friends. Properly governing the moral sense requires studying how our actions affect all sentient beings, which ultimately leads to lasting pleasure and the attainment of honor through the eventual triumph of truth.</summary>

THAT we may not be engaged in anything contrary to the public good, or to the true schemes of virtue, by the desire of *false honor*, or *fear* of *false shame*, it is of great use to examine the real *dignity* of those we converse with, and to confine our *intimacies* to the truly virtuous and wise. From such we can expect no **honor**, except according to our sincere pursuit of the *public good*; nor need we ever fear any *shame* in such a course. THE tender regards for a *family* and *offspring* are often the strongest *bonds* restraining a generous mind from submitting to *death*. What shall be the *fate* of a *wife*, a *child*, a *friend*, or a *brother* when we are gone are the frequent subjects of grievous *anxiety*. BUT if we exist and think after death, and retain our *senses* of good and evil, no consolation against death can be suggested to a *wicked man*; but for the *virtuous*, there are the best grounds of *hope* and *joy*. Now there is no presumption that the absolute *misery* of any virtuous person can be necessary to any good end: such persons, therefore, are the most likely to enjoy a state of perfect happiness.

<header>The Nature and Conduct</header>

<margin>Section 6.</margin>
“...way convinced of it. Let us inquire then what is meant by it.”

If by natural we mean “that which we enjoy or do when we first begin to exist, or to think,” it is impossible to know what *state*, *temper*, or *actions* are natural. Our *natural state* in this *sense* differs little from that of a *plant*, except in some accidental *sensations* of *hunger*, or of *ease* when we are well nourished.

Some elaborate treatises of great philosophers about <term>innate ideas</term>, or principles, practical or speculative, amount to no more than this: “That in the beginning of our existence we have no *ideas* or *judgments*.” They might have added, too, of *sight*, *taste*, *smell*, *hearing*, *desire*, and *volition*. Such dissertations are just as useful for understanding human *nature* as it would be, in explaining the <term>animal economy</term> <note>the physical and physiological organization of a living being</note>, to prove that the *fetus* is animated before it has *teeth*, *nails*, or *hair*, or before it can eat, drink, digest, or breathe; or, in a *natural history of vegetables*, to prove that *trees* begin to grow before they have *branches*, *leaves*, *flowers*, *fruit*, or *seed*; and consequently that all these things were adventitious <note>acquired from without; not inherent</note>, or the *effect of art*.

# of the PASSIONS.

<margin>Sect. 6.</margin>
In others we may observe *humanity*, *compassion*, and *good nature* sometimes raised by *habits*, as we say, to an *excess*.

If we were to strike a *medium* of the several passions and affections as they appear in the whole species of mankind, to conclude from there what has been the natural *balance* prior to any change made by custom or habit—which we see casts the *balance* to either side—we would perhaps find the *medium* of the *public affections* not very far from a sufficient *counterbalance* to the *medium* of the *selfish* ones. Consequently, the *overbalance* on either side in particular characters is not to be looked upon as the *original constitution*, but as the *accidental effect* of custom, habit, associations of ideas, or other preternatural causes. So that a universal *increasing* of the strength of either might, on the whole, be of little advantage. Universally raising the *public affections*, the desires for *virtue* and *honor*, would make the hero of *Cervantes* <note>A reference to Don Quixote</note>, pining with *hunger* and *poverty*, no rare character. The universal increasing of *selfishness*, unless we had more accurate understandings to discern our *most precise interests*, would fill the world with universal *rapine* and *war*. The consequences of either universally abating or increasing the desires between the sexes, the *love* of *offspring*, or the several
