<meta>This page marks the beginning of Treatise II, titled "Illustrations upon the Moral Sense," shifting from the introductory reflections on human nature in the preface to a formal inquiry into moral philosophy and the definition of terms.</meta>

# TREATISE II.

## ILLUSTRATIONS *upon the* MORAL SENSE.

The differences between actions, by which some are constituted *morally Good* and others *morally Evil*, have always been considered a very important subject of inquiry. Therefore, every attempt to free this subject from the usual causes of error and dispute—the *confusion of ambiguous words*—must be excusable.

<margin>Definitions.</margin>
In the following discourse, *Happiness* denotes a pleasant *sensation* of any kind, or a continued state of such *sensations*; and *Misery* denotes the contrary *sensations*.

Such actions as tend to procure happiness for the agent are called *privately useful*; and such actions as procure misery for the agent are called *privately hurtful*.

<summary>This page introduces an inquiry into the nature of moral good and evil, emphasizing the need for clear terminology. It provides foundational definitions for happiness, misery, and actions that are privately useful or hurtful.</summary>
<keywords>moral sense, moral good, moral evil, definitions, happiness, misery, privately useful, privately hurtful</keywords>

--- Page 236 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory definitions of Treatise II, transitioning from privately beneficial actions to those that affect the public, and setting the stage for an inquiry into the motives and approval of moral actions.</meta>

ACTIONS procuring happiness to others may be called *publicly useful*, and the contrary actions *publicly hurtful*. Some actions may be both *publicly and privately useful*, and others both *publicly and privately hurtful*.

THESE different natural tendencies of actions are universally acknowledged; and in proportion to our *reflection* upon human affairs, we shall enlarge our knowledge of these differences.

<margin>Two questions about morality.</margin>
WHEN these *natural differences* are known, it remains to be inquired into: 1st, “What *quality* in any action determines our *election* of it rather than the contrary?” Or, if the mind determines itself, “What *motives* or *desires* excite an action, rather than the contrary, or rather than its *omission*?” 2ndly, “What *quality* determines our *approbation* of one action, rather than of the contrary action?”

THE words *election* and *approbation* seem to denote simple ideas known by *consciousness*; which can only be explained by *synonymous words*, or by concomitant or consequent circumstances. *Election* is purposing to do an action rather than its contrary, or than being inactive. *Appro-*

<meta>catchword: bation</meta>

<summary>This page defines actions based on their public utility or harm and introduces two fundamental questions of moral inquiry: what drives our choice to act and what determines our approval of an action.</summary>
<keywords>publicly useful, publicly hurtful, reflection, election, motives, desires, omission, approbation, consciousness, morality</keywords>

--- Page 237 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the definitions of moral choice and approval, specifically completing the definition of "approbation" and distinguishing it from "election."</meta>

->MORAL SENSE.<-

...probation of our own *Action* denotes, or is accompanied by a *Pleasure* in the *Contemplation* of it, and in *Reflection* upon the *Affections* which inclined us to it. *Approbation* of the *Action* of another is pleasant, and is accompanied by *Love* toward the *Agent*.

THE *Qualities* moving us to *Election*, or exciting us to *Action*, are different from those moving us to *Approbation*: We often do *Actions* which we do not approve, and approve *Actions* which we omit: We often *desire* that an *Agent* had omitted an *Action* which we approve; and *wish* he would do an *Action* which we condemn. *Approbation* is applied to the *Actions* of others, where there is no room for our *Election*.

Now in our search into the *Qualities* exciting either our *Election* or *Approbation*, let us consider the several *Notions* advanced regarding moral *Good* and *Evil* in both these *Respects*; and what *Senses*, *Instincts*, or *Affections*, must be necessarily assumed to account for our *Approbation* or *Election*.

<margin>The *Epicurean Opinion*.</margin>
THERE are two *Opinions* on this subject entirely opposite: The first is that of the ancient *Epicureans*, as it is beautifully explained in the first book of *Cicero*, *De finibus*;

<header>MORAL SENSE. Sect. I.</header>

### SECT. I.

# Concerning the Character of *Virtue*, Agreeable to *Truth* or *Reason*.

**S**INCE Reason is understood to denote our *power of finding out true propositions*, *reasonableness* must denote the same thing: *conformity to true propositions*, or to *truth*.

REASONABLENESS in an *action* is a very common expression, but yet upon inquiry, it will appear very confused, whether we suppose it the *motive* for *choice*, or the *quality* determining *approval*.

<margin>*Conformity to truth examined.*</margin>
THERE is one sort of *conformity to truth* which determines neither one nor the other; *namely, that conformity which exists between every true proposition and its object.* This sort of conformity can never make us *choose* or *approve* one *action* more than its contrary, for it is found in *all* actions alike: Whatever attribute can be ascribed to a *generous, kind action*, the opposite attribute may be just as truly ascribed to a *selfish, cruel action*: Both propositions are equally *true*, and the two contrary actions, the objects of the two

<margin>No exciting Reasons for ultimate Ends.</margin>
BUT are there not also exciting Reasons, even previous to any end, moving us to propose one end rather than another? To this *Aristotle* long ago answered, “that there are *ultimate Ends* desired without a view to anything else, and *subordinate Ends* or *Objects* desired with a view to something else.” To *subordinate Ends* those *Reasons* or *Truths* excite, which show them to be conducive to the *ultimate End*, and show one *Object* to be more effectual than another: thus *subordinate Ends* may be called *reasonable*. But as to the *ultimate Ends*, to suppose exciting Reasons for them, would infer, that there is no *ultimate End*, but that we desire one thing for another in an infinite Series.

<meta>This page continues the argument that reason alone cannot provide a motive for ultimate ends, focusing on the examples of self-interest (wealth and happiness) and benevolence (public good). It follows the previous page's discussion of Aristotle's distinction between ultimate and subordinate ends. Catchword: WE.</meta>

<header>Illustrations upon the</header>

### Sect. 1. “Reason excites him to desire Wealth”?

---

He will give this reason, that “wealth tends to procure pleasure and ease.” Ask his reason for desiring pleasure or happiness: one cannot imagine what proposition he could assign as his exciting reason. This proposition is indeed true, “there is an instinct or *desire* fixed in his nature, determining him to pursue his happiness;” but it is not this *reflection* on his own nature, or this proposition which excites or determines him, but the *instinct* itself. This is a truth: “Rhubarb strengthens the stomach.” But it is not a *proposition* which strengthens the stomach, but the *quality* in that medicine. The *effect* is not produced by *propositions* showing the *cause*, but by the *cause* itself.

---

<margin>proof</margin>
**In** like manner, what reason can a benevolent being give for exciting him to hazard his life in just war? This perhaps: “such conduct tends to the happiness of his country.” Ask him, “why he serves his country?” he will say, “his country is a very valuable part of mankind.” Why does he study the happiness of mankind? If his *affections* are really *disinterested*, he can give no exciting reasons for it: the happiness of mankind in general, or of any valuable part of it, is an ultimate *end* to that series of desires.

<summary>The author argues that ultimate desires, such as the pursuit of happiness or the welfare of others, are driven by internal instincts rather than logical propositions. He uses the analogy of medicine to show that just as a physical quality heals rather than a statement about it, our instincts move us to action rather than the mere reasoning behind them.</summary>

--- Page 249 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument regarding the motivations behind human desires, specifically addressing whether they stem from abstract concepts of a "greatest good" or from specific, immediate instincts. Catchword: The</meta>

<header>MORAL SENSE.</header>

### Sect. I.

<margin>Men have many ultimate ends.</margin>
We may briefly observe one mistake which many fall into, who in their philosophical inquiries have learned to form very *abstract general ideas*: They suppose, because they have formed some conception of an *infinite good*, or *greatest possible aggregate*, or *sum of happiness*, under which all *particular pleasures* may be included, that there is also some *one great ultimate end* with a view to which every *particular object* is desired; whereas, in truth, each *particular pleasure* is desired without further view, as an ultimate end in the *selfish desires*. It is true, the *prospect* of a greater inconsistent pleasure may surmount or stop this desire; so may the fear of a prepollent <note>superior or prevailing</note> evil. But this does not prove that "all men have formed ideas of *infinite good*, or the *greatest possible aggregate*, or that they have any *instinct* or *desire* without an idea of its object." Just so in the *benevolent affections*, the happiness of any one person is an ultimate end, desired with no further view. And yet, observing its *inconsistency* with the happiness of another more beloved, or with the happiness of many—even if each one of them were but equally beloved—may overcome the former desire. Yet this will not prove that in each kind *action* men do form the abstract conception of *all mankind* or the *system of rationals*.

<summary>The author argues that individual pleasures and the happiness of specific people are sought as ultimate ends in themselves, rather than solely as means to an abstract "greatest possible good." While competing desires may override these ends, this does not imply that humans always act with a universal system of happiness in mind.</summary>
<keywords>abstract general ideas, infinite good, ultimate ends, selfish desires, benevolent affections, system of rationals, moral philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 250 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on how abstract ideas of "universal good" influence human desires, specifically addressing whether our actions for the public good are motivated by pure reason or instinctive affection.</meta>

<header>Illustrations upon the</header>

Sect. 1. Forming such large conceptions is indeed useful, so that we may gratify either our *self-love* or *kind affections* in the fullest manner, as far as our power extends; and may not content ourselves with smaller degrees of either *private* or *public good* while greater ones are in our power. But when we have formed these *conceptions*, we do not serve the *individual* only from love for the *species*, any more than we desire *grapes* with the intention of achieving the *greatest aggregate of happiness*, or from an understanding that they make up a part of the *general sum* of our *happiness*. These conceptions only serve to suggest greater ends than would occur to us without reflection; and by the *prepollency* <note>superior power or influence</note> of one desire toward the greater good, either private or public, to stop the desire toward the *smaller good* when it appears inconsistent with the greater.

<margin>The common reasons examined.</margin>
Let us examine the truths assigned as motivating the pursuit of public good, even by those who, though they allow *disinterested affections* and a *moral sense*, yet suppose something *reasonable* in it beforehand. They assign reasons such as these: "Public good is the end proposed by the DEITY." Then what *reason* excites men to concur with the DEITY? Is it this: "Concurring with the DEITY will make the agent happy"? This is a motivating *reason* in—

The *reasons* which motivate *one nature* may not motivate *another*: The tendency of an *action* toward the happiness of *one agent* may motivate him, but it will not motivate *another agent* to concur, unless there appears a similar tendency toward the happiness of that *other*. They may say, “they are sure the divine *ends* are good.” What does *goodness* mean? Is it *moral* or *natural*? If the divine *ends* are a *natural good*—that is, *pleasant*, or the *cause of pleasure*—to whom is this *pleasure*? either presuppose some *affection*, if they are motivating; or some *moral sense*, if they are justifying. — Is the divine end naturally good to us? This is a motivating *reason*, but it presupposes *self-love*. If we say the divine ends are morally good, we are exactly where we began. What is *moral goodness*? *Conformity to reason.* What are the *reasons, motivating or justifying?*

If anyone alleges as the *reason* motivating us to pursue the public good, this truth,
> "the happiness of a *system*, a *thousand*, or a *million*, is a greater quantity of happiness than that of *one person*: and consequently, if men desire happiness, they must have stronger desires toward the greater *sum* than toward the *lesser*."

This *reason* still presupposes an *instinct* toward *happiness* prior to it. And again: To whom is the happiness of a *system* a greater happiness? To *one individual*, or to the *system*? If to the individual, then his *reason* motivating his desire for a happy *system* presupposes *self-love*. If to the *system*, then what *reason* can motivate one to desire the greater *happiness* of a *system*, or any *happiness* to be in the possession of *others*? Surely none which does not presuppose *public affections.* Without such *affections*, this truth—
> "that a hundred felicities is a greater sum than one felicity,"

More particularly, the motivating *reasons* for a nature which had only *selfish affections* are those truths which showed "what *object* or *event* would provide it the greatest quantity of *pleasure*;" these would motivate the *pursuit* of it. The motivating *truths* about *means* would be only those which pointed out some *means* as more certainly effective than any other, or involving less *pain* or *trouble* for the *agent*. *Public usefulness* of *ends* or *means*, or *public harmfulness*, would neither motivate nor dissuade, except insofar as the *public state* might affect that of the *agent*.

If there is any nature with *public affections*: The truths motivating any *end* of this kind are those that show "that any *event* would promote the happiness of others." That *end* is called most *reasonable* which our *reason* discovers to contain a greater quantity of *public good* than any other in our power.

### Sect. 1. *For what reason do we approve of concurrence with a benefactor?*

Here we must return to a *sense*. Is this the reason moving us to approval: "The study of the *public good* tends to the advantage of the person who approves"? Then the quality moving us to approve an action is its being advantageous to us, and not its *conformity* to a *truth*. This scheme is intelligible, but not true in fact. Men approve without any perception of *private advantage*; and they often do not condemn or disapprove of what is plainly harmful, as in the execution of a *just sentence*, which even the criminal may approve.

<margin>*Obligation presupposes either affections or sense*.</margin>
**There** are some other *reasons* assigned in words differing from the former, but more confused, such as these: “It is our duty to study the public good. We are obliged to do it. We owe obedience to the Deity. The whole is to be preferred to a part.” But let these words *duty*, *obligation*, *owing*, and the meaning of that *gerund* phrase, *is to be preferred*, be explained; and we shall find ourselves still at a loss for *exciting reasons* prior to *affections*, or *justifying reasons* without recourse to a *moral sense*.

<margin>*The meaning of obligation*.</margin>
**When** we say one is obliged to an *action*, we either mean, 1. *That the action is necessary to obtain happiness for the agent, or to avoid misery*; or, 2. *That every spectator, or the agent himself upon reflection, must approve his action and disapprove of his omitting it, if he considers fully all its circumstances*. The former meaning of the word *obligation* presupposes *selfish affections* and the *senses* of *private happiness*; the latter meaning includes the *moral sense*. These *Demonstrations* would be accompanied by that *Pleasure* which is unique to *Truth*, just as much as the *Demonstrations* of the public *Happiness* that results from *Faith*, *Humanity* and *Justice*. There is equal *Truth* on both sides.

<page-num>233</page-num>
<header>MORAL SENSE. Sect. 1.</header>

<margin>How it happens that Virtue is called reasonable and Vice is not.</margin>
WE may briefly observe what has caused the use of the word *reasonable* as an epithet for *only virtuous actions*. Although we have *instincts* directing us to desire *ends* without assuming any previous *reasoning*, yet it is by the use of our *reason* that we find out the *means* of obtaining our *ends*. When we do not use our *reason*, we are often disappointed in our *end*. We therefore call those actions which are *effective* in reaching their ends *reasonable* in one sense of that word.

AGAIN, in all men there is probably a *moral sense*, making publicly useful actions and kind affections agreeable to the agent and to every observer. Most men who have thought about human actions agree that what is *publicly useful* is, on the whole, also *privately useful* to the agent, either in this life or the next. We conclude that all men have the *same affections and senses*. We are convinced by our *reason* that it is by publicly useful actions alone that we can promote *all our ends*. Whoever then acts in a contrary manner, we presume is mistaken, ignorant of, or inattentive to these truths which he might know; and we say he acts *unreasonably*. Hence some have been led to imagine some *reasons*, either exciting or

<meta>catchword: justi-</meta>

<summary>The author explains that virtue is called "reasonable" because reason identifies the most effective means to achieve our instinctive ends, specifically showing that public utility is the best path to private happiness. Consequently, acting viciously is considered "unreasonable" because it suggests a failure to recognize these truths.</summary>
<keywords>moral sense, reason, instincts, virtuous actions, public utility, private utility, means and ends</keywords>

### Sect. 1. [On] justifying [moral goodness] prior to all *Affections* or a *moral Sense*.
---

<margin>Objections based on the fact that we judge even our *Affections* and *Senses* themselves.</margin>
Two arguments are brought in defense of this term, as being prior to any *Sense*: namely, "That we judge even our *Affections* and *Senses* themselves, whether they are morally *Good* or *Evil*."

The second argument is that "if all *moral Ideas* depend upon the *Constitution* of our *Sense*, then all *Constitutions* would have been equally reasonable and good to the **DEITY**, which is *absurd*."

<margin>1. That we judge our *Senses* themselves.</margin>
As to the first argument, it is plain that we judge our own *Affections*, or those of *others*, by our *moral Sense*, by which we approve of kind *Affections* and disapprove of the *contrary*. But no one can apply *moral Attributes* to the very *Faculty* of perceiving *moral Qualities*; or call his *moral Sense* morally *Good* or *Evil*, any more than he calls the *Power* of *Tasting* "sweet" or "bitter," or the power of *Seeing* "straight" or "crooked," "white" or "black."

<margin>*Answered.*</margin>
**EVERY** person judges the *Affections* of others by his own *Sense*; so that it seems not impossible that in these *Senses*, *Men* might differ just as they do in *Taste*. A *Sense* approving *Benevolence* would disapprove *that*

He counters that while we judge affections using our moral sense, the faculty of the sense itself cannot be labeled morally good or evil any more than the sense of taste can be called sweet.</summary>

that *temperament* which a *sense* that approves of *malice* would delight in. The *former* person would judge the *latter* by his own *sense*, just as the *latter* would judge the *former*. Each one, at first glance, would think the *sense* of the other was perverted. But then, is there no difference? Are both *senses* equally good? Yes, he might; but not because of any *perception* of *moral goodness* in one *sense* more than the other. Any rational *being* observing two *men* formed in this way, with opposite *senses*, might through reasoning see—not *moral goodness* in one *sense* more than in its opposite—but a *tendency* toward the *happiness* of the *person* himself who had the *former sense* in that one *constitution*, and an opposite *tendency* in the *opposite constitution*. Indeed, the *people* themselves might observe this, since the *former sense* would make those *actions* pleasing to the *agent* which were useful to others; those others, if they had a similar *sense*, would love him and return his *kind services*. If this *observer* had a *moral sense*, he would think that *constitution* which was contrary to *his own* was *strange* and *surprising*, or *unnatural*. If the *observer* had no *affections* toward others and were separated from mankind so as to have neither *hopes* nor *fears* from their *actions*, he would be indifferent about their *constitutions* and have no *desire* or *preference* for one over another, though he might see which were *advantageous* to them and which were pernicious.

<margin>The second objection—that all constitutions would have been equally reasonable—answered.</margin>

As to the second argument, what is meant by [ *alike reasonable or good to the* DEITY*?* ] Does it mean "that the DEITY could have had no *reasons* exciting him to make one constitution rather than another?" It is plain that if the DEITY had nothing *essential to his nature* corresponding to our *sweetest* and *most kind affections*, we can scarcely suppose he could have any reason exciting him to anything he has done. But grant such a *disposition* in the DEITY, and then the manifest tendency of the present constitution toward the *happiness* of his creatures was an exciting reason for choosing it before the contrary. Each sort of constitution might have given men an equal immediate *pleasure* in present *self-approbation* for any sort of action; but the actions approved by the present sense procure all the *pleasures* of the other senses; and the actions which would have been approved by a contrary *moral sense* would have been productive of all the *torments* of the other senses.

## Concerning that Character of Virtue and Vice, the Fitness or Unfitness of Actions.

We come next to examine some other explanations of morality, which have been much insisted on lately. <note>* See Dr. Samuel Clarke’s Boyle’s Lectures; and many recent authors.</note> We are told, “that there are eternal and immutable differences of things, absolutely and antecedently: that there are also eternal and unalterable relations in the natures of the things themselves, from which arise *agreements* and *disagreements*, *congruities* and *incongruities*, *fitness* and *unfitness* of the *application* of *circumstances*, to the *qualifications* of *persons*; that *actions agreeable to these relations are morally good, and that the contrary actions are morally evil*.” These expressions are sometimes given the same meaning as those more common ones: “*acting agreeably to the eternal reason and truth*

<margin>Three sorts of relations considered.</margin>
To show particularly how far *morality* can be concerned with *relations*, we may consider them under these three classes: 1. The *relations* of inanimate *objects*, regarding their *quantity* or *active* and *passive powers*, as explained by Mr. *Locke*. 2. The *relations* of inanimate *objects* to rational *agents*, regarding their *active* or *passive powers*. 3. The *relations* of rational *agents* among themselves, founded on their *powers* or *actions*, past or continued. Now let us examine what *fitnesses* or *unfitnesses* arise from any of these sorts of *relations* in which the *morality* of *actions* may consist, and whether we can place *morality* in them without presupposing a *moral sense*. It is plain that the ingenious author says nothing against the supposition of a *moral sense*; but many imagine that his account of moral *ideas* is independent of a *moral sense*, and therefore are less willing to allow that we have such an immediate *perception* or *sense* of *virtue* and *vice*. What follows is not intended to oppose his scheme, but rather to suggest what seems a necessary explanation of it, by showing that it is not otherwise in-

Section 2. the other. Thus *compassion* is *fit* to make others *happy*, and *unfit* to make others *miserable*. *Violation of property* is *fit* to make men *miserable*, and *unfit* to make them *happy*. Each of these is both *fit* and *unfit* with respect to different ends. The mere *fitness*, then, to an end is not the idea of *moral goodness*.

PERHAPS the virtuous *fitness* is that of ends. The *fitness* of a *subordinate* end to the *ultimate* one cannot make the *action* good unless the *ultimate* end is good. To keep a *conspiracy* secret is not a good end, though it is *fit* for obtaining a further end: the *success* of the *conspiracy*. The *moral fitness* must be that of the *ultimate* end itself: the *public good* alone is a *fit* end; therefore, only the *means* that are *fit* for this end are good.

WHAT does the *fitness* of an *ultimate* end mean? For what is it *fit*? Well, it is an *ultimate* end—not *fit* for anything further, but *absolutely fit*. What does that word *fit* mean? If it signifies a *simple idea*, it must be the *perception* of *some sense*; thus, even in this framework, we must return to a *moral sense*.

<header>M O R A L S E N S E.</header>

<margin>Section 2.</margin>
“*Action, or End, to the Relations of Agents.*” But what does *Agreement* mean? Which of these four meanings does it have? 1. We say one *quantity* agrees with another of equal *dimensions* in every way. 2. A *corollary* agrees with a *theorem* when our knowing the latter to be truth leads us to know that the former is also a true *proposition*. 3. *Food* agrees with that *body* which it tends to *preserve*. 4. *Food* agrees with the *taste* of that being in whom it raises a *pleasant perception*. If any one of these is the meaning of *agreement* in the definition, then one of these is the idea of *fitness*. 1. That an *action* or *affection* is of the same *bulk* and *figure* as the *relation*. Or, 2. When the *relation* is a true *proposition*, so is the *action* or *affection*. Or, 3. The *action* or *affection* tends to *preserve* the *relation*, and contrary *actions* would destroy it: so that, for instance, God would no longer be related to us as *creator* and *benefactor* if we disobeyed him. Or, 4. The *action* raises *pleasant perceptions* in the *relation*. All these expressions seem absurd.

THESE gentlemen probably have some other meanings for these words *fitness* or *agreement*. I hope what has been said will show the need for an *explanation* of them, even though they are so common. There is one meaning

<meta>catchword: ing</meta>

<summary>Hutcheson analyzes four possible meanings of "agreement" to show that none of them logically explain "moral fitness" without resulting in absurdity. He argues that those who define morality through "fitness" fail to provide a clear explanation of their terms.</summary>

### SECT. III.

## Mr. Woolaston’s Significance of Truth, as the *Idea* of Virtue considered.

**MR. WOOLASTON** *has introduced a new explanation of* moral virtue, *namely, the significance of truth in actions, supposing that in every action there is some* significance, *like that which* moralists and civilians *speak of in their* tacit conventions, *and quasi-contracts!*

<margin>Signification: wherein it consists.</margin>
<margin><unclear>Cwm</unclear></margin>
The word *signification* is very common, but a little reflection will show it to be very ambiguous. In the *signification* of *words* these things are included: 1. An *association of an idea with a sound*, so that when any *idea* is formed by the speaker, the *idea* of a *sound* accompanies it. 2. The *sound* perceived by the hearer excites the *idea* to which it is connected. 3. In like manner a *judgment* in the speaker’s mind is accompanied with the *idea* of a *combination of sounds*. 4. ### Sect. 3. *Sounds heard raise the* apprehension *of that* judgment *in the* mind *of the* hearer. *Nothing further than these* circumstances *seems to be denoted by* signification.

<margin>Conclusions drawn from speech.</margin>
Hearing a proposition does not of itself produce either *assent* or *dissent*, or *opinion* in the hearer, but only presents to his apprehension the *judgment*, or *thema complexum*. But the hearer himself often forms *judgments* or *opinions* upon this occasion, either immediately without reasoning, or by some short *argument*. These *opinions* are one or more of the following *propositions*. 1. *That a sound is perceived, and a judgment apprehended.* 2. *Such a person caused the sound heard.* 3. *The speaker intended to excite in the hearer the idea of the sound, and the apprehension of the judgment, or thema complexum.* This judgment is not always formed by the hearer, nor is it always true, when men are heard speaking. 4. *The speaker intended to produce assent in the hearer:* This judgment is not always true. 5. *The speaker assents to the proposition spoken:* This judgment in the hearer is often false, and is formed upon the opinion of the speaker's *veracity*, or speaking what expresses his opinion usually. 6. *The speaker does not assent to the proposition spoken:* This judgment of the hearer is often false,

<summary>Hutcheson explains that hearing a statement does not automatically cause belief or disagreement, but rather presents a judgment for the mind to consider. He lists several secondary judgments a hearer might form regarding the speaker's intentions and honesty.</summary>
<keywords>signification, judgment, assent, dissent, speaker intention, veracity, thema complexum, apprehension</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of how morality in speech is determined, shifting focus from the logical significance of words to the hearer's role in forming judgments and the speaker's underlying intentions.</meta>

->Illustrations upon the<-

### Sect. 3.

<margin>Nor in conclusions formed by hearers.</margin>
As to the *opinions* formed by the *hearer*, they are entirely his own *action*, as much as any other *conclusion* or *judgment* formed from *appearances* of any sort whatsoever. They are true or false according to the sagacity of the *observer* or his *caution*. The hearer may form perfectly true *opinions* or *judgments* when the speaker is guilty of the basest fraud; and he may form false *judgments* when the speaker is perfectly innocent and spoke nothing false in any sense.

The *evils* which may follow from the false judgments of the hearer are no more attributable to the *speaker* than the evil consequences of another’s *action* of any kind may be attributed to any person who cooperated; or if, by his *action* or *omission*, the consequence of which he might have foreseen, he either actually intended this evil or *lacked* that *degree* of *kind affection* which would have inclined him to prevent it.

<margin>The morality of speech in the intention.</margin>
The *intention* of the speaker is what all *moralists* have previously thought the *virtue* or *vice* of *words* depended upon, and not the bare *significance* of *truth* or *falsehood*. This *intention* is either: 1. *To lead the hearer into a true or false opinion about the sentiments of the speaker.*

<meta>catchword: 2. To</meta>

<summary>Hutcheson argues that a speaker is not responsible for a hearer's false conclusions unless there was an intent to deceive or a lack of benevolence. He concludes that the morality of speech is rooted in the speaker's intention rather than the literal truth of the statement.</summary>
<keywords>ethics of speech, hearer's judgment, speaker's intention, moral responsibility, truth and falsehood, kind affection, moral philosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the categorization of the speaker's intentions regarding speech, specifically focusing on the speaker's goal to influence the hearer's beliefs or to achieve broader public consequences.</meta>

2. *To make the hearer assent to the proposition spoken.* Or, 3. *Both to make the hearer assent to the proposition, and judge that the speaker also assents to it.* Or, 4. *To accomplish some end, by means of the hearer’s assent to the proposition spoken. This end may be known by the speaker to be either publicly useful, or publicly hurtful.*

<margin>Sect. 3.</margin>

Some moralists <note>Barbeyrac’s Notes on Pufendorf, *Lib.* iv. *c.* 1, 7.</note> of late have placed all *virtue* in speech in the intention of the last kind, namely: “Accomplishing some publicly useful end, by speaking either *logical truth* or *falsehood*; and that all *vice* in speaking consists in intending to effect something *publicly hurtful* by speech, whether *logically true* or *false*, and known to be such; or by using speech in a manner which we may foresee would be *publicly hurtful*, whether we actually intend this evil consequence or not.” Some stricter moralists assert that “the public evils which would ensue from destroying mutual confidence, by allowing the speaking of *propositions* known to be *false* on any occasion, are so great, that no particular *advantage* to be expected from speaking known *logical falsehoods* can ever overbalance

<margin>
The significance of actions.
</margin>
The significance of some *actions* depends upon a similar *association of ideas* with them, made either by *nature*, or *arbitrarily* and by *custom*, as with *sounds*. *Letters* are, by *custom*, the *signs* of *sounds*. A *shriek* or *groan* is a *natural sign* of *fear* or *pain*: A *motion* of the *hand* or *head* may signify *assent*, *dissent*, or *desire*. The cutting down of tall *poppies* was an answer: The sending of *spurs* was advice to *flee*: *Kindling* many *fires* raises the *opinion* of an *encampment*: *Raising* a *smoke* will raise the *opinion* of a *fire*.

Sect. 3. "...infers nothing from it, except upon having reason to believe that the causer of the *appearance* intended to convey some *sentiment* to the *observer*." 3. Other signs are used when "the *signifier* gives no reason to conclude any other intention, but only to raise an *apprehension* of the *judgment*, or the *thema complexum* <note>a complex proposition or subject</note>, without professing any design to communicate his *sentiments*, or to produce any *assent* in the *observer*."

To perform actions from which the observer will form *false opinions*, without having reason to imagine an intention in the agent, is never in itself considered evil, whether the signs are natural or instituted; provided there is no malicious intention or neglect of the public good. It is never called a crime in a *teacher* to pronounce an *absurd sentence* as an example; in a *nobleman* to travel without *coronets*; or a *clergyman* in *lay clothing* for private convenience, or to avoid troublesome ceremony; or to leave lights in a lodge to make people conclude there is a *watch* kept. This significance may be in any action which is observed; but as true conclusions prove no virtue in the agent, so *false ones prove no vice*.

--- Page 293 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of William Wollaston's theory of morality, focusing on the problems of defining virtue based on the "significance of truth" conveyed by an action to an observer.</meta>

<margin>Section 3.</margin>

If *virtue* consisted in this *first* sort of *significance of truth*, it would depend not upon the *agent* but upon the *insight* of the *observer*. The acute *penetration* of one would constitute an *action virtuous*, and the rashness or stupidity of another would make it *vicious*. Furthermore, the most barbarous *actions* would raise no *false opinion* regarding the *sentiments* of the *agent* in a judicious *observer*.

The second sort of *significance* would also make *virtue* consist in the *power of observers*. A *precise reasoner* would receive no *false opinion* from the worst *action* concerning the *object* or the *relation* of the *agent* to it; and a *false opinion* might be formed by a weak *observer* from a *perfectly good action*. An *observer* who knew an *agent* to have the *basest temperament* would not, from his worst *action*, conclude anything *false* concerning the *object*. Indeed, all such *false opinions* would arise only upon the *supposition* that the *agent was virtuous*.

Sect. 3. Malice, in this instance, was more intense than regard for his *Interest*. A beneficent *Action* would prove only one of these two: either that his *Opinions* of the *Constitution* were true; or that, if he was mistaken about the *Constitution*, he also had a *false Opinion* of the natural *Tendency* of the *Action*. SUPPOSE “a *Constitution* wherein a *private Interest* could be advanced in *Opposition* to the *public*” (this we may call an evil *Constitution*): Suppose only *Self-Love* in the *Agent*; then a *publicly useful Action*, any way toilsome or expensive to the *Agent*, would evidence *false Opinions*. And the most cruel, selfish *Actions* would evidence *true Opinions*.

IN an evil *Constitution*, suppose kind *Affections* in the *Agent*; a *publicly useful Action* would not certainly argue either *true* or *false Opinions*. If his *Opinions* were *true*, but kind *Affections* were stronger than *Self-Love*, he might act in the same manner as if his *Opinions* were *false* and *Self-Love* were the reigning *Affection*.

<margin>To what extent it is a characteristic of virtue that it flows from true opinions.</margin>
IN our present *constitution*, it is probable that no person would ever do anything publicly harmful except based on some false *opinion*. Flowing from true *opinions* is indeed a reasonable *characteristic* or *property* of *virtue*, and flowing from some false *opinion* is a reasonable *characteristic* of *vice*; although neither is strictly universal. But, 1. This is not the proper *significance*. A judicious observer never imagines any intention to communicate *opinions* in some of the most important *actions*, whether good or evil. 2. If an *action* signified *falsehood*, it is generally only *logical*. 3. The false *opinion* in the *agent* is not the *quality* for which the evil *action* is condemned; nor is the true

<summary>Hutcheson explains that while virtuous actions generally align with true opinions in our current world, this is due to our nature and social structure rather than an inherent logical link. He argues that the moral quality of an action is not determined by the truth or falsehood of the agent's beliefs.</summary>

And *false opinions* generally lessen *crimes*, unless the very *ignorance* or *error* has flowed from an *evil disposition*, or a total *absence* of good.

It is surprising, for instance, how anyone should place the *evil* of *ingratitude* in denying that the *person* injured was a *benefactor*. The *observer* of such an *action*, if he supposed the *agent* really held that *false opinion*, would think the *crime* was lesser for it. But if he were convinced that the *agent* held a *true opinion*, he would think his *ingratitude* more odious. Where we most abhor *actions*, we often assume *true opinions*; and we sometimes admire *actions* flowing even from *false opinions*, when they have evidenced no lack of good *disposition*.

To write a critique of a book as well-designed as Mr. *Wollaston’s*, and so full of very good *reasoning* upon the most useful subjects, would not show much good nature. **One** may see that he had some other *idea* of moral *good* prior to this *significance of truth*, by his introducing, in the very explanation of it, words presupposing the *ideas* of morality already known: such as [*right*], [*obligation*], [*lie*], and [*his*] denoting [*property*].

<margin>The significance of truth is equal in unequal virtue.</margin>
Mr. *Wollaston* acknowledges that there may be very little evil in some actions signifying falsehood, such as throwing away that which is of but little *use* or *value*. It is objected to him that there is equal contrariety to truth in such actions as there is in the greatest *villainy*; he, in answer to it, really gives up his whole cause unawares. He must admit that there may be the strictest *truth* and *certainty* about trifles; so there may be the most obvious *falsehood* signified by trifling actions. If then the *significance of falsehood* is the very same thing as moral *evil*, all crimes must be equal. He answers that crimes increase according to the *importance* of the truth denied; and so *virtue* increases as the *importance* of the truths affirmed.
Then

<summary>Hutcheson argues that Wollaston's theory of "significance of truth" relies on pre-existing moral concepts and fails to distinguish between the severity of different crimes. He notes that if morality were based solely on the truth or falsehood an action signifies, then trivial lies would be as evil as great villainies.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the critique of William Wollaston's theory, specifically arguing that the moral value of an action depends on the underlying affections rather than the truth or falsehood the action signifies.</meta>

### Sect. 3.
---

*Virtue* and *vice* increase according to the *importance of propositions* affirmed or denied; but the *significance of truth and falsehood* does not increase in this same way. Therefore, *virtue* and *vice* are not the same as the *significance of truth* or *falsehood*.

But what is this *importance of truth?* It is nothing other than the *moment* <note>The weight, importance, or consequence.</note> or *quantity* of good or evil, either private or public, which would be produced by actions concerning which these true *judgments* are made. But it is plain that the *significance* of truth or falsehood is not changed by this *importance*; therefore, *virtue* or *vice* denote something different from this *significance*.

Furthermore, the *importance* of actions toward the public good or evil is not the *idea of virtue or vice*. Nor does the former prove *virtue* in an *action* any further than it shows *kind affections*; nor does the latter prove *vice* any further than it shows either *malice* or a *lack of kind affections*. Otherwise, a casual *invention* or an *action* performed entirely for the sake of private *interest* might be as virtuous as the most kind and generous *deeds*. And *chance-medley* <note>A legal term referring to a sudden, unintended fight or accidental killing.</note>, or kindly intended but unsuccessful *attempts*, would

<summary>Hutcheson argues that virtue and vice are distinct from the "significance of truth," as the moral weight of an action is determined by its impact and the actor's intentions rather than logical truth. He maintains that without considering "kind affections," purely self-interested or accidental actions could mistakenly be labeled virtuous.</summary>
<keywords>virtue, vice, significance of truth, falsehood, importance of truth, judgments, kind affections, malice, public good, chance-medley</keywords>

<margin>Some ambiguities in Mr. Wollaston.</margin>
**One** of Mr. *Wollaston’s illustrations*—that the *significance of falsehood* is the *idea* of *moral evil*—ends in this: "It is *acting* *a lie*." What then? Should he not first have shown what *moral evil* was, and that every *lie* was such?

**Another** illustration or proof is that "it is acting contrary to that reason which **God** has given us as the guide of our actions." Does this not place the original idea of *moral evil* in *counteracting the* **Deity**, and not in *signifying falsehood*? But, he may say, "*Counteracting the* **Deity** denies him to be our benefactor, and signifies falsehood." Then why is *signifying falsehood* evil? Why, it is *counteracting the* **Deity**, who gave us reason for our guide. Why is this evil again? It denies the truth that "he is our benefactor."

**Another** illustration is this: "That signifying falsehood is altering the natures of things, and making them be what they are not, or desiring at least to make them be what they are not." If by "altering the natures" is meant *destroying beings*, then *moral evil* consists in *desiring the destruction of other natures*,

<summary>Hutcheson critiques William Wollaston's theory that moral evil is defined as "acting a lie," arguing that Wollaston's reasoning is circular and ultimately relies on the counteracting of God's will rather than the falsehood itself. He questions whether Wollaston has sufficiently proven that all lies are inherently evil without first defining moral evil independently.</summary>
<keywords>William Wollaston, moral evil, falsehood, Deity, reason, benefactor, circular reasoning, nature of things</keywords>

MANY of Mr. *Wollaston's* propositions contradicted by actions are about *rights*, *duties*, *obligation*, *justice*, and *reasonableness*. These are long words, the principal *names* or *attributes* in sentences. The little word [*his*] or the particles [*as* or *according*] are much better: they may escape observation and yet may include all the ambiguities of *right*, *property*, *agreement*, and *reasonableness*: "*treating things as they are, and not as they are not*." "*Treating things as they are, and not as they are not*," or "*according to what they are, or are not*," are expressions he probably learned from another truly great name <note>Likely referring to Samuel Clarke.</note> who has not explained them sufficiently.

<margin>*In quasi-contracts or tacit ones, there is no expression of truth.*</margin>
IT may perhaps not seem improper on this occasion to observe that in *Quasi Contracts* <note>A legal obligation that is imposed by law, regardless of the consent of the parties.</note>, the *civil lawyers* <note>Practitioners of civil law, often based on Roman law.</note> do not imagine any act of the mind of the *person* obligated to be really signified. IN *tacit conventions*, it is not a judgment which is signified, but an *act* of the will transferring a right, in which <margin>Sect. 3.</margin> there is no *relation* to *truth* or *falsehood* in itself. The *non-performance of covenants* is made punishable, not because they signify *falsehoods*, as if this were the crime in them; but it is necessary, in order to preserve *commerce* in any society, to make effective all *declarations of consent* to transfer *rights* by any usual *signs*. Otherwise, there could be no *certainty* in men's transactions.

He explains that the obligation to keep contracts arises from the social necessity of certain commerce, not from the inherent truth or falsehood of the agreement.</summary>

# SECT. IV.

### Showing the *Use of Reason* concerning *Virtue* and *Vice*, upon the *Supposition* that we receive these *Ideas* by a *Moral Sense*.

---

<margin>*Truths about Morals, four sorts.*</margin>
If those who insist so much upon the *antecedent reasonableness of virtue* had told us distinctly what is *reasonable* or *provable* concerning it, many of our debates would have been prevented. Let us consider what *truths* concerning *actions* men could desire to know, or prove by *reason*. I imagine they may be reduced to these headings. 1. “To know whether there are not some *actions* or *affections* which obtain the *approbation* of any *spectator* or *observer*, and others provoke his *dislike* and *condemnation*?” Just as every man can answer this question for himself, so *universal experience* and *history* show that this is the case in all nations; consequently, the *moral sense* is *universal*. 2. “Whether there is any particular *quality* which, wherever it is perceived, gains *approbation*, and the contrary raises *disapprobation*?” We shall

<meta>catchword: find</meta>

<summary>Hutcheson begins exploring the role of reason in morality, arguing that if virtue's "reasonableness" were clearly defined, many debates would end. He identifies the first two major questions of moral inquiry: whether certain actions universally trigger approval or dislike, and what specific qualities elicit these reactions.</summary>
<keywords>moral sense, reason, virtue, vice, approbation, universal experience, moral truths, moral philosophy</keywords>

Sect. 4. find this *Quality* to be *kind Affection*, or *concern for the Good of others*; and thus the *moral Senses* of *Men* are generally *uniform*. About these two *Questions* there is little *reasoning*; we know how to answer them from reflecting on our own *Sentiments*, or by consulting others. 3. “What Actions do really evidence *kind Affections*, or do really tend to the *greatest public Good?*” About this *Question* is all the *specific Reasoning* of those who treat of the particular *Laws of Nature*, or even of *Civil Laws*: This is the largest *Field*, and the most useful *Subject* of *Reasoning*, which remains upon every *Scheme* of *Morals*. 4. “What are the *Motives* which, even from *Self-Love*, would motivate each Individual to do those *Actions* which are publicly *useful?*” It is probable indeed, no *Man* would approve as *virtuous* an *Action publicly useful*, to which the *Agent* was motivated only by *Self-Love*, without any *kind Affection*: It is also probable that no view of *Interest* can raise that *kind Affection*, which we approve as *virtuous*; nor can any *Reasoning* do it, except that which shows some *moral Goodness*, or *kind Affections* in the *Object*; for this never fails, where it is observed or supposed in any *Person*, to raise the *Love* of the *Observer*; so that *Virtue* is not properly *taught*.

<summary>Hutcheson identifies "kind affection" as the quality that triggers universal moral approval and defines the primary role of reason as determining which actions best serve the public good. He argues that true virtue cannot be produced by self-interest or logical instruction alone, but arises naturally when we observe goodness in others.</summary>

### Sect. 4.

**YET** since all men naturally have *self-love* as well as *kind affections*, the former may often counteract the latter, or the latter the former; in each case the agent is uneasy, and in some degree unhappy. The first hasty *views* of human affairs often represent *private interest* as opposite to the *public interest*: When this is perceived, *self-love* may often engage men in publicly harmful *actions*, which their *moral sense* will condemn; and this is the ordinary cause of vice. To represent these motives of *self-interest*, to engage men in publicly useful *actions*, is certainly the most necessary point in morals. This has been so well done by the ancient *moralists*, by Dr. *Cumberland*, *Puffendorf*, *Grotius*, and *Shaftesbury*; it is made so certain from the *divine government* of the world, the *state of mankind*, who cannot subsist without society, from universal experience and consent, from inward *consciousness* of the *pleasure* of *kind affections*, and *self-approbation*, and of the *torments* of *malice*, or *hatred*, or *envy*, or *anger*; that no man who considers these things can ever imagine he can have any possible *interest* in opposing the public *good*; or in checking or restraining his *kind affections*; nay, if he had no *kind affections*, his very *self-love* and regard to his private *good* might excite him

<margin>How our Moral Sense is corrected by Reason.</margin>
If the question means, “How are we sure that what we approve, all others shall also approve?” We can be sure of this under no scheme; but it is highly probable that the *senses* of all men are fairly *uniform*: That the DEITY also approves of *kind affections*; otherwise he would not have implanted them in us, nor determined us by a *moral sense* to approve of them. Now, since the *probability* that men shall judge truly, setting aside any presupposed prejudice, is greater than that they shall judge falsely; it is more probable, when our actions are really *kind* and *publicly useful*, that all observers shall judge truly of our intentions and of the *tendency* of our actions, and consequently approve of what we approve ourselves, than that they shall judge falsely and condemn them.

If the meaning of the question is, “Will doing what our *moral sense* approves tend toward our *happiness*, and toward avoiding *misery*?” It is in this way that we call a *taste wrong*—when it makes food pleasant at present which shall cause future *pains* or *death*. This ques-

<meta>catchword: tion</meta>

<summary>Hutcheson argues that our moral sense is likely uniform across humanity and aligned with the Deity's approval of kind affections. He explains that actions that are truly kind and useful are more likely to be judged correctly by others, and that a "wrong" moral taste is one that leads to future misery despite immediate approval.</summary>
<keywords>moral sense, reason, Deity, kind affections, uniformity of senses, probability, prejudice, happiness, misery, moral judgment</keywords>

**THUS**, there seems to be no part of that *reasoning* which was ever used by *moralists* that is superseded by supposing a *moral sense*. And yet, without a *moral sense*, no *explanation* can be given of our *ideas* of *morality*, nor of that *reasonableness* supposed to be antecedent to all *instincts*, *affections*, or *senses*.

> “ **BUT** may there not be a *right* or

So, may not our *moral sense* approve that which is *vicious* and *disapprove of virtue*, just as a *sickly palate* may dislike *pleasant food*, or an *impaired sight* misrepresent *colors* or *dimensions*? Must we not know, therefore, beforehand what is *morally good* or *evil* by our *reason*, before we can know that our *moral sense* is right?

Section 4. *Colors*, *Sounds*, *Tastes*, *Smells*, *Pleasure*, *Pain*.

---

Other *ideas* are *images* of something external, such as *duration*, *number*, *extension*, *motion*, and *rest*. These latter, for the sake of distinction, we may call *concomitant ideas of sensation*, and the former *purely sensible*. As to the *purely sensible ideas*, we know they are altered by any *disorder* in our *organs*, and made different from those that arise in us from the same *objects* at other times. We do not name *objects* based on our *perceptions* during the *disorder*, but according to our *ordinary perceptions*, or those of others in *good health*. Yet nobody imagines that, because of this, *colors*, *sounds*, and *tastes* are not *sensible ideas*. In the same way, many *circumstances* diversify the *concomitant ideas*; but we name *objects* from the *appearances* they make to us in a uniform *medium*, when our *organs* are in *no disorder*, and the *object* is not *very distant* from them. But none, therefore, imagines that it is *reason* and not *sense* which discovers these *concomitant ideas*, or *primary qualities*.

The *idea* of the external *motion*, known first by *sense*, and its *tendency* to the *happiness* or *misery* of some sensitive *nature*, often inferred by *argument* or *reason*. 2. *Apprehension* or *opinion* of the *affections*

Our Reason often corrects the report of our *senses* regarding the *natural tendency* of the *external action*, and corrects rash conclusions about the *affections* of the *agent*. But whether our *moral sense* is subject to such a disorder that it might have *different perceptions* from the same perceived *affections* in an *agent* at different times—just as the *eye* might perceive the *colors* of an unchanging *object* differently—it is not easy to determine. Perhaps it would be difficult to find any instances of such a change. What *Reason* could correct, if the sense fell into such a disorder, I do not know; except by suggesting to its *remembrance* its former *approbations* and representing the *general sense* of mankind.

<margin>Sect. 4.</margin>kind. But this does not prove *ideas* of *virtue* and *vice* to be prior to a *sense*, any more than a similar *correction* of the *ideas* of *color* in a *person* with *jaundice* proves that *colors* are perceived by *reason* prior to *sense*.

If anyone says, “this *moral sense* is not a *rule*,” what does that word mean? It is not a *straight, rigid body*; it is not a *general proposition* showing what *means* are fit to obtain an *end*; it is not a *proposition* asserting that a *superior* will make those happy who act one way, and miserable who act the contrary way. If these are the *meanings* of *rule*, it is no *rule*; yet by reflecting upon it, our *understanding* may find out a *rule*. But what *rule* of *actions* can be formed without *relation* to some *end* proposed? Or what *end* can be proposed without presupposing *instincts*, *desires*, *affections*, or a *moral sense*? This will not be easy to explain.

should a person by mere choice stab a man, without knowing him to be a *public robber*; here both free choice and public usefulness may concur: Yet will anyone say there is *merit* or *virtue* in such actions? Where then shall we find merit, unless in kind *affections*, or the *desire* and *intention* of the public good? This moves our *approbation* wherever we observe it: and the lack of this is the true reason why a searcher for *mines*, a free killer of an unknown robber, the *warming sun*, or the *fruitful tree*, are not counted *meritorious*.

But it may be said, that to make an action *meritorious*, it is necessary not only that the action be *publicly useful*, but that it be *known* or *imagined* to be such, before the agent freely chooses it. But what does this add to the former scheme? Only a *judgment* or *opinion* in the *understanding*, concerning the *natural tendency* of an action to the public good: Few, it may be presumed, will place *virtue* in *assent* or *dissent*, or *perceptions*. And yet this is all that is added to the former case. The agent must not desire the public good, or have any kind *affections*. This would spoil the *freedom of choice*, according to the scheme of those who insist on a *freedom opposite to affections*.

WHAT has probably led many into this way of speaking—that virtue is the *effect* of rational choice and not of *instincts* or *affections*—is this: they find that "some actions flowing from particular kind affections are sometimes condemned as *evil*" because of their *bad influence* upon the state of larger societies, and that the *rush* and *confused sensations* of any of our passions may divert the mind from considering the *whole effect* of its actions. They require, therefore, a *calm and undisturbed temper* for *virtue*.

THERE is indeed some reason to recommend this *temper* as very necessary in many cases, and yet some of the most *passionate actions* may be perfectly good. This was shown above in the first *section*.

->MORAL SENSE.<-

<margin>
Section 5.
</margin>

**IF** meritorious actions are those which anyone who does not approve of them is himself condemned by others, the quality by which they are constituted *meritorious* in this sense is the same quality that moves our approval. We condemn any person who does not approve of what we ourselves approve. We presume the sense of others is formed like our own, and that any other person, if he would attend to the actions which we approve, would also *approve* of them and love the agent. When we find that another does not *approve* of what we approve, we are apt to conclude that he has not had *kind affections* toward the agent, or that *some evil affection* makes him overlook his virtues, and on this account we condemn him.

Perhaps by *meritorious* is meant the same thing as another word used in a similar way, *namely, rewardable*. Then indeed the *quality* on which *merit* or *rewardableness* is founded is different from that which is denoted by *merit* in the former meanings.

**REWARDABLE**, or *deserving reward*, denotes either that *quality* which would incline a *superior nature* to make an agent *happy*; or, secondly, that *quality* of actions which would make a spectator *ap-*

Let anyone try to give a meaning to the word *rewardable* that is distinct from these, and not satisfy himself with the words *worthy of* or *deserving*, which have very complex and ambiguous meanings.

Now, the *qualities* of an action that determine a powerful nature to reward it must vary according to the *constitution* and *affections* of that Superior. If he has a *moral sense*, or *something analogous* of a more excellent sort, by which he is determined to *love* those who evidence *kind affections* and to desire their happiness, then *kind affection* is a *quality* moving him to reward.

Furthermore, if this Superior is *benevolent* and observes that inferior natures can, by their mutual actions, promote their mutual happiness, then he must be inclined to encourage them to *publicly useful actions* by offering *prospects of private interest* to the agent, if it is necessary. Therefore, he will engage them to perform publicly useful actions by the *prospects of rewards*, whatever the internal principle of their actions may be, or whatever their affections are. *Affections* and public *usefulness* concurring undoubtedly incline the benevolent *Superior* to confer *happiness*. But the want of kind *affections*, although there be publicly *useful actions*, may be so offensive to the *moral sense* of the *superior nature* as to prevent *reward* or excite him to *punish*, unless this *conduct* would occasion greater public *evil* by withdrawing from many *agents* a necessary *motive* to public *usefulness*—namely, the *hope* of *reward*.

But if the Superior were *malicious* with a *moral sense* contrary to ours, the contrary *affections* and *tendency* of *actions* would excite him to reward, if any such thing could be expected from such a *temper*.

### Sect. 5. Rewards for publicly useful *Actions*, from whatever *Affections* they proceed, will evidence *Benevolence* in the *Superior* to do so. And this is the *Case* with human *Governors*, who cannot dive into the *Affections* of *Men*.

<margin>
Whether motives or inclinations toward evil are necessary to make an agent worthy of reward?
</margin>
Some strongly assert (which is often the only proof provided) that "to make an *Action* worthy of reward, the *Agent* should have had *Inclinations* toward evil as well as toward good." What does this mean? That a good governing **Mind** is only inclined to make an *Agent* happy, or to confer a *Reward* on him, when he has some evil *Affections*, which are yet overcome by benevolent *Affections*? But would not a benevolent *Superior* incline toward making any benevolent *Agent* happy, whether he had any weaker evil *Inclinations* or not? *Evil Inclinations* in an *Agent* would certainly tend more toward *diminishing* the *Love* of the superior *Mind*. Cannot a good *Mind* love an *Agent*, and *desire* his *Happiness*, unless he observes some *Qualities* which, if they were alone, would excite *Hatred* or *Aversion*? Must there be a *Mixture* of *Hatred* to make *Love* strong and effectual, just as there must be a *Mixture* of *Shade* to set off the *Lights* in a *Picture*, where there are no *Shades*? This poor idea of *rewardability* is taken from the *poverty* and *weakness* of *human governors*: Their *funds* are soon exhausted; they cannot make happy all those whose *happiness* they desire. Their *small reserves* must be managed frugally; no one must be rewarded for what good they will do without *reward*, or for abstaining from *evils* to which they are not inclined. *Rewards* must be reserved for the *insolent minister* who, without a reward, would openly defy his *prince*; for the *turbulent demagogue* who will raise *factions* if he is not bribed; for the *covetous, mean-spirited, but artful citizen* who will serve his *country* no further than it serves his *private interest*. But let any kind, honest *heart* declare what sort of *characters* it loves? *Whose happiness* it most desires? *Whom* it would reward if it could? Or what those *dispositions* are which, if it saw them rewarded by a *superior nature*, it would be most pleased by, and most approve of the *conduct* of that *superior*? When these *questions* are answered, we shall know what makes *actions rewardable*.

*IF* we call all *actions rewardable* simply because we *approve* of rewarding them, then indeed we shall approve of rewarding all *actions* which we *approve*, whether the

<summary>The author argues that human definitions of "rewardable" actions are limited by the scarce resources of earthly rulers, who bribe the selfish to ensure order. In contrast, true rewardability should be judged by the character traits and dispositions that a benevolent, higher nature would naturally desire to see happy and rewarded.</summary>

<page-num>297</page-num>
<header>MORAL SENSE.</header>
<meta>catchword: IF</meta>

<margin>Sect. 5.</margin>
agent has had any inclinations or motives toward evil or not: we shall also approve the *promising of rewards* to all publicly useful *actions*, whatever the *affections* of the *agents* were. If, by this *prospect of reward*, either *malicious natures* are restrained from mischief, or *selfish natures* are induced to serve the public, or *benevolent natures*—not able without reward to surmount real or apparent *selfish motives*—are encouraged: in all these cases, the *proposing of rewards* does really advance the happiness of the *whole*, or diminish its *misery*. This demonstrates *benevolence* in the superior mind and is, consequently, approved by our *moral sense*.

In this last meaning of the word *rewardable*, these dispositions are rewardable: 1. *Pure, unmixed benevolence*. 2. *Predominant good affections*. 3. *Such weak benevolence as will not, without reward, overcome apparently contrary motives of self-love*. 4. *Unmixed self-love, which by the prospect of reward may serve the public*. 5. *Self-love, which by the assistance of rewards, may outweigh some malicious affections*. If in these cases *proposing rewards* will increase the happiness of the system, or diminish its misery, it evidences *goodness* in the governor when he cannot so well accomplish as much good for the whole by other means.

If we suppose a *Necessity* of making all *virtuous Agents* equally *happy*, then indeed a *Mixture* of *evil Dispositions*, though surmounted by the *good*, or of *strong contrary Motives* outbalanced by *Motives to Good*, would be a *Circumstance* of some *Importance* in the *Distribution of Rewards*: Since such a *Nature*, during the *Struggle* of *contrary Affections* or *Motives*, must have had less *Pleasure* than that *virtuous Nature* which met with no *Opposition*: But as this very *Opposition* did give this *Nature full Evidence* of the *Strength* of its *Virtue*, this *Consciousness* may be a *peculiar Recompense* to which the *unmixed Tempers* are *Strangers*: And there seems no such *necessity* of an *equal Happiness* of *all Natures*. It is in no way inconsistent with *perfect Goodness* to make *different Orders* of *Beings*; and, provided all the *Virtuous* be at last *fully content*, and as *happy* as they *desire*, there is nothing absurd in supposing *different Capacities* and *different Degrees*; and during the *Time of Probation* <note>A period of testing or trial</note>, there is no *necessity*, not the least *show* of it, that all be *equal*.

Now if men's *Affections* are naturally good, and if there be in their fellows no *Quality* which would necessarily raise *Malice* in the *Observer*; but, on the contrary, *all Qualities* requisite to excite at least *Benevolence* or *Compassion*: it may be justly said to be in the *Power* of everyone, by due *Attention*, to prevent any malicious *Affections*, and to excite in himself *kind Affections* toward all. So that the intricate debates about human *Liberty* do not affect what is here alleged, concerning our *moral Sense* of *Affections* and *Actions*, any more than any other *Schemes*.

SOME allege, that MERIT supposes, besides *kind Affection*, that the Agent has a *moral Sense*, *reflects* upon his own *Virtue*, delights in it, and *chooses* to adhere to it for the *Pleasure* which attends it*. We need not debate the *Use* of this Word *Merit*: it is plain, we *approve* a generous kind *Action*, though the Agent had not made this *Reflection*.

<page-num>300</page-num>
<header>Illustrations upon the</header>

<margin>Section 5. Reflection.</margin>
This *reflection* shows him a motive of *self-love*, the combined view of which does not increase our *approbation*. But then it must again be admitted that we cannot form a just conclusion of a *character* from one or two kind, generous actions, especially where there have been no very strong *motives* to the contrary. Some apparent *motives* of *interest* may afterward overbalance the kind *affections* and lead the agent into vicious *actions*. <margin>|</margin>But the *reflection* on virtue, once being *charmed* with the lovely *form*, will discover an *interest* on its side, which, if well attended to, no other motive will overbalance. This *reflection* is a great *security* to the *character*; this must be assumed in such creatures as *men* are before we can well depend upon a *constancy in virtue*. The same may be said of many other motives to virtue from *interest* which, though they do not immediately influence the kind *affections* of the agent, yet do remove these *obstacles* to them arising from *false appearances of interest*. Such are those from the *sanctions* of *divine laws* by future rewards and punishments, and even the manifest *advantages* of *virtue* in this *life*. Without *reflection* on these, a steady *course of virtue* is scarcely to be expected amidst the present confusion of human affairs.

If this reasoning is just, then the best of men are infinitely evil. The distinction between *habitual* and *actual intention* will not remove the difficulty, since these arguments require *actual intention*. An *habitual intention* is not a present act of love to the DEITY that influences our actions more than *actual love* for *creatures* (which this argument requires); rather, it is a prior general *resolution* that is not repeated at the present moment.

To find what is right on this subject, we may first state some propositions of which men must convince themselves through *reflection*.

BUT our *understanding* and *power* are limited, so that we cannot know many other *natures*, nor is our utmost *power* capable of promoting the *happiness* of many: our *actions* are therefore influenced by some stronger *affections* than this general *benevolence*. There are certain *qualities* found in some *beings* more than in others, which excite stronger *degrees* of *goodwill*, and determine our *attention* to their *interests*, while that of others is neglected. The *ties of blood*, *benefits* conferred upon us, and the *observation of virtue* in others, raise much more vigorous *affections* than that general *benevolence* which we may have toward all. These *qualities* or *relations* we may call the *causes of love*.

<page-num>304</page-num>
<header>Illustrations upon the</header>

### Sect. 6.
<image-desc>A decorative calligraphic flourish or brace appears to the left of the start of the first paragraph.</image-desc>

Through the *associations of ideas*, and from the more sudden *emotions* of certain particular *passions*, the *temper* that has the most lively *gratitude*, or is the most susceptible to *friendship* with *virtuous characters*, would also have the strongest *general benevolence* toward indifferent *persons*. And on the contrary, where there is the weakest *general benevolence*, there we should expect the least *gratitude*, and the least *friendship* or *love* toward the *virtuous*. If this *proportion* is observed, then, if we express all these desires for the good of others by the name of **benevolence**, we may denote the several *degrees* in which men possess these various kind *dispositions* as the *goodness of the temper*. And the *degrees* of *desire* toward the *happiness* of any *person*, we may call the *quantity of love* toward him. Then,

The *quantity of love* toward any *person* is in a compound *proportion* of the perceived *causes of love* in him, and of the *goodness of temper* in the observer. Or $L = C \times G$.

When the *causes of love* in two *objects* are perceived as equal, the *love* toward either in different *persons* is as the *goodness of temper*; or $L = G \times 1$.

WHEN the *goodness* of *temper* is the same or equal, the *love* toward any *objects* will be in proportion to the *causes*; or L = C x 1.

THE *goodness* of any *temper* is therefore the *quantity* of *love* divided by the perceived *causes*, or G = $\frac{L}{C}$. And since we cannot perceive any *goodness* in having a *degree* of *love* that exceeds the *proportion* of its *causes*, the most *virtuous temper* is that in which the *love* equals its *causes*, which may therefore be expressed as *unity* *.

HENCE it follows that if there were any *nature* incomparably more excellent than any of our *fellow creatures*, from whom we ourselves and all others had received the greatest *benefits*, there would be less *virtue* in any small *degree* of *desire* for *his happiness* than in a similar *degree* of *love* toward our *fellow creature*. But *not loving* such a *being*, or having a smaller *degree* of *love*, must evidence a much greater *defect* in *virtue* than a similar lack of *love* toward our *fellow creatures*. The general rules applied to the love of God.

III. To apply this to the DEITY is very obvious. Our *Affections* toward him arise in the same manner as toward our Fellows, in proportion to our *Attention* to the *Causes of Love* in him, and the *Goodness of our Temper*. The *Reflection* on his *Goodness* raises *Approbation* and *Complacency*, his *Benefits* raise *Gratitude*, and both occasion *Good-will* or *Benevolence*. Some imagine that “his *Happiness* is wholly detached from all events in this world, absolute, and unvaried in himself.” And yet the same *Inclination of Mind* might remain in us, though we held this opinion. When the *Happiness* of a *Friend* is in suspense, we desire it; when he has obtained all that which we desired, the same *Inclination of Mind* seems to remain toward him, only without that *Uneasiness* accompanying the desire for an uncertain object: Thus *Gravity* may be said to be the same when a *Body* is resting on a fixed base as when it caused its descent.

If we imagine that the DEITY has such *perceptions* of *approval* or *dislike* toward *actions* as we have ourselves, then indeed our *love* for him would directly excite us to do whatever he approves, and shun what he condemns. We can scarcely avoid imagining that the frequent recurrence of *disapproved events* must be uneasy to any nature, and that observing *approved actions* must be delightful.

If we imagine that the divine *happiness*, or any part of it, is connected with the *happiness* of his creatures, so that their *happiness* constitutes the occasion of his, then indeed our *love for the* DEITY will directly excite us to all manner of *beneficent actions*. It is true, many good men deny these last two opinions; yet it is probable that when their minds are diverted from *speculations* by opportunities for *action*, there recurs some imagination of *offense*, *uneasiness*, and *resentment* in the DEITY upon observing evil *actions*; of *delight* and *joy* in beholding good *actions*; of *sorrow* upon observing the *misery* of his creatures, and *joy* upon seeing them happy. <meta>This page continues the discussion on how our conceptualizations of the divine nature influence human morality and temper, transitioning into "speculative opinions" regarding divine happiness and its relation to the universe.</meta>

<page-num>308</page-num>
<header>Illustrations upon the</header>
<sig>X</sig>

### Sect. 6. *Speculative Opinions.*
---
In our conceptions of the DEITY, we are continually led to imagine a resemblance to what we feel in ourselves.

Whoever maintains these opinions of the DEITY to be true, must also suppose "a particular *determination* of all events in the universe"; otherwise this part of the divine happiness is made *precarious* and *uncertain*, depending upon the undetermined *will* of creatures.

The diversity of opinions concerning the divine happiness may lead men into different ways of accounting for the *influence* which the *love* of GOD may have upon our actions toward our fellows: but the affections toward the DEITY would be much the same in both schemes. Where there were the same just *apprehensions* of the divine *goodness* in two persons, the *love* for the DEITY in both would be proportional to their *goodness of temper*. Though the highest possible degree of love for a perfectly good DEITY would evidence no more *virtue of temper* than a proportional *love* for creatures; yet having only smaller degrees of love for the DEITY would evidence a greater *defect* of goodness in the temper than any lack of *affection* toward creatures.

<sig>X 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: HERE</meta>

<summary>The author explains that attributing happiness to the Deity requires believing in divine determination of all events to ensure that happiness is not dependent on human will. He concludes that while love for God and love for others both reflect a good temper, a lack of love for a perfectly good God indicates a deeper moral failing than a lack of love for fellow humans.</summary>
<keywords>Deity, speculative opinions, divine happiness, determination, love of God, virtue of temper, goodness of temper, affection</keywords>
