<page-num>309</page-num>
<header>MORAL SENSE.</header>

<margin>
Sect. 6.
</margin>

HERE it must be remembered that in arguing concerning the *Goodness of Temper* directly from the *Degree of Love*, and inversely from the *Causes of Love*, *actual Attention* to the *Causes of Love* is assumed to be present in the *Person*. For it is plain that in even the best *Temper*, no single *Affection* or *Idea* can always remain present, and there can be no *Affection* present in the *Mind* toward any *Object* while the *Idea* of it is not present. Therefore, the simple *Absence* of *Affection* while the *Mind* is employed upon a different *Object* can prove no evil in the *Temper*, except insofar as a lack of *Attention* may indicate a lack of *Affection*. In like manner, in the best *Temper*, there can be no love toward an unknown *Object*. Therefore, the lack of love for an *Object unknown* can prove no evil in the *Temper*, except insofar as *Ignorance* may indicate a lack of *Affection*. It is indeed certain that he who knows that there is a good DEITY and actually thinks of him and of all his benefits, yet does not have the strongest *Love* and *Gratitude* toward him, must have a *Temper* void of all *Goodness*. But it does not follow that a mind is void of goodness because it is not always thinking of the DEITY, or actually loving him, or even if it does not know him. How far the lack of *Attention* to the DEITY and *Ignorance* of him may indicate an evil *Temper*,

<margin>*What degrees of affection are necessary for innocence.*</margin>
IV. But prior to these inquiries, we must consider "what degrees or kinds of *affection* are necessary to obtain the *simple approval of innocence*." It is plain that the bare *absence* of all *malice* is not enough. We may have a general *benevolence* toward a mere *sensitive nature* which has no other desire but *self-love*; but we can perceive no *moral goodness* in such a being. Indeed, it is not every *small degree* of kind *affections* which we *approve*. There must be some proportion of kind *affections* to the other *faculties* in any nature—particularly to its *understanding* and *active powers*—to obtain *approbation*. Some animals demonstrate small degrees of *goodwill*, which makes them *approved* within their kind; but the same *degrees* would not be approved in a *human*. There is a higher degree expected in mankind, which if they do not reach, we do not consider them *innocent*. It is not easy to fix precisely that *degree* which we approve as *innocent* by our *moral sense*. Every kind *affection*, if it is considered only in relation to its own *object*, is indeed approved—such as *natural affection*, *gratitude*, *pity*, and *friendship*. And yet, when we take a more extensive view of the tendency of

<summary>This page explores the criteria for moral innocence, arguing that a simple lack of malice is insufficient for approval. It suggests that humans are held to a higher standard of benevolence than animals, proportional to their greater intellectual and active capacities.</summary>

Regarding some actions proceeding even from these Sect. 6. *affections*, we may often condemn these actions when they are understood as harmful to larger *systems* of mankind. In the same way, we often condemn actions done out of *love* for a particular country when they appear to be harmful to *mankind* in general. Similarly, *self-preservation* and pursuing private *advantage*, considered in the abstract, are innocent. But when they are understood as very harmful to the safety of others in any case, they are condemned.

**Mankind** is capable of large, expansive *ideas* of great *societies*. And it is expected of them that their *general benevolence* should continually direct and limit not only their *selfish affections* but even their closer *attachments* to others: that their *desire* for the public *good* and *aversion* to public *misery* should overcome, at least, their *desire* for *positive private advantages*—whether for themselves or their particular *favorites*—so as to make them refrain from any *action* which would be positively harmful or hurtful to *mankind*, no matter how beneficial it might be to themselves or their favorites. To endure *positive evil* for the sake of *positive good* for others seems to be a degree of *virtue* above simple *innocence*, which we do not universally expect. But to reject *positive* at-

WHAT is said here relates only to the *perceptions* of our *moral sense*, and not to those degrees of virtue which the **DEITY** may require through *revelation*: And everyone’s heart may inform him whether or not he approves—at least as *innocent*—those who omit many good offices which they might *possibly* have done, provided they do a great deal of good; and those who carefully abstain from every perceived offense toward the **DEITY**, though they might possibly be more frequent in *acts of devotion*. It is true indeed, that the *omission* of what *we know to be required* is positively evil: so that by a *revelation* we may be obliged to further services than were necessary prior to it, which we could not innocently omit after this *revelation* is known: But we are here only considering our *moral sense*.

<margin>How a lack of attention indicates a bad temperament.</margin>

### VII. It is next to be considered how far a lack of *attention* to the **DEITY** can indicate a lack of good affections in any person to whom He is known.

Every good temperament will have strong affections for a good **DEITY**, and where there is *strong affection*, there will be *frequent reflection* upon the beloved object, a *desire to please*, and a *caution against causing offense*. In like manner, every person of good temperament who has had knowledge of a country, a *system*, or a *species* will consider how far these *great societies* may be affected by his actions, with the same attention that he uses in his own affairs, and will abstain from what is injurious to them.

BUT an *injurious action* which appeared to the agent not only *pernicious* to *his fellows*, or to particular *persons*, but *offensive* to the <term>DEITY</term>, and *pernicious* to a *system*, is much more *vicious* than when the agent did not reflect upon the <term>DEITY</term>, or a *community*.

### Sect. 6.

<margin>Nothing in this *scheme* supersedes the duty of *love* to the <term>DEITY</term>, and general benevolence.</margin>

VIII. We must not hence imagine that, in order to produce greater virtue in ourselves, we should regard the <term>DEITY</term> no further than merely to abstain from *offenses*. Were it our sole intention in beneficent actions only to obtain the *private pleasure of self-approbation* for the degree of our virtue, this might seem the proper means of having great *virtue* with the least *expense*. But if the real intention, which constitutes an action virtuous, be the *promoting of public good*, then voluntarily to reject the consideration of any motive which would increase the *moment* <note>In this context, "moment" refers to the total weight, importance, or quantity of good produced by an action.</note> of public good, or would make us more vigorous and steadfast in virtue, must demonstrate a want of *good affection*. In any given *moment* of beneficence, the unaffected *want of regard to the* <term>DEITY</term>, *or to private interest*, does indeed demonstrate greater virtue. But the *retaining of these motives* with a view to increase the *moment* of public good in our actions—if they really do so—demonstrates virtue equal to, or greater than, that in the former case. And the *affected neglect* of these motives, so that we may acquit ourselves virtuously with the least *expense* to ourselves, or with the least *moment* of public good, must evidence a *want of good affections*, and a base *trick* and *artifice* to impose upon

It is also plain from universal *experience* that a regard for the **DEITY**, frequent *reflection* on his *goodness*, and consequent *acts of love* are the strongest and most universally prevailing *means* of obtaining a good temper. Whatever *institution*, therefore, does most effectively tend to raise men's *attention*, to recall their minds from the hurry of their common affairs, and to *instruct* them in the ways of promoting public *good* further than the busy part of the world would probably understand without assistance, must be so wise and good that every honest mind should rejoice in it, even though it had no other *authority* than a human one to recommend it. Everyone will understand that by this is meant *public worship* on set days, in which a stop is put to commerce, and the busy part of mankind is instructed in the duties of piety and *humanity*.</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 325, completing the sentence regarding how "particular good affection" relates to virtue. It challenges the narrow definition of virtue held by certain theologians and philosophers who claim that actions are only virtuous if they are specifically intended as acts of devotion toward the Deity.</meta>

<page-num>326</page-num>
<header>Illustrations upon the</header>
<meta>catchword: To</meta>

### Sect. 6. affection, there is less virtue in the former than in the latter.

---

Men may use names as they please, and may choose to call nothing *virtue* but "what is intended chiefly to evidence *affection* of one kind or other toward the **DEITY**." Writers on this system are not well agreed about what this virtuous *intention* is; whether it is only to evidence *submission*, or *submission* and *love*, or to obtain the divine *benevolence* and *private happiness* for the *agent*, or to give *pleasure* to the **DEITY**. But let them not assert, against universal *experience*, that we approve of no *actions* which are not thus intended toward the **DEITY**. It is plain that a generous, compassionate *heart*, which at the first view of the *distress* of another flies impatiently to his *relief* or spares no *expense* to accomplish it, meets with strong *approbation* from every *observer* who has not perverted his *sense of life* by *school divinity*<note>Theology as taught in the medieval schools (Scholasticism), often criticized for being overly logical and detached from practical experience.</note> or *philosophy*. It is to be suspected that some *vanity* must be at the bottom of these *notions*, which place *virtue* in some *nicety* that *active tempers* have no leisure to comprehend, and which only the *recluse student* can attain.

<vocab>virtue, DEITY, affection, submission, benevolence, private happiness, school divinity, philosophy, active tempers, recluse student</vocab>

<summary>The author argues that virtue should not be narrowly defined solely as actions intended for the Deity, as people naturally approve of spontaneous acts of compassion. He suggests that overly complex definitions of virtue may stem from the vanity of academic or religious students rather than common human experience.</summary>
<keywords>virtue, Deity, moral intention, compassion, school divinity, religious philosophy, moral sense, benevolence</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the argument from page 326, where the author began refuting the idea that only actions consciously intended for the Deity can be considered virtuous. It explores the relationship between finite attention and moral motives.</meta>

<page-num>327</page-num>
<header>MORAL SENSE. Sect. 6.</header>

To be led by a weaker *Motive*, where a *stronger* one is equally present to the Mind—to love a Creature *more* than GOD, or to have stronger *Desires* of doing what is pleasing to Creatures than to GOD, when we pay equal attention to both—would certainly argue some *Perversion* of *our Affections*; or to study the particular Good of one person more than that of a *System*, when we reflected on both: But as no *finite Mind* can retain a *Multiplicity* of *Objects* at once, so it cannot *always* retain any one Object. When a Person, therefore, not thinking at present of the DEITY, or of a *Community* or *System*, performs a beneficent Action out of particular *Love*, he demonstrates *Goodness* of *Temper*. The bare *Absence* of the Idea of a DEITY, or of *Affections* toward him, can evidence no evil; otherwise it would be a Crime to fall asleep, or to think of anything else: If the bare *Absence* of this Idea be no evil, the Presence of kind *Affections* toward Fellow-Creatures cannot be evil. If indeed our Love to the DEITY urged us toward any Action, and at the same time Love to a Creature urged us toward the *Omission* of it, or toward a contrary Action, we must be very criminal if the former does not prevail; yet this will not prove all Actions to be evil in which *pleasing* the DEITY,

Indeed, that disposition must really be very deficient in *goodness* which needs, in order to excite it to any good office, to recall the thoughts of a DEITY, or a *community*, or a *system*. The frequent recalling of these thoughts, indeed, does strengthen all good affections and increases the *force* of beneficence to be expected from any disposition; and with this *view*, frequently to recall such thoughts must be one of the best *helps* to virtue and evidence high degrees of it. Indeed, one cannot call that disposition *entire* and *complete* which does not have the strongest *affection* toward the greatest benefactor and the most worthy object.

BEINGS of such degrees of *knowledge* and such extent of *thought* as mankind are not only capable of, but generally obtain when nothing interrupts their inquiries, must naturally arise to the knowledge of the DEITY if their disposition is good. They must form *general conceptions* of the whole and see the *order*, *wisdom*, and *goodness* in the *administration* of nature to some degree. <margin>*Whether the* DEITY *is the sole proper object of love.*</margin>
IX. THERE is one very subtle argument on this subject. Some allege, "That since the DEITY is really the *cause* of all the good in the universe, even of all the *virtue*, or *good affection* in creatures—which are the seeming *causes* of *love* toward them—it must argue a *strange perversion* of temper to love those in whom there is no *cause* of *love*, or who are (as they prefer to speak) nothing, or an *emptiness* of all *goodness*. The DEITY alone is lovable, in whom there is an infinite *fullness* of every lovable *quality*. The DEITY," they say, not without some reason, "is the *cause* of every *pleasant sensation*, which he immediately excites according to a *general law*, upon the occasion of *motions* arising in our bodies; likewise, he gave us that general *inclination*, which we modify into all our different *affections*; GOD, therefore," they say, "is alone lovely. Other things are not to be loved, except for the *goodness* of God appearing in them; indeed, some make the loving of them—without considering GOD as display-
->ing<-

<summary>This page argues that a mind has not reached its full potential until religion becomes its primary delight. It also introduces a subtle theological argument suggesting that because God is the ultimate cause of all goodness and sensation, He should be the sole object of our love.</summary>

<margin>Sect. 6.</margin>
<image-desc>decorative horizontal flourish</image-desc>
“ing his goodness in them, to be infinitely evil.”

In answer to this, it must be admitted that “God’s being the cause of all the good in the universe will no doubt raise the highest love for him in a good disposition when it reflects upon it.”

But 1st, if all men had this belief that “there was no good in any creature,” they really would not love them at all. But men generally imagine, with very good reason, that there are *good beings* distinct from God, though produced by him; and whether this opinion is true or false, it demonstrates no evil.

2. Since according to this theory God is the cause of all *pleasant sensation*, so he is the cause of all pain. He is, according to them, the cause of that *inclination* which we modify into *evil affection*, as well as into *good*. If then we are to love God only for what we call *good affection* in creatures, and not the creatures themselves, we must also only love God upon observing *evil affections* in creatures and have no *aversion* to the *basest character*, since God gave the general inclination alike in both cases.

<meta>catchword: 3. IF</meta>

<summary>The author counters the idea that only God should be loved, arguing that people reasonably perceive goodness in created beings themselves. He also points out that if God is seen as the sole cause of all human inclinations, one would logically have to love God even for the existence of evil characters.</summary>
<keywords>God, Goodness, Universe, Love, Creature, Sensation, Inclination, Evil Affection, Character, Disposition</keywords>

3. **If** we may suppose real *beings* distinct from **God**, and that *their affections* are not **God's** *affections*; if **God** is not the only *lover* and *hater*; if our *moral sense* is determined to approve *kind affections*, and our *love* or *benevolence* must arise toward what we *approve*; or if we find an *instinct* to desire the *happiness* of every *sensitive nature*, we cannot avoid loving *creatures*, and we must *approve* any *kind affections* observed in others toward their *fellows*. It is true, we must approve the *highest affections* toward the **Deity**, and condemn, as a *deficiency* of just *affections* toward **God**, any *degree* of love which is not superior to our other *affections*. But still, *affections* toward *creatures*, if they are distinct *natures* from **God**, must be approved.

# PROPOSALS
->*For printing by subscription,*<-

An historical, critical, geographical, chronological, and etymological Dictionary of the *Holy Bible*; in which all the proper names mentioned in the *Old* or *New Testament* are explained, whether of men, cities, countries, rivers, mountains, etc. As well as the significant and remarkable appellatives that occur anywhere within it, and all natural productions, such as plants, animals, stones, gems, etc. The whole work is arranged in alphabetical order and illustrated with accurate maps and a wide variety of beautiful copper plates, representing the *antiquities* and other curiosities of the *Jews*.

To which is prefixed *Bibliotheca Sacra*, or an extensive catalog of the best editions and versions of the *Bible*. Together with an account of the most valuable commentaries, expositions, and paraphrases on the whole or any part of it. Concluding with a brief chronology of the history of the Bible, a *Jewish* calendar, tables of all *Jewish* coins, weights, and measures converted to our own, and a literal translation of all the *Hebrew*, *Chaldee*, *Syriac*, and *Greek* names in the Bible. With prefaces appropriate to each part. Written originally in *French* by Father *Augustin Calmet*.

<summary>This page presents a proposal for a subscription-based printing of Father Augustin Calmet's comprehensive Bible dictionary, covering biblical names, geography, natural history, and Jewish antiquities. The work is described as including maps, copper plates, a bibliography of biblical versions, and a chronology of history.</summary>

Calmet, a *Benedictine Monk*, Abbot of *St. Leopold* in *Nancy*. According to the closest estimate, this work will consist of about three hundred and forty sheets in *folio*, in addition to one hundred and eighteen exquisite copper plates—forty-nine of which are whole-sheet plates, and sixty-four are half-sheets, including six large maps. The price for subscribers will be three and a half guineas in sheets: one and a half guineas to be paid in advance, and the remainder upon delivery. Subscriptions are accepted by *John Smith* and *William Bruce*, booksellers in *Dublin*; *John Osborn* and *John Longman* at the *Ship* in *Paternoster Row*, and by most other booksellers in *Great Britain* and *Ireland*.
